AWONDERFUL DISCOVERY.—This Fi the age of research and experiment, when all nature, so to speak, is ransacked by the scientific for the oomfoit and happiness of man. Science has indeed made giant strides during the past century, and among the—by no means least important—discoveries in medicine comes that of

- THRRAPION. This preparation it unquestionably one of the most Rennin:, and reliable Patent Medicines ever introduced, and has, we understand, been used in the Continental Hospital, by Ricord, Rostan. Jobert, Velpeau, Maisonneuve, the well- known Chassaignac, and indeed by all who are regarded as authorities in such matters, including the celebrated Laßemand, and Roux, by whom it was seine time since uniformly adopted, and that it is worthy the attention of those who require such a remedy we think there is no doubt. From the time of Aristotle downwards. a potent agent in the removal of these diseases has (like the famed philosopher's stone) been the object of search of some hopeful generous minds ; and far beyond the mere power—if such could ever have been discovered—of transmuting the baser metals into gold is Bluely the discovery of a remedy so potent as to replenish the failing energies in the one case, and in the other so effectually, speedily and safely to expel from the system the poisons of acquired or inherited disease in all their protean forms as to leave no taint or trace behind. Such is the new French Remedy THRRAPION, which may certainly rank with, if not take precedence of, many of the discoveries of our day, about which no little ostentation and noise have been made, and the extensive and ever-increasing demand that has been created for this medicine wherever Introduced appears to prove that it is destined to cast into oblivion all those questionable remedies that were formerly the sole reliance of medical Men. WOMEN'S CHAT.

Quite the most enthusiastic yachtswoman among the , members of our Royg Family is Princess Henry of Battenberg. Her Royal Highness is never so well or happy as when on board her beautiful yacht, and her daughter Princes.s Ena is eqtially devoted to this parti- cular pastime. Princess Henry is very fond of garden- ing, and moreover ranks as quite a distinguished musician,but she cares little for State functions,and like the Duchess of Fife, seldom appears at any of them. 'There are already rumours concerning the betrothal of Princess Ens, who has quite fulfilled the promise of her -childhood, and grown a very beautiful and charming girl An Ancient Monastry.—Rufford Abbey, where the King is to be the guesi of Lord and Lady Savile for the Doncaster Race Meeting, has not altered greatly since the old Cavalier days. There is the great hall, with its carved oak -roof and minstrel gallery, and also the long oak tables and benches, which served the needs of- the Cistercian monks, who were quartered there since the year TIM. It passed to the Saviles by rnarriage, and Played a prominent part in the Civil Var. Sir William -avile was the leader of the Royalist forces in Yorkshire. After his death.his widow long held -the castle against the Roundheads, who however,eventu- ally gained possesSion.. The Stuart room contains many relics of those stirriag times and in the Chapel none of the Prayer Books are of later date than the time of Charles If. Rufford Abbey also rejoices in a ghost, which takes the form of a little old lady in black. Expecting a Disaster.—A clergyman related that on one occasion when'he was preaching in a strange church, he was struck by the very marked attention with which he was listened to, and sabsequently mentioning the matter to a member of the congregation, obtained an -explanation. Theirulpit being unsafe, the Vicar was accustomed to preach from the lectern, and the intent- ness of the congregation was largely due to the fact that they were expecting to see the pulpit collapse with the preacher. Now we read of a similar peril at Cox- wold Church, Yorks, of which the Vicar says that," for some time past parson and people have been preaching and worshipping atthe imminent peril of their lives" and' although the-sermons were always short, yet the danger as there. An appeal is made for funds, and the fact is adduced that the present incumbent is a successor of the Rev. Lawrence Sterne. Open Windows.—Whatever the autumn may bring forth we may faitly claim to have had a really fine summer, and if we could always rely upon fine warm -seasons, it would no doubt lead to many reforms in our dress and habits during the hot weather. One that it encourages, but is all too quickly discarded is, Oeeping with open windows. In hot weather open windows are the rule but with the first breath of autumn they are shut again, with the consequence that vast numbers of people spend the nights throughout the greater part of the year in unhealthy, stuffy bedrooms. Some object to open windows because the frames and blinds may rattle, but it is easy to prevent this, and everybody in sound health should train themselves to sleep in a well ventil- ated room which is one of the best preventives of headache, lassitude and consumption. The Favourite Gem.—The ruby is the most expensive -stone now-a-days, provided it is of that pure, full colour known as pigeons' blood, an intensely deeplustrous red. As in the case of peatls, his necessary to order a set of rubies some months before they are required, and even then it is not always possibleto obtain a large number which are perfection in every detail. The collection of -these gems,possessed by same of our great ladies, has ,extended over many years, and even generations. For young girls' wear, the very slender gold necklet, with drops of amethysts, interspersed with pearls, is very modish, and has quite takes] the place of the row of pearls. Not infrequently, the topaz or cornelian is utilised instead of the amethyst, but the latter is the most desirable from every point of view. ' Crown Jewels.--The finest pearls'in Europe are said to be among the Saxon Crown Jewels, but the Queen 'Dowager of Italy possesses a magnificent collection of these gems which are supposed to be destined for the Princess Yolande, the eldest daughter of the King and flueen. The largest and most magnificent collection of ' Crown Jewels in the world is that Epossessed by the Imperial house of Russia. One item is a -shawl of ex- quisite filigreeiwork in gold,whick is a mass of diamonds and many other of th 3 ornaments-are of antique form, and therefore but seldom worn. The Austrian and Hungarian Crown Jewels are very fine, also the Prussian Crown Jewels, which are of great value and rvery beautifully set. The Passing of the Tiara.—The tiara has, in great measure, given way to theless aggressive bandeau. which to be a success, should not measure more than five to six inches across. Some women allow it to extend round the sides of the head, which is a decided mistake 'The passing of the tiara is -probably due to the fact that it has become rather too popular,but there is something to be said for the reason set forward, viz., tkat with the present arrangement of our hair in front, the bandeau proves much more becoming. The Dressing of our Hair.—The majority of women are always altering their style of coiffure, following all the ideas, and often the eccentricities of fashion in this direction. But there are a few, who, like Queen Alexandra, remain faithful to the one style of coiffure that best suits their features and general appearance. For'example, Lady Curzon of Kidleston always has her beautiful dark hair parted in the middle, and arranged in smooth classic waves on each side of her face, while Mrs. George West keeps to the same style of hairdress- ing as prevailed a quarter of a century ago. Lady -Granby and Lady Lytton dress theirlair always in the picturesque fashion adopted by the" Souls," and Mrs. , Langtry still follows the classic style as seen in the Grecian statues, This latter demands a perfect profile, faultless outlines, and a neck that is full, white, and round charms which, taken altogether, are exceedingly rare. Red Footwear.—ln Paris, the craze for red shoes and hose for outdoor.wear, shows no sign of abating, but whatever the Parisienne may don in this way, it is not always safe to follow her example. Frenchwomen of

the higher classes, are, as a rule possessed of small feet, arched insteps, and pretty ankles, and for such and such only, is anything so striking as ' red outdoor footgear possible. With a black house gown, red shoes are often piquant and charming, but taken as a whole, the women of our Islands are safer in remaining faithful to the black, or dark brown varieties for ordinary wear. The Craze for Silver Grey.—Silver grey fabrics are in great request, but undoubtedly they are much less becoming to the generality of women than the putty and fawn shades which we have had with us so long. More- over the latter show dust and mud less, being more akin to these undesirable adjuncts of our climate. The modish silver grey, on the other hand shews every speck of dust, or splash of mud, and if held up by dark, or black gloved hands, tho chalices are that the traces of such a procedure will be visible afterwards. MADGE.

VEGETABLES UP-TO-DATE.

The strict vegetarian and all others who appreciate the very useful part ,which vegetables play in our daily dietary, must often wish for a means of making the homelier vegetables more appetising. A simple and most successful way to accomplish this, is to serve the vegetables with an appetising sauce. Such sauces are easily made with thoaid of Brown & Poison's "Patent" Corn Flour. Brown Sc Polson's publish a special recipe book entitled "Fish and Vegetable Sauces," a copy of which will be forwarded to every lady sending a id. stamp to B. & P.. Paisley. The recipes include sauces for even the most common vegetable in daily use, such as carrots, turnips, cabbage, ttc., and to serve such vegetables with a good sauce gives them an added relish

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

VACCINATION EXEMPTION CERTIFICATES REFUSED AT

R UNCORN.

At Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday Frank Littler, Waterloo-road. applied for a certificate exempting him from having his infant son vaccinated. Mr.' A. Thomas refused to grant the certificate. Mr. Littler said he would try again.—Mr. 'Thomas: You may try where you like. I shall not grant it.—Joseph Lightfoot, Halton-road, made a similar application, and in reply to Mr. Thomas said he had not got a doctor's certificate to the effect that vaccination would be detrimental to the health of the child.—Mr.Thomas :That's quite sufficient shall not grant it.

===== 0002601_19040903_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
CAMDEN MISSIONARY SOCIETY, RUNCORN.

At a meeting of the Camden Wesleyan Missionary Society, on Sunday, Mr. T. C. Beckett in opening the proceedings alluded to his official career, and pointed out that he had held office for 30 years. The announce- ment was received with much satisfaction by the teachers and scholars. Mr. Beckett introduced Mr. G. Williams as chairman, and the Rev. S. H. Ravenscroft, of South Africa,and formerly of Runcorn,as the speaker. He said they were two old Camden scholars. Mr. Williams referred to the great benefit he had received from his connection with the school, and urged the scholars to repay the effort „s of the teachers by giving their lives to the Master. The Rev. S. H. Ravenscroft congratulated the workers upon the financial success shown by the treasurer's report. He paid a tribute to the services rendered by Mr. Beckett and congratulated him upon his long and successful term of office. The rev, gentleman then gave a short account of his experi- ences in South Africa, and offeted many illustrations of the value of the ministry there. A. collection in aid of the missions was taken.

RCNCORN LIBRARY—THE ENLARGEMENT SCHEME. The monthly meeting of the Runcorn Library Com- mittee was held at the Town Hall on Friday evening, Mr. W. Handley presiding.—Tne Librarian presented the 22nd annual report of the library. It showed that during the year 891 borrowers' tickets had been issued, an increase of 65 on the last year. The afternoon issues were 3,145, and the evening issues 25,564. The total number of books taken from the lending department was 27,709, an increase of 2,258 over last year. Eight hundred and twenty-six books were utilized in the refer- ence department, a decrease of 152. The total number of issues was 29,335, an increase of 2,105. The average daily issue was 121, an increase of six. Four hundred books had been added to the stock, 316 to the lend- ing department and 51 to the reference depart- ment. Two hundred volumes had been rebound. The report was adopted, on the motion • of Mr. Bisbrown, seconded by Mr. Rankinson, and it was decided to print 150 copies.—The Book Selection Com- mittee recommended the purchase of 130 new books.— The Chairman said that Mr. Wilding had attended to explain his plans for the proposed enlargement of the library. The committee would be glad to know that the Council approved of him doing this work, and had further stated that there should be no increase in the administrative expenses even if the committee were successful with Mr. Carnegie.—Mr. Wilding. then pro- ceeded by the aid of plans to explain the scheme in detail. The proposed accommodation was :—Basement. 650 feet; ground floor: hall, 260 feet; reading-r00m,498 feet; magazine room, 260 feet; ieference and ladies' room, 481 feet; first floor, 630 feet; total, 2,799 feet. The shelving displaced was 1,364 feet, and the shelvins provided on the ground floor was 1,344 feet. The esti- mated cost of the alterations was £2,560, and the cost of, the fittings £5OO. The large room on the first floor could be divided by movable screens.—Mr. Cunningham said they had better ask also for the cost of the fittings from Mr. Carnegie. (Laughter.l—On the motion of Mr. Pope, seconded by Mr. Riley. it was decided to submit the plans to the Finance Committee of the Council, and Mr. Wilding was thanked for his services in the matter.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

One of the most sensible and economica coyeities in -.regard to the ever serviceable coat and skirt costume which has met with sincere FipproN-al froth. . everyone who has adopted it, provides two skirts for wear with one and the same coat. This may at first glance appear unnecessary and extravagant as, m course, the first cost of the costume is greatly increased, but as of course this refers to only those of tweed, serge or cloth it will be found much less expensive in the long ' run foi it has .fallen to the experience of . most of us to find ourselves face to face with a coat which ie very little the worse of the wear it has had while the accompanying skirt shows cut edges and a general shabbiness so that one can hardly believe they are part and parcel of the same costume. This state of affairs is prevented by • skirt, the one

the doubl,

A USEFUL CAP:

being cut very short and made as plainly as possible, while the other is more liberally strapped and stitched and fuller altogether and is, besides, of an ordinary length. The short skirt is, of course, designed for wet weather and country wear which saves the more elaborate one for more important bccasions, and by using them as required the coat will wear in harmony with the rest of the 3ostume. A long skirt always looks out of place on a stormy day or a country road. Especially useful is the double skirt for the girl of sporting proclivities, for the short skirt is suited to the requirements of golf, fishing, ar even shooting. A serge costume. of this mode completed by a cap of the same materil fashioned in the same style as that in my first sketch looks businesslike and is becoming especially when a long veil 'is twisted round it in the same way as that effected by the motorists and loosely knotted at one side with the ends allowed to float free.

These flat crowned caps prove most com- fortable wear on a windy day as they fit well on to the head and lend themselves to the firm adjustment of the necessary hatpins. There is evinced in the first nint of the fashions for the icoming autumn a marked tendency towards plaids, and some of the smartest dres- Ses of this material will .be made with a short pleated skirt, and in every instance a cap tc

JESSICA'S CAPRICE.

Er CLARA 11IMHOLLAND,

Author of "Kathleen Mavourneen," "Lady Strath- more's Stratagem," "John Lawson's Ward," "Two Against One," "A False Step," tte.

"Jessica is sweet to look upon," cried Mrs. Fairbairn, trailing her long, silken skirts over the rich carpet and bending gracefully to• kiss a small, delicate woman lying upon a couch at the far end of a big drawing-room in Queen's-gate. "You ought to be proud of your daughter, Marion, and see that she does well in life. Where is she off to now by herself? You really give her too much freedom."

Marion raised her dainty. eyebrows and shrugged her shoulders.

"I don't give Jessica freedom ; she takes it. I'm not strong enough to cope with a will like hers. You've no idea how delicate I am,. Floss dear. A ball or a dinner quite knocks me up, and we have so many to go to nowadays." "Yet Jessica looks as fresh as a rose." "She seldom comes with us," laughing. "She's too serious • for such frivolities. Now, I dance the night through, but Jessica "—she threw up her hands—"is tired and ready to go home, when she does come, after quite a few dances." "In our day the mothers sat and watched—the young girls danced." "But that's many years ago. And our mothers mere old-fashioned., "

"If I had a daughter," thoughtfully, "I'd do as they did. It's the right and proper thing, and evidently dancing does not agree with you, Marion." "Nothing "—sighing—" does. The days are long, and "—yawning— 'l'm bored to death.'

. - "You want a tonic, or, better still, a little work."

"That I am sure of. But, nevertheless, you ought to go out with her more than you do." "Now "—with a frown—" don't lecture too much, Floss. I won't stand it. I couldn't possibly go with Jessica to-day. The room is quite small, and not a chair to sit on."

"But where has she gone?" "To her bookbinding class, of course. Oh, see, you have not heard of her latest craze." "No, indeed, I have not." "Well, she's learning to bind books at Mr. Sharpies, in Mudie-street." "Learning to bind books? For goodness' sake why'?" "Don't look so tragic. It's only "—laughing-- "a fair maid's fancy, a caprice that does no one any harm. I thought you'd be shocked." "I'm not shocked, but I confess to feeling a little vexed. It seems a crying shame that Jessica should waste her time in such a way. The years will soon pass, and she ought to be getting married. Has she no admirers ?"

"What a question! My dear, she ;night count them by the hundred. But you know Jessica—her head in the clouds, she dreams of a paragon. A chevalier sans reproche,' and Pm afraid' that in London society such men are rare: So she refuses everyone and goes on—with her bookbinding."

Airs. FairbWirn swept up and down the room, absorbed in thought. "And this teacher? " pausing suddenly. "Is he--"

"Safe as can be," laughed Mrs. Lennox. "Elderly, plain, rough in manner, so don't be uneasy. Sharpies is all be ought to be." "And the other pupils? Afe they--" "My dear," impatiently, "you want to know too much. it's not likely Jessica would waste her time on a pupil when "—in scornful and scathing tones —"she refused Sir Loftus Dunstable."

"The foolish girl! How unwise. You must really tell her to re-consider—give Sir Loftus another chance. He—for, oh !" —clasping her hands—"supposing anything were to happen—and business is very low in the City—before Jessica is married. Think how awful that would be."

"Don't talk of anything so horrible," shivering. "I declare you are a regular wet blanket to-day. There's no danger of a crash. John is in first-rate spirits." Mrs. Fairbairn looked curiously at her cousin. "I am truly glad to hear that, and one ought never to listen to vague rumours." "Certainly not. But you ought to talk to Jessica, Floss, and urge her to accept Sir. Loftus. I've done so till I'm tired. She doesn't mind one word . I say, and just laughs and tells me she prefers bookbinding. Ali! there's • the luncheon gong. Thank goodness." Meanwhile her father's carriage bore Jessica Lennox swiftly on her way. She waa in radiant spirits, and little dreaming of her cousin's anxiety for her future. his ne.w occupation, caprice her mother called it, gave her immense pleasure. She had long wanted some scope for her energies, and she now told herself she had found it. Book- binding was useful, artistic work. It provided her with employment and interest. "Some day I'll bring home an exquisitely-bound volume, the cover designed and made by my own fair hands," she cried, gaily, as she climbed up the narrow stairs leading to the studio in a small and dingy street, not far from New Oxford-street, "and then they'll all be pleased and tell me I'm really quite clever." Behind a big screen in the corner Jessica removed her lace boa and large picture hat and feathers, and, putting on a long blue overall, which completely concealed her pretty dress, stepped out. amongst the pupils and began at once to work. Half-a-dozen girls and it couple of young men looked up, and smiled she appeared, then resumed their occupation wit hout a word. "And I had better follorr their example. Adrian Barton was "7 -- smiling and blushing—"too attractive. It is a good thing, perhaps, that he has gone." That evening Mr. Lennox spoke seriously to hia daughter, and urged her to accept Sir Loftts. Dunstable's offer of marriage, which he wao willing and eager to repeat if only she would give him the opportunity. "I could not marry Sir Loftus, father," she replied, &mil-. "I do not love him." "Nonsense Love is only absurd sentimentality. Hard times are upon us, Jessica. Any day I—we may all be on the brink of starvation. Marry this good man--" "Father," she cried, white to the lips, "this is terrible !" "You will do as I ask? As Lady Loftus—" "I could not. Oh, father, don't ask me to sell myself," wailed the girl in anguish. "Anything rather than that." "You're a fool!" he cried, and swung out of the room angry and indignant. Months passed, and although everyone in the house knew of the grave danger that threatened them, things went on as before in the Lennox establishment There were dinner-parties, luncheon parties and receptions. Mrs. Lennox ordered new and expensive dresses; Mr. Lennox smoked the finest cigars and drank the best of wine, and all the time they were on the edge of an abyss. No one seemed to view the situation in a proper spirit —that is, no one but Jessica. She alone made an effort to retrench; she shine considered seriously what she should do in the future.

"I could not marry Sir Loftus Dunstable, and he would have acted- generously towards us all," six would cry, wringing her hands despairingly. "It would be a sin to do so—a sin against him, t sin against- myself—when I do not love him. BM: I must do something—work. Mother will want so much. But what can I do? Ali !" a glance of joy in her eyes, "I know. My caprice, as she called it. :My bookbinding will save us. Thank Heaven there is at least one thing that I can do well." At last the crash came, awful and more terrible even than was expected. • John Lennox was ruined, and the shock and horror was more than he could bear. An attack of his heart laid him suddenly prostrate one day upon the floor. Before night he was dead!

Upon Jessica now devolved the entire manage- ment of everything. Her mother, weak and help- less, was incapable of thought or action. So, cut to the heart, sorrowing, yet brave and courageous, the girl set to work with a will, resolved to do what she could to spare the poor woman all possible pain and suffering. It was, a difficult task; but when at last the furniture was sold and they had left the big house in Queen's-gate for a tiny flat in West. Kensington, Mrs. Lennox seemed fairly well satisfied. She praised her daughter loudly for her good management and sweet kindness; and Jessica, feeling well rewarded for her hours of anxiety and fatigue, began to think how she could settle down to make a little money. "There'll be barely enough left, when all Nor father's debts are paid, for the rent and most common necessaries of life," she told herself; "so now work, real work, must be done, Jessica Lennox. No more playing for you. • Henceforth you are a working woman." With this intention she took a couple of small rooms in a street off the High-street, Kensington, and set up with a hopeful and courageous heart as a bookbinder.

In this emergency Florence Fairbairn, her mother's first cousin, proved an invaluable friend. The girl's sturdy independence was a revelation to her, accustomed as she was to the prettY, frivolous maidens she met in society, thinking of little but their own pleasure and adornment, their one object being to amuse themselves first, and ultimately secure a wealthy husband.. Compared with these contemptible creatures, Jessica seemed a heroine, and the kind-hearted woman resolved to help her in every way she could. So she. went round her rich acquaintances blowing the girl's horn, telling them how beauifully she bound books, and how fashionable it was to have all one's. pet poets and authors in one's drawing-room in elegant and artistic covers. In a certain set Mrs. Fairbairn was looked im to as a person of taste, and her word was law. So,very soon,good-orders poured into the little studio in Kensington. Two or three nice girls followed as pupils, and in a few months Jessica was fairly well launched, and doing useful and paying work as a binder of books. "Jessica's caprice will be her saving after all," Mrs. Fairbairn would say. "She's a good girt. and I'm delighted with her; but she deserved 0. better fate. A good and wealthy husband is whir she ought to have, lovely as she is; but, of course " with a shrug of her shoulders.—"all that is over now. Working for her bread, shut up in a studio from morning till night with a set of girls in blue overalls, where on earth could such a being come from ?"

Such thoughts as these never troubled Jessica, however. She was happy at her work and deeply thankful Hist she was getting on so well. -

Years went ever, and with various fluctuations— for in every business there are good days and had— Jessica progressed. She had a good deal of artistic taste, and her designs for binding were grentty appreciated and sought after. The work took time and labour; but as she liked it and it paid her well, she was content.

"You're a marvellous girl," Mrs. Fairhairn rhid one day, glancing round the little studio. "Is there anything more I can do for you ? " Jessica laughed gaily. "My dear fairy godmother, I think not. I could do very well with another pupil. But---" . • "You shall have her. I feel sure I shall find one soon. Au revoir. I'm off to look her up amongst ft:iends and admirers." Jessica smiled. It was a relief to know that. She would have resented the idea of Adrian Barton looking upon her as a charity and sending his sister to take lessons in bookbinding in order to help her. Now that she was persuaded that this was not the case, she was delighted to welcome pretty Phyllis as a pupil. Her days at the studio were pleasant ones for Phyllis Barton, and she conceived quite a romantic affection for her clever and beautiful teacher. At home she raved about her from morning till night, and Adrian, having a shrewd suspicion that the bookbinder of Kensington was the Jessica Lennox that he had known many years before, longed with a great longinc, to renew his acquaintance with her; but a certain shyness held him back. • He did not dare to intrude upon her. "It is so long since we met," he told himself. "She has probably forgotten my very name. If-- Oh, Jessica! I could not' bear to be greeted as a stranger. Through all these years I have thought of you, and dreamt of seeing you again, and yet now— I will not force myself upon you. If we are to meet it must come about naturally; but, thank -Heaven, I may see you sometimes, gaze upon you from afar. You are as lovely as ever, and have proved yourself a noble woman. Heaven bless you!" When Phyllis had been going to the studio for some weeks she caught cold, and the doctcr forbade her leaving the house till it was quite well. Much vexed at this sudden interruption of her happy days in the society of her beloved friend, she wrote a despairing letter to Jessica, who at once hurried round to Queen's-gate to console and comfort her. Upon the stairs as she was leaving Jessica came suddenly face to face with Adrian Barton, and, looking at him with her sweet, clear eyes, held out her ha.wi. • "You have forgotten me?" she said, softly. "I am Jessica Lennox!" "Indeed I have not," he cried; "that would be impossible. I have thought of and longed for this moment since the day we parted, Miss Lennox." "Oh, that is kind!" with a quick, bright blush. "And "—laughing nervously—"all the things we used to talk about have come true. We have changed places, as it were. I have had a struggle, but it's over. I shall never "—gaily—"make a fortune, but I am content. We women don't ask for much."

And then she passed away from him, smiling; and he, dazed and bewildered with joy, stood with throbbing heart and misty eyes gazing helplessly after her retreating figure. During the following week they met frequently by the little invalid's couch, and .the awkwardness of the first meeting over they fell back into their old easy ways again. With one on each side of her Phyllis was supremely happy; but as her cold yielded to treatment and she knew she would soon be well, a new terror took possession of her.. Jessica would now never come to see her. Their pleasant, cosy evenings would be at an end for ever. It was too bad, and with all the wilfulness of a spoilt child she resolved to malinger a little, just to keep her dear friend near her for some time longer. Never suspecting what was going on Jessica grew anxious, and, speaking to Adrian upon the subject one evening, she strongly advised him to bring a specialist to see and examine his little sister. "She looks fragile," she said. "It would be wrong to allow a cold to settle on her. The results might be fatal ! " ' Adrian smiled. lie knew and understood Miss Phyllis better than Jessica did. 'EVENTS OF :THE WEEK.

TEE STRUGGLE IN THE FAR EAST.

AIVREN

. , : - •

The siege of Port Arthur will go down to .history as one of the great sieges of ,the world, and General Stoessel has written his own name and those of his men large on the page of heroes. For some weeks the fall has been daily expected, but the persistent assaults of the Japanese, who have been 'prodigal of pluck and life, have been met wisth equal determination by the defenders: The news at the time of writing would seem to show that the last hours of the gallant defence have come, as it appears that the Japanese have captured all the outlying fortifications and , have actually established themselves on the parade-ground, from which they will deliver the final assault upon .the citadel of .Antstishan at the north-west of the town and the fortresses on Golden Hill and 'Tiger's Tail Promontory. These, however, still mean serious fighting, as the whole scheme of defences has been drawn up on the lines of a fight to the death. General Stoessel has sworn that Port Arthur shall. be his to nb, and it is said that in the last e.xtr(mi y a desperate sortie will be made in

pre.erence to surrender. But so close a censorship are the Japanese keeping that all the news is fragmentary and unofficial. Meanwhile General Kuroki, though once more moving upon General Kuropatkin, has been delaying his final attack upon Liaoyang until the fall of Port Arthur, and his renewed activity seems to presage the end of the siege. Unquestionably a great crisis has been reached, and the west is eagerly awaiting news of the east. General Fukushima, whose portrait we give to-day, on account of his -great linguistic abilities (he speaks English, French, German, Russian, and Chinese), has during the war • been in charge of the military attaches and war correspondents, and, owing to the unflinching policy of keeping the latter back in Tokio as long as possible, it is a great testimonial to his tact and good humour that things have gone so smoothly. He has a very distinguished career behind him and will always be emembered for his famous ride across Russia and Siberia in 1891. He is well-known in Europe as for four years he was military attache at Berlin and he came over to England for the Corona- tion on the staff of General Oku. General Fukushima, who is fifty years of age, also came into contact with European forces as chief of the Japanese staff during the Boxer rebellion.

ANOTHER NEW JUDGE.

By the appointing of Mr. A. T. Lawrence, K.C., to the King's Bench Division in succes- sion to the late Sir Robert Wright, a worthy successor has been found. It was expected for many weeks that Mr. Lawrence would be offered the position and now that it has become an es- tablished fact general satisfac- tion has been given not only to the legal pro- fession, to whom the qualities of Mr. Lawrence are well known, but also to the great body of the public who have become acquainted with his judicial ability when sit- ting as Commis- sioner of Assize. It has been

pointed out that

some little in- _onvenience may be caused by the fact that we have already one Mr. Justice Lawrence, but a Way cut of the difficulty will .be by refer- ring to the new judge as Mr. Justice A. T. Lawrence. It is not the 'first time that the Judicial Bench has a double of this kind, for in the reign of • William IV. we had a Bain Parke (afterwards Lord Wensleydale) and a Mr. Justice Park, but. the .or was in the Exchequer and the other in the Common Fleas • Division, distinctions • which no longer exist. The incident also recalls the fact that for many years Dr. Henry Phillpotts was occupying the episcopal see of Exeter while Dr. Henry Philpett was Bishop of Worcester. "Henry of Exeter" often alluded to the more northern prelate with the very similar name as "my peculiar. brother.". Mr. A. T. Lawrence thoroughly merits the confidence bestowed upon him. Somewhat slow in method, he is very sure as regards his law, and scrupulouily careful in ascertaining fact. He is Recorder of 'Windsor ands Deputy- Lieutenant of the County of Nairn. Another blow to _ the doctrine gut the nearest way to the Bench is through the House of Com- mons, Mr. Lawrence has never been a member of Parliament, nor even a .professed politician. When Mr. Justice Farwell was made a judge five years ago it was. said that ,his best friends had no idea of his political views. Hardly as much can be said of Mr. Lawrence, though it ,is evident he owes his appointment solely to the reputation he' has made in the courts. .

DEATH OP DEAN HOLE.

Universal regret will be expressed by the death of the venerable Dean Hole, who passed away at Rochester in his eighty-fifth year. He had enjoyed remarkably good health until about the commencement of the present year, and his stay at Ramsgate—his favourite seaside resort—at Easter did no good, and he returned to Rochester in a _ _

ierious condition, suffering from heart trouble and dropsy. Afterwards he rallied wonder- fully, and when Mr. Chamberlain visited Rochester the Dean was able to receive the ex-Colonial Secretary in a bath chair and accompany him round the deanery grounds. The Very Rev. Samuel Reynolds Hole, D.D., Dean of Rochester, was the head of one of the old county families of England, and was born in 1819, at Caunton Manor, Notts. As an ecclesiastic, a preacher, a platform orator, a rose-grower, an advocate for hunting and all true sports, he was one of the best-known and most popular Church dignitaries of the day, and made hosts of friends amongst all classes, both at home and in America. nx many years, at each succeeding Lent, he delivered a course of midday addresses at St. Paul's Cathedral, attracting thousands to hear him. His affection and respect for working men was shown not only in his addresses to them, but in a hymn which he wrote to be sung at special services, begin- ning, "Sons of Labour dear to Jesus." He wrote also a "Hymn for Children," and a "Hymn for Time of War " ; and when Queen Victoria died he wrote a funeral hymn, "God rest our Queen," which was set to music by Mr. Bertram Luard Selby, or- ganist of Rochester Cathedral. He had had

DEAN HOLE. considerable experience of missions, ana dignitary of the Chlirch ever gained, the affection and " the confidence of "nil sorts and conditions of men" more thoroughly than did the late Dean Hole. His relations with the citizens of Rochester were always of the most kindly character, and in May, 1901, an address of congratulation was adopted for presentation on the fortieth anniversary of his wedding day, the hope being added that the Dean and Mrs. Hole might long be spared in health and happiness to dwell among them. The late Dean's only son is Mr. Samuel Hugh Francklin Hole, barrister- at-law, who served as a lieutenant with the City Imperial Volunteers in South Africa, where he greatly distinguished himself at Zoutpan's Drift.

THE FATHER OF THE BAR.

Despite the proverbial atmosphere of the Law Courts, barristers are by no means a short-lived race. Indeed, to become a judge in itself is synonymous with having passed middle age, and the head of the Bench, Lord Halsbury, is just unon eighty years of age. Mit even he

cannot claim to be the Father of the Bar, Bar, nor can Lord Brampton, - who in a few days will be c:::----- . - ) eighty - seven. This honour be- longs to Mr. itif Arthurßurrows, who last week celebrated hi s - ninety - second birthday, an d 414 until a few 1

months ago was a familiar figure

in and about

Old Square, MR. ARTHUR BURROW'S.

where lie had his chambers.

He was caned to the bar at Lincoln's Inn M November, 1836, several months before the late Queen Victoria's accession, and for sixty- seven years he carried on a conveyancing practice at the Chancery Bar. His earliest link with his profession dates back even further, as he entered as a legal student at Lincoln s Inn in 1830. Mr. Burrows is five years senior in the matter of call to Lord Gr'mthorpe, who is doyen of the King's Coonsel, and Lord Macnaughten, who is Senior Lord of Appeal in Ordinary, was only born in the year that Mr. Burrows entered at Lincoln's Inn ! Of the other leaders of the legal profession, nearly all could in point of age have been Mr. Burrows' sons, and many his grandsons. The Lord Chief Justice was not born until six years after Mr. Burrows was called, and the Master of the Rolls was not called till Mr. Burrows was fifty-five. It is a splendid record of longevity and the Father of the Bar carries the good wishes of the whole of his profession with him.

Majority for Cemetery 49. POLICE AT BOWLS.

On Tuesdayafternoeu, a bowling main took place at the Longford Hotel between teams representing the Police forces of Warrington and St. Helens. Consider- able interest was taken in the fixture, for although the Police of the two boroughs are old :rivals at cricket, Tuesday was the first occasion on which they opposed each other at bowls, although in the ranks of both forces are included some very enthusiastic bowlers. The result of the contest was avery decided victory for the local men in blue, the figures being : Warrington, 186; St. Helens, 137. After play the two teams sat down to a substantial tea, purveyed in a highly satis- factory manner by .the landlord of the Longford Hotel, Mr. Joseph Horlock. A splendid feeling of comrade- ship prevailed, and the St. Helens men went home thoroughly pleased with their day's outing.

THE STORY OF A GREAT DISCOVERY.

How it Affects Warrington.

Ancient Greece will always be remembered for the fine types of manhood supplied by the heroes of her battlefields and the athletes of her arenas. Still more will these heroes and athletes themselves be remem- bered for the custom they bequeathed to later ages of healing sore and injured places on their bodies by the external application of some secret balm or salve. Th Greek charioteers did not emerge from their mad races without some severe bruise or gaping wound; and to anoint each injury carefully with their favourite balm was an indispensable part of the day's programme. The old Roman heroes, too, who were injured by the wild beasts in the now ruined Colosseum of Rome, had to adopt similar methods of he.ging. If we travel back to the days of the Bible—where we read of the wonderful virtue of the balm of Gilead—we find that this external " rubbing "has prevailed right from the earliest times, and the only explanation of its survival, amidst so i many changes n science, seems to lie in the fact that :the external use of salves and balms is dictated to us by Nature herself. Our own instinct tells us to rub a part that hurts: and in Zarn-buk the ideal substance to apply to an injured or diseased surface is universally believed to have been found at last. We owe its discovery to the researches of an eminent chemist, who recognised the deficiencies of existing preparations such as oint- ments, salves, liniments, and embrocations. It isa well known fact that these old-fashioned preparations are not only Imperfect in their action, but they frequently contain quantities of rancid animal fat and mineral pro- duets of a harmful nature. In the belief that, as ature had given to men the instinct to rub, she must surely have secreted somewhere the proper substances to rub with, the great chemist made a search for them amongst the rarest medicinal herbs growing beyond the borders of civilization.

All available knowledge was gleaned about these plants; and it was discovered how some of them could be made to yield gums and juices possessing healing, soothing, and antiseptic properties of a wonderful order. Costly experiments were made with mixtures or blend- ings of these juices, and at last a combination with astonishing powers as a healer and antiseptic was secured. To this unique preparation the name of Yam- buk was given, and this is the great healer which is to-day generally regarded as a household necessity.Ziam- buk has fulfilled the expectations of its discoverer, and many medical men to-day freely, extol its merits. It possesses a wide range of usefulness, being especially recommended for cuts, bruises, burns, scalds, sprains, stiffness, weak ankles, swol,lftn joints, bad legs, blind and bleeding piles, running sores, eczema, ulcers, pimples, boils, rash, raw chapped hands, scalp irritation, sore heads, barber's rash, raw chin after shaving, insect-bites, nettle-stings, sore throat and chest, sore and aching feet, chilblains, cold sores, festering sores, poisoned wounds, and other diseased, injured, and inflamed or irritated conditions of the skin. 'tubbed well into the parts affected, Zam-buk gives great relief from neuralgia, toothache, sciatica, lumbago, rheumatism, chest and back pains, and colds. A box may be obtained of any chemist for one-and-three-half-pence, or two-and-nine for a large family size (which contains nearly four times the quantity in the one-and-three-halfpenny); or post free for the same price direct from the Zam-buk co., Red- cross-street, London, E.C. A free sample box may be had by sending a Id. stamp to the Zam-buk provincial depot, Greek-street, Leeds, and mentioning this paper. Every home in Warrington needs it.

PIANO-PLAYING FOR SEVENTEEN HOURS,

William Frost, pianist at a Hull public-house, sat down before a piano at six a.m. on Wednesday with the intention of trying to establish a record for continuous playing. A Bradford man recently played for twelve hours without a cessation. Frost's ambition was to keep at the instrument until eleven p.m., making seven- teen hours. He succeeded in his object, never stopping throughout the day. While taking refreshments he played with the left hand. At the finish he was ex- hausted, and his bands were much swollen.

A plague of wasps prevails in the southern part of Lincolnshire. and the fruit crops are suffering severely from their ravages. Plums are not over plentiful this year,but the hint is being so badly damaged that some g rowers are gathering the crops before they are ripe, so hat they can put them on the market in a saleable ndition.

In order that any player may judge for him- self how difficult it is to hit a ball without im- parting any side to it, let him try the following experiment:—

Place a ball on the centre of the D line and hit it straight up the table (so that it travels over the centre, pyramid and billiard spots) with sufficient strength to cause it to return to baulk. If the ball be hit truly in the centre it will return over exactly the same line by which it travelled up the table, but if hit ever so slightly to the right of the centre it will return .to the right of the starting point, and to the left of this point if hit slightly to the left, and a, player is striking a very true ball if he can re- peatedly bring it back to within two or three inches of the starting. point. The ordinary player will find that more often than not ha will be six or eight inches and sometimes a foot iiide of the mark. There can be no better prac- tice for true hitting than this playing a ball up and down the table, and the stroke should be varied by occasionally hitting the ball with plenty—'of strergth. The faster the- stroke be played the more difficult does it become to strike the ball truly, and often when one thinks he has hit the ball pretty well in the centre, it will cross the baulk line on its return journey half a yard or more wide of its starting point, thus conclusively proving that in striking hard the ball was hit quite differently from what was intended.

Side is simply rotation or spiii on a ball, and the more side a ball carries the faster will it spin. When a side-laden ball is travelling fast the spin is not so noricealle to the eye as when the ball is travelling slowly, but this is simply because a quickly moving ball cannot be kept under close observation. A trial of the follow- ing little experiment will, however, clearly de- monstrate to the eye how quickly a fast travel. ling ball may be rotating with side.

DIAGRAM 2.

Attach a small piece of paper to a ball in such a way that it projects from the surface of the ball as shown in Diagram 2 (this can be done by simply wetting the paper.) Place the ball on the table with the paper projecting exactly half-way up, and at the side of the- ball. If the ball be now struck on either side, the paper will show in a very marked manner the rotation of the ball: If, however, the ball be hit in the centre the paper will show forward rotation only.

In more than one book on billiards it has been stated that more side can be imparted to a ball by a slow stroke than by a fast one. This is, however, quite incorrect, for the cleaner and harder a hall be struck with side the more spin is there communicated to the ball, but the same amount of spin on a ball is much more effective in altering the angle'of reflection from a cushion when the ball is travelling slowly or at only a moderate pace than when it is travelling very fast. This is, however, simply owing to the yielding nature of rubber cushions. When a ball strikes a cushion with any force the rubber gets compressed, and the ball is momentarily more or less imbedded in the cushion, and the tendency of this is always to diminish the angle of reflection, and thus reduce in a greater or less degree the effect of the side or spin on the ball. When the cushions are very yielding this alteration in the angle of reflection is very marked indeed, and the full effect of side can only be obtained when the ball is travelling slowly.

When side has been imparted to a ball it is retained -by the ball until the friction of the cloth and the check that every contact with a cushion has on the spin wears it away. As a rule side wears off a ball before it stops travel- ling, but often when a ball laden with side hits another ball full in the face, or nearly so, it will rotate round a vertical axis long after it has ceased moving along the cloth. The explanation being that the full impact has taken practically all the momentum out of the ball, and thus the side or spin is given full play. As a rule the better the player, the more side can he impart to a ball. Novices generally miss- cue when attempting to put on a lot of side. and the reason they ao so is that they try to hit the cue ball too much on the edge. To hit a ball on its extreme edge without miss-cueing is an im- possibility, and novice& should never attempt to hit a ball very near tlte edge. Many poor players, on the other hand, though taking their aim at the extreme edge of the ball,- and even sometimes .with seven-eighths of the diameter of the tip 13.?.y0nd the ball, change their aim at the actual moment of striking, and hit their ball on a part very little removed from the centre, and are afterwards surprised to find how little side has been imparted to the ball. The poor player, therefore, when playing with side, should always :take care to hit his ball where he intends to, but should not attempt to plant his cue on the extreine edge of the ball. Those who have closely watched professional players may often have noticed how much side these players can impart to their ball without striking it very close to the edge. This is very noticeable in gentle strokes in a top-of-the-table break. The reason that a good player can impart so much side to his ball when playing a gentle stroke is because the cue bail, hit quite gently, is struck with a clean crisp stroke. A ball struck in this manner will have more spin imparted to it than if hit with a stroke which is a modified kind of a push.

• The strokes shown on diagrams 3 and 4 are good practice strokes for the ordinary player who wishes to increase the amount of side ,he can im- part to a ball, and for a better player are a test of how much side he can -give a ball. If two

the ball on to the bottom cushion a foot away from the corner pocket can give a ball as much side as is required for most strokes. Of course, in trying this test stroke different cushions will give different results; though the difference will never be very great provided that the cushions are in decent condition. Care must-, however, be taken not to play the stroke with more than sufficient strength to bring the ball across the table, otherwise the full effect of the side will not be obtained owing to the ball being, as pre- viously explained, more or less pressed into the rubber through striking the side cushion full in the face. This fact should always be borne in mind when playing to disturb a tall or balls in baulk. Such a stroke is generally played with plenty of pace, and when so played more side must be imparted to the cue ball (to obtain the same effect) in order to counterbalance the squarer throw-off of a fast ball.

DIA.GRAM 4.

Two examples of check side destroying the angle ,1 reflection.

In practising the strokes shown on Diagram 4, the cue ball should at first be aimed at a point high up on the side cushion, and after being thrown from this cushion on to the top cushion, •the check side (right hand side in A stroke) will bring the ball back to the side cushion. Every time that the stroke is successful, the side cushion should be struck at a point farther and farther away from the top pocket until a point is reached lower down than which the player is always unsuccessful. This prac- tice stioke is a fine test bel2ween two players to decide which one can give most side to a ball. As a matter of fact, it is possible on many tables to strike the side cushion only just above the centre pocket and still. bring the ball back to the side cushion above the centre pocket as shown by B stroke on the diagram.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

St. Peter's Church, Woolston.

The annual charity sermons were preached in St. Peter's Churn, Wodston, on Sunday, and there were large congregations. The preacher at the morning service was the Rev. J. Lowe, rector of the Mission. In the afternoon the Rev. Father Magrath from St. Marl's, Latchford, took for his text the words of St. Paul "By the grace of God I am what I am, and His grace in me bath not been void." The Rector thanked all those who had helped to make the services such a success.

===== 0002601_19040903_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
Organ Recital at Walton.

At Walton Church on Sunday afternoon an organ recital in aid of the funds of the village library was given by Mr. T. Burgess Lane, organist of Darwen Parish Church and for ten years organist at Walton. A vocal solo," 0 Divine Redeemer," was rendered by is Houghton, in the place of Mrs. Darwall, who was unable to be present. The programme consisted of the follow- ing items which were all submitted in a highly credit- able manner :—Prelude in E flat (H. Smart); organ con- certo in B flat (Handel) ; "The Question," "The "Answer" (Wolstenholmo) ; Toccato in G " (Dubois) "Allegro Pomposo " (Vincent), (a), Melodie (Rubinstein), (b), " Salut ..I.'Amour " (Eiger) Triumphal March." (Callaerts).

The Open-Air lervicas at 11111 Otte.

At Hill Cliffe on Sunday evening, the last of the. series of evening open-air services was held. The weather was favourable and a good congregation gathered under the spreading branches of the big tree and in the lane. The Pastor (the Rev. j. S: Hughes) preached, taking as his theme "Prodigals." He ex- plained the three types of prodigals as given in Scrip- ture, and showed prodigals in their relation to the home, to society, and to God, revealing the cause of prodigality to be extravagance-Textravagant extravagant living, and those leading to embezzlements gambling, artificial living, and frauds, suicides and murders. Special hymns were sung, Mr. James Strange presiding at the organ. The summer has been so genial thtt m.)re gatherings have been held during this season than since these services were commenced in 1899.

The strength of Eng land indeed of any nation, was not to be measured by her army or her navy, but was to be found in the purity and manhood of her sons and in thevirtue of her daughters. He had no hesitation in saying that the weakness and paralysis of our nation, and also largely of our industry, was due to the attitude the nation had assumed during the last decade or two towardssome important, matters. It was not only a, fact that a man reaped something but there was a close and inseparable connection between the sowing and the reaping. As an illustration of this the preacher took the case of Pharoah, who issued the cruel edict that all the male children of the Israelites should be destroyed and then raped the death of the firstborn among the Egyptians. Jacobalso reaped the fruit of his deception of his aged father, when in later years he was deceived by his own sons. It was quite true that God forgave our sins and put them behind His back for ever, but it was nevertheless true that we reaped th enatural result of our sin. There was,however,another side to the text, which should give encouragement to Christian men and. women who work and make their sacrifices for Christ.

Departure of the Rev. W. C. J. Neville.

Presentations from Padgate and Latchford Wesleyans.

After three years' work in the Warrington Wesleyan circuit the Rev. W. C. J. Neville left this town on Thursday to enter upon his duties in the Green Hill circuit, Derby. During his stay in Warrington, Mr. Neville has made a large circle of friends, who, while regretting his departure, will wish him everyisuccess in his new sphere. The rev, gentleman will be suc- ceeded here by the Rev. Joseph Manning, of King-street circuit, Green Hill, who arrived in War- rington yesterday and will preach at Penketh Church to-morrow (Sunday), morning and evening. Fitting reference to Mr. Neville's departure was made at Pa,dgate Church on Sunday evening on the con- clusion of the memorial service to the late Mr. Edwin Richardson. Short speeches were delivered by Mr. William Bennett and Mr. Fred Wright, who paid a tribute to the valuable and raithful services Mr. Neville had rendered to Methodism. It was stated that as a mark of esteem, Mr. Neville had been privately presented with a purse of gold subscribed by the congregation. Mr. Neville thanked the friends for their kindness to him during the past three years, and expressed the pleasure it had afforded him to work in the circuit. He had never met with more appreciative people than those living at Padgate. Alluding to the presentation, he said he found that the list of sub- scribers containedj the names of 110 people, rich and poor, old and young. He was pleased to leave the church in a prosperous condition, and he was especially glad to know that so many young people sc ere connected with the guild and other organizations. The hymn "God be with you till we meet again," was sung at the close of the service. Mr. Neville has had special charge of the work at -Padrate and Latchford, and it is pleasing to learn that under his care, progress has been made. A debt of £3OO has been cleared off the Latchford Church trust, and the members are hoping that before long a schoolroom will be erected To show their appreciation of Mr. Neville's work, the members of the Latchford Wesley Guild presented him with a Swan fountain pen with gold bands. Had it not been for the lack of 11,,commodation the guild work at Latchford, in which Mr. Neville has taken great interest would no doubt have been still more extensive and. successful. The Rev. Joseph Manning has been for the last three years in Derby, and has travelled nine years. Before going to Derby he spent three years at Sheffield and two at Beccles, his first appointment from college.

===== 0002601_19040903_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, September 3, 1904.

doee not concern Ireland alone. The rest

of the United Kingdom sutlers from the overwork of Parliament. We want devolution pretty badly in this country, and it is equally needed in Wales and Scotland. Mr. LLOYD- GEORGE has sketched a plan for making the Welsh National Council the centre of a new and extended local administration in Wales. Nobody has yet discovered that the Welsh National Council is a " thinly-disguised " instrument for "separation." It is significant that the "Times " takes no heed of the case for devolution in Great Britain,although it harks back to Lord RANDOLPH CHuRcHrLL's declaration for " equality, similarity, and simul- taneity " in the local governing institutions of the whole kingdom. This, we are told, summed up the "British system of local government, with the acquiescence of the Unionist party as a whole." The Unionists were so far from acquiescing that they put off the extension of British local government to Ireland for ten years. But the point now is that if we should see fit to amplify that system for ourselves on the lines of devolu- tion,it will be impossible to deny the same reform to the Irish. Clearly the Times does not re- lish this application of " equality, similarity, and simultaneity." It will be no easy matter to make out that local business in England, Scotland, and \Vales is in a perfectly satistactory state, and that only politicians of no consequence desire any change anywhere. The bulk of the Irish Unionists may agree with the " Times " rather than with Lord DUNRAVEN but they are the men who denounced the local government scheme which commanded the "acquiescence of the Cnionist party as a whole." An extension of that scheme may not satisfy the Nationalists. They have not met Lord DUNRAVEN'S overtures with the ardour of politicians on the look-out for "thinly-disguised Home Rule of the Gladstonian pattern. But the real question is whether the programme of the Irish Reform Association will make for the growth of public spirit in Ireland as distinguished from party spirit. If so, it is a very hopeful sign.

There are signs of an RUSSIAN" DIPLOMACY. accommodating

spirit at St. Peters- burg in regard to the question of neutral shipping. " Mr. BALFOUR'S last speech," says the" Novosti," proves that the conclusion of an agreement would be easy in view of the pacific dispositions of the two Governments." A joint commission repre- senting the Russian Foreign Office and Admiralty is to be" held immediately," under the presidency ot Professor MARTENS, and " the result will pro - ably be known before the end of the week." If Professor MARTENS could p3rsuade the Russian Admiralty to act in accord with Count LAMS- DORFF'S department, we might feel a little more confidence in Russia's pacific disposition. But it is not exactly encouraging to learn that the British and American views "re- garding the distinction betweeh conditional and absolute contraband have been long under con- sideration both at the Ministry for Foreign Affairs and the Ministry of Marine." The case of the Smolensk was also considered by both departments with mature deliberation, and yet the assurances given to us by Count LAMSDORFF are still unknown to the commander of that cruiser. Just about the time when we were told that her "special com- mission" had "expired," she must have been in- structed by the Ministry of Marine to leave the Red Sea and resume her commission in Sonth African waters. There is even more method in her second adventure than in the first, for, as the military critic of the "Times " points out, she is much more advantageously placed for meddling with our sea-borne commerce to the Far East than she was before. We have to remember this when we read the smooth words of the " Novosti," and especially when we note that "Russia has the right to ask that British trade with Japan should not be of an exclusively contra- band character." This indicates that fixed idea which will continue to be the chief obstacle to our agreement unless Professor MARTENS and his Commission can remove it. It is the idea that everything in a British ship must be absolute contraband. This assumption greatly simplifies the work of the Priz3 Court at Vladi- vostock, where the German steamer Arabia was released when a small portion of her cargo had been confiscated, although the same liberal treat- ment is denied to British vessels. Moreover, the owners of the German Thea appear to have been compensated for the destruction of their ship; hut we have not yet heard of any compensation to the owners of the British Knight Commander. I It has been contended that Russia has not departed from the principle of even-handed con- fiscation in her dealings with contraband. She has seized more British ships than German ships, because there are more British ships to seize. This is true ; but there is another consideration, which is rather disquieting. The German liners bought by Russia to act as craiisers, with that per- fectly legitimate authority which the Smolensk does not possess, are beginning to assert themselves on the ocean highways. Is it likely that they will impartially harass German shipping ? Toe great trading companies from which they were purchased have intimate relations with the German Government, and it seems im- probable that the German Government would sanction the sale of ships to be usei for injuring German commerce. This may have something to do with the readiness of German shipowaers to take risks from which British shipowners shrink, I and with the difference between the German and British rates of insurance on freights to the Far East.C3rtainly it is by no means safe to assume that all these points have not neat] sattled by a diplo- matic arrangement between Germany and Russia. The KAISER is likely to be much better acquainted than the British Government with the aims of the Russian Ministry of Marine. It is significant that I the Peking correspondent of the " Times," writing as far back as J uly 14th, foresaw that the Russian warships, sallying from Port Arthur, would have as their objective not Vladivostock, but the Ger- man port of Kiau-chau. It was well known in China that they were " bound for Kiau-chati harbour there to be dismantled to escape falling into the, hands of the Japanese." To suppose that what actually happened was not carefully pre-arranged between St. Petersburg and Berlin is to suppose that every Foreign Office in the world is as dilatory and short-sighted as our own. It is not the practice of Germany to do anybody a good turn without an equivalent; and we may perceive some signs of the equivalent :in the present case in the action of the converted German liners under the Russian naval ensign, and in the vagaries of the Prize Court of Vladivostock. At all events, the need of vigilance in the protection of British com- merce grows more rather than less acute with the progress of the war.

As Parliament is THE NEW IRISH admittedly overwhelmed MOVE NIENT with business with which it cannot cope, there is nothing surprising in the fact that certain Irish Unionists have discovered the necessity of extend- ing the principle of "devolution." Lord DUNEAVEN and his colleagues have made this the basis of the new political body, to which they give the name of the Irish Reform Association. They maintain that devolution, "while avoiding matters of Imperial concern, and subjects of common interest to the kingdom as a whole, would be beneficial to Ireland, and would relieve the Imperial Parliament of a mass of business with which it cannot now deal satisfactorily, and which occupies its time to the detriment of much more important concerns." This very moderate and reasonable statement has thrown some organs of Unionist opinion into a fever of alarm. Lord DtiNRAVEN is severely lectured by the "Times " for his apostasy. "What this policy amounts to is. nothing more nor less than the revival, in a slightly weakened and thinly disguised form, of Mr. GLADSTONE'S fatal enterprise of 1886 under cover of an extension of local self-government." Lord DUNRIVEN and his friends are further told that they are not persons of any consequence, and that the great body of Unionists will repudiate their proposals. But the " Times," with a slight inconsistency, remembers they are pretty much the same people who brought about the Land Conference, which was the prelude to Mr. WYNDHA M'S new Land Purchase Act. Our contemporary did not like that policy either; -)ut it was carried out all the same. There is an evident uneasiness lest its original authors, despite their personal insignificance, should set going another movement which will have moment- ous consequences. Lord DUNRAV EN and his friends have certainly no desire to meddle with the Union, or to do anything towards the creation of an Irish Parliament. But they are dissatisfied with the administration of Ireland from Dublin Castle, especially in regard to finance; and they think that a decentralised system would manage it better. And they believe that the co-operation of Irish business men for this purpose can be effected with- out prejudicing what they regard as the Unionist case.

in principle this opinion becomes all the more reasonable when we consider that it

The Mectical Officer's reference in his annual re- port to the unpaved streets and passages has not only had the effect of drawing public attention to the backwardness of this class of work, but it has resulted in a counter-blast from the Borough Surveyor, who has also reported on the same sub- pet, presumably at the request of the Paving and Sewerage Committee. Mr. Longdin questions the Medical Officer's figures and alludes to 16 passages and streets which had been done so far back as eight years, 27 ditto whica are mentioned twice over, and 8 which "can- not be found." Perhaps the latter have slowly sunk out of sight during the wet seasons which have been experienced in recent years. The doctor's figures are, however, not materi- ally affected by Mr. Longdin's revision—which still leaves about 300 streets and passages requiring attention. A good deal evidently depends on the point of view in this matter of street paving. The Borough Sur- veyor says that " during the past ten years no less than 13 miles of streets and passages have been paved," and that "only 6 miles remain to be done." Tne Committee is there- fore some six miles in arrears,—a pretty severe handicap we should think. Basing our calculation on Mr. Longdin's own information it will take the Committee nearly 5 years to cover these six miles, and by the end of that period there will be a few more miles of streets and passages requiring atten- tions. It hardly seems as clear to us as it does to the Borough Sarvey-)r that "the department has made every endeavour to keep pace with the rapid building operations that have taken place within the Borough."

There are worse fates to endure than that which falls to the lunatic, especially if he be fortunate enough to be sent to Winwick Asylum. A mem- ber of the Chorley Board of Guardians visited the institution just beyond o•ir borders last week, and was much impressed by the splendours which were revealed. "There was a piano there which must have cost over £100" he said, while a fellow member remarked : " the Empress ball- room is not in it with the entertainment room." No wonder the "Daily News" suggests that the BlacApool trippers should take the place of the insane, being better able to appreciate the glories of the asylum. What with the improvements in modern asylums, workhouses, and prisons it hardly seems a matter for much congratulation if one is sound mentally, morally and physically. Lunatics, tramps and gaol-birds may with reason smile in a superior way at the hardworking, honest man who worries himself into a premature grave with striv- ing to support them and make ends meet.

There were one or two interesting points raised at an otherwise dull meeting of the Education Committee on. Monday evening. For the first time in the history of the committee there. was some approach to a clashing of denominational interests. Seven schools have been "passed "by the Inspector as suitable for the traEning of pupil teachers, and as only one of the seven is on- denominational, the Nonconformists on the committee lost no time in raising a protest against the apparent injustice. Any sectarian disagreement was deprecated on all hands, and an amendment that the matter be referred back in order that the circumstances might be brought to the notice of the Inspector found almost unanim- ous acceptance. What promised to be a religious difficulty was therefore avoided for the time being, and we hope when the next recommendation is made by the inspector it will be more in conform- ity with the just claims of the Nonconformists, who are able to claim a majority of the school- children in the town. There is such an amicable understanding between the members of the Com- mittee that we have no fear of any serious diffi- culty or dispute arising.

The accommodation question was again before the committee, and it is likely to remain a stumbling block to educationallprogress for some time to come. Years upon years of a hand-to-mouth policy have resulted in a state of things which occasions much anxiety. The increase in the number of children eligible for school is as steady as the recurrence of the seasons, while accommodation remains almost at a standstill. In desperation tke School Attend - ance Commitlee has urged once more that tem- porary accommodation—in iron buildings if neces- sary—be at once provided, and the Education Committee has accepted the recommendation. Whether they will act upon it remains to be seen. Sites for new schools are already before the Board of Education awaiting their approval, and many members are inclined to stake all on the rapid erection of the new buildings. But if it will be two years before even one of the new schools is built then the question of tem- porary accommodation ought not to be shelved. Either make-shift structures must be proceeded with at once or some other means devised immediately for relieving the con- gestion in theschools and preventing the forfeiture of the Go vernment grants. A threatened authority —like a threatened man—may live a long time, but it would be the height of folly for the com- mittee toltry the patience of the Education Depart- ment too far. Let us see some practical steps to meet the difficulty taken at once. The combined wisdom of so enlightened and erudite a committee ought to be equal to the occasion.

Mr. A. J. Jolley raised a peculiar point in regard to the maintenance of two local children in the Manchester schools for the deaf and dumb. The School .tittenaance Committee has fixed 2s. per month as the amount of the parental contribution in one of the cases, and here Mr. JoHay falls foul of the committee. In his view, they are usurping the office of the Poor Law Guardians in taking a child off the hands of its parents at so low a figure. Mr. Jolley may be quite right or. the question of principle, but the committee has to fix so me ratio of:maintenance upon the parents, and the specific sum must of necessity be dependent largely upon the ability of the parents to pay. Would Mr. Jolley really have the School Attendance Committee charge an amount commensurate with the actual cost of maintenance whether the parents could afford to pay or not? It appears to us that the result would eventually be the same so far as the ratepayers are concerned—they would have to pay —and it does not much matter whether they pay through the poor rate or the education rate. In this:particular :case the father of the child earns 18s. a week and has a wife and family of seven to maintain out of this munificent wage. How he will find the extra 6d, a week in cash for his child at Manchester puz.z/es us as much as it will probably puzzle him. We should rather like Mr. Jolley to suggest what course the School Attendance Committee should, in his view, really have taken in this particular instance.

Halifax is one of the towns where there is a

ye-law, as in Warrington, directed against the efacing of shop fronts, public buildings and t he like by the striking of matches thereon. In the Yorkshire town the police seem more active than in Warrington and are -attempting the almost hopeless taEk of bringing to book the offenders under this particular bye-law. There was a case the other lay in which a person was summoned fo damaging the south wall of. the Halifar Town Hall to the extent or a penny by striking a match thereon. The defendant challenged the " police to prove the damage and as the prosecuting constable was unable to identify the defendant's scratch from the thou- sands of others the case failed. There is a "tip " here for both police and public, but the latter will find it easier to profit therefrom than the former. How the diligent P.C. is to find a means of identifying the scratch even when he has seen it scratched is a problem for the police. If he chalked it round he would double the damage, ' and if he put a brother officer on guard while he instituted proceedings the authorities would have to double the force and see to the maintenance of the officer on the watch. Is might be cheaper to let the public strike its matches where it wills.

Stockton Heat a newly formed sanitary com- mittee has lose n. time in getting; to work,and the outcome of the oiirst meeting is a request to the District Council to appoint an additional sanitary inspector. At first glance this may seem a rather large order, and no doubt the District Council

will be disposed to hesitate at creating a nv„TV office for the benefit of a particular locality- Tile circumstances, however, appear to justify Stoctou Heath's claims. Of late years the parish has Wide rapid strides, and the duties devolving upon the sanitary officials have correspondingly increased. The Runcorn Rural District embraces a large areal and, as one of the members put it, the inspector cannot always be at Stockton Heath. It is nacos; sary that the parish should receive the fn. attention which a large and growing distrl.ll c; demands, and we hope the District Council Wi view the committee's request in a favourable light.

Since the country will not have conscription, there is tact in asking how much that is valuah.le in that direction it is prepared to acceptb report of the Inter-Departmental Committee oil Pnysical Deterioration, raises a cognate subject' In the "Independent," Mr. J. B. Atkins seeks to inculcate a greater regard for physical culture as a civic and patriotic duty. His survey ranges, from the feeding of infants to the taxation ei ground values, and from free food for needy school children to diluted military drill in secondarY schools. Municipalities must abate the smoke nuisance, provide open spaces, and eliminate slums. There should be compulsory drill in elementary schools on a scientific bisis, beginning with proper breathing, and adapting physical training to all tie functions of generous and sound education. This, it is admitted, the new course of the Educattoh Department already aims to secure. Play- grounds must be provided where no OW grounds exist, and a Gaines Committee should come into touch with local clubs to secure the use of grounds and the help of members for the reasonable provision of sports tor the young. Ali, this of course will tend to the manufacture 01 better material for the legions of war as well as those of peace ; and here the War Office is invited to regard the movement with complacency and sympathy. Finally, a co-ordinating State Depart- ment is necessary, with a Health Minister at its head, into whose purview should come other local public health concerns. Mr. Atkin's proposals cover a wide area, and though they may not he, adopted in their entirety, they contain plenty el good features which will commend them to every judicious student.

31444-Diiical-i- Local Works. An inquest was conducted at the Police Station on Tuesday evening hv Mr. S. Brighouse, County Coroner, touching the death- of Richard Dowes, aged 54, of 45, Gladstone-street who died with startling suddenness on Monday.—The evie.ence showed that deceased had worked as a wire drawer at Messrs. Greening and Co.'s works, Crown-street, for the past five years. On Monday he attended to his duties as usual, and as was his custom did not go home for dinner,but had it at the works. At 3.35 Thomas Wilson the engine driver, happened to go into the ;hop where the deceased was employed, and found him in a sitting position on the stool where he had sat whils the had dinner. Noticing that his appearance was strange, Wilson looked at him closely and found he was dead. Deceased's pipe was lying on the floor, as if it had dropped from his mouth whilst he had been having an after-dinner smoke. It was stated that for some months, deceased had been in a weakly state and appeared to have scarcely any life in him.— A verdict of "Death from natural causes " was returned.—Mr. E. Draper. solicitor, represented Messrs. Greening and Company.
