SUGAR KEEPS DEAR.—Advt.

Mr. H. Greenall (Warrington),coroner of the manor of Hale, held an inquest at the Childe of Hale Hotel, Bale, on Monday, touching the death of Thomas Mason a labourer, aged 18 years, living at the Dungeon Bale Cliff, who met his death by drowning whilst 'bathing in the Mersey on Sunday afternoon--John Hannon, Hale Cliff, said on Sunday he went to bathe With the deceased and they undressed and went into -the river. The deceased could swim a little. After swimming about 12 yards from the bank, witness -thought deceased was getting a bit tired and told him that he had better be getting near the shore. A com- panion named Edwin Store was with them. Witness _went swimming about, and when he saw .deceased last he was standing close by Store. Some littlfi time later they heard a shout, and found that deceased had disappeared. Witness dived repeatedly, but failed to -find the deceased. When the tide receded the deceased's body was left on the bank, about 50 yards away from the place where he had disappeared. The accident happened about 1.30, and the body was recovered about three hours later.—Thomas Joynson, lighthouse keeper, said he had examined the spot where deceased disappeared. There were marks on the mud as if be had slipped. The shore shelved rapidly there, and in about two yards deceased would have been overheal in the water. It was a very dangerous place to bathe. --A verdict of" Accidental drowning" was returned; H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is the KING OF TONICS.-63. SANKEY-STRRRT. Warrington.—Advt. Colonal Richardson's Retirement. Colonel Richardson, the officer commanding the Bth and 40th Regimental Districts whose headquarters are at Orford Barracks, has retiredi from the services. It is a comparatively short time since he took over the command at Orford in succession to Colonel Fitz- herbert, but he has gained many friends in the dis- trict. Colonel Richardson's connection with the troops o' the districts, including the militia and volunteer battalions of the• King's (Liverpool) and South Lancashire Regiments, has been a happy one. The fact of some of the volunteer battalions of the districts being in brigades not.under Colonel Richard- son's command necessarily limited his opportunities of supervising corps subject to his control as regi- mental district commander. Colonel Richardson commanded the Liverpool Brigade in camp at Conway this season, and at Lewaigue, Ramsey, last year. He also inspected the 6th V.B. and Bth Scottish V. 8., as also the Ist Cadet Battalion of the King's Liverpool Regiment specially. His health of late has not been satisfactory. His association with the Liverpool Volunteers has been of a pleasant character, and those Officially in contact with him always received courtesy 'and consideration. For an extended period, Colonel Aichardson commanded the South Lancashire Regi- rnent Under Mr. Arnold-Forster's scheme of army reorganization,the regimental depot system is intended to be concentrated, and several present depots will cease to exist. Probably no successor will be ap- pointed to the position Colonel Richardson vacates, -except, perhaps, as a temporary expedient. At present the Warrington Regimental Districts, as distinct from the double depot command, pass to the Major-General 'commandingthe North-Western district, at Chester.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Webster—Lingley.

A pretty wedding and one which aroused consider- able interest, took Place at St. Peter's Church on Tues- day, the contracting parties being Mr. Frederick Webster, the deputy organist at the church, and Miss Cissie Lingley, grand-daughter of Councillor John Roberts. A large congregation assembled, the bride and bridegroom being well known and esteemed in the parish. Miss Lingley has been if teacher at St. Peter's school and an active church worker, and Mr. Webster, in addition to acting as deputy-organist has discharged the duties of organist at the men's Sunday afternoon services. The church had been beautifully decorated for the occasion with plants and ferns sent by the Mayor (Alderman Bolton). The service was choral and included the well-known hymm "The voice that breathed o'er Eden." The Vicar (Rev. 13—Percival) officiated. Mr. P. Edgar (organist), and Mr. Smith played appropriate selections. The bride, who was given away by Councillor Roberts, was becomingly attired in beaver-coloured voile, trimmed with silk and lace to match, and wore a tulle hat trimmed with lace and autumn leaves. She carried a handsome bouquet of white lilies and orange blossom. She was attended by, two bridesmaids—Miss Webster (sister of the bride- groom) and Miss Nora Pemberton (cousin of the bride) Miss Webster wore a pretty dress of claret-coloured material trimmed with lace, with hat to match, and Miss Pemberton was attired in white silk. Their presents from the bridegroom were, respectively, a gold ring set with emeralds, opals and rubies, and a lace brooch. The bridegroom's gift to the bride was a silver- fitted dressing case, and the bride's present to the bride- groom was a diamond pin. Mr. Fred Yeomans acted as best man. The reception was held at the residence of Councillor Roberts and many guests attended. Among those present, in addition to Councillor Roberts and the bride and bridegroom, were Mrs. Linglev (the bride's mother), the Rev. B. and Mrs. Percival, Mr. and Mrs. T. Pemberton, Mr. and Mrs. John Cowdell, Mr. W. Kay, Mr. Thomas S. Steel, Mr. and Mrs. P. Edgar, Mr. Joseph Heron, Mr. and Mrs. Clayton, Mrs. Yeomans, Mrs. Burns, Mrs. Webster, Mr. and Mrs. H. Roberts, Mrs. Smith, Mrs. Thomas Roberts, Miss Bee, Mr. J. T. Boardman, and others. Congratulatory telegrams were received from the Rev. W. and Mrs. Bracecamp, and the Rev. D. R. and Mrs. James, who were unable to be present. Later in the day the newly-married couple departed for London and Brighton, where the honey- moon is being spent. The bride wore a green travelling costume with a cream hat._

White—Wainwright.

The marriage was solemnized at St. Paul's Church, on Tuesday, in the presence of a large congregation, of Mr. John Ernest White, son of Mr. James White, Fern Bank, Manchester-road, and Miss Clara Inez Wainwright, second daughter of Mr. John Wainwright, Froghall House. Both parties are well known in Warrington, and numerous well-wishers assembled to witness the ceremony. The officiating clergy were the Revs. T. W. Barber and R. H. Bowen. The bride, who was given away by her father, was charmingly attired in a pale blue silk voile dress trimmed with rich lace, and a white felt hat. Her bouquet was composed of choice white roses. The bridegroom's gift to the bride was a gold brooch, and that of the bride to the bridegroom a diamond pin. The bridesmaids were Miss Kathleen Wainwright (sister of the bride) and Miss Kathleen White (sister-of the bridegroom), and they were attired in French muslin and carried bouquets of pale pink and white carnations. The bridegroom's presents to the bridesmaids were gold brooches. The duties of best man were discharged by Mr. Frank White. Mr. Ernest Wilkes presided at the organ and played suitable selections including Mendelssohn's Wedding March. There was a large attendance at the reception which was held at Froghall House, and the usual toasts were honoured. The company included the Revs. T. W. Barber and R. H. Bowen. The bride and bridegroom and the wedding guests were subsequently photographed by Mr. James Parkinson, of Egypt-street. During the afternoon Mr. and Mrs. White left for a tour in North Wales, the bride's travelling dress being a blue costume with hat to match. At Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Thursday, the marriage was solemnize of Mr. John William Jackson, eldest son of Mr. Peter Jackson, Liverpool-road, Sankey Bridges, and Miss Mary Hewitt Boon, youngest daughter of Mr. Frederick Boon, Wellfield-street. The Rev. G. H. Carnson officiated. The bride who was given away by her brother, Mr. Samuel Boon, was attended by two bridesmaids—Miss Clara Jackson (sister of the bride- groom) and Miss B. Boon (cousin of the bride.) Mr. P. Jackson acted as best man.

He was going to prove to them that during the last thirty years the Consevative Government had been practically the only temperance party in the cokuntry. (" Oh.") The next question was "Has the policy advocated by Mr. Chamberlain got the support of the whole Unionist party?" Mr. FEARNELEY : NO, it has not. A member of the audience interrupted with a re- mark about the Independent Labour party. Mr. FEARIsIELEY : The Independent Labour party is more split up than any party in the United Kingdom. Tell them I said so. _

A VOICE : Will our friend say that the cotton trade doesn't turn out more weight now than ever it did in its existence?

Mr. FEA.ENELEY No, he won't state that, because what you say is true. We have more spindles at work and quicker machinery; but how does that benefit you if you are being thrown out of work because of the facility of machinery? Numerous other questions were asked, one being "Did our Borough member vote against altering the law with reference to the Taff Vale decision? Mr. FEARNELEY ; I cannot tell you that. ("Oh.") A VOICE: How many members of the Conservative Labour party are trade unionists? Mr. FEARNELEY : I cannot tell you exactly, but roughly speaking I should say there are something like

3,70. " Another QUF:STIONER : Is a Conservative Labour candidate going to be put forward for us to support at Warrington.? Mr. FE ARNELE Y : I cannot tell you that. lam only a member of the National Conservative Labour party, and your local Conservative Labour party will deal with that matter. If they think proper they will nominate one, but I see no necessity for nominating one when you are going to have three good eladidates to choose from—good of a sort, two of them. Questioned in regard to Mr. Shackleton's Bill, Mr. Fearneley said: "The Conservative Labour party are bringing as much pressure to bear upon the present Government as they possibly can to bring forward Mr. Shackleton's Bill or its equivalent and to see that it is carried through definitely and clearly and without fail during the next session." He quoted a resolution to this effect which will be submitted at a conference of the Conservative Labour party. "What is there to stop the Chinese coming to Eng- land ?" was another query. Mr. FEARNELEY : Nothing. You won't pass a Bill to keep out of the country undesirable foreigners who are prepared to come and work at sweat( d labour to undersell you. A question submitted in writing was: "Who pays you for giving these answers ?" Mr. FEARNELE Y said he received nothing but his rail- way fare from Manchester which was paid by the local Conservative Labour party. The meeting which had lasted two hours and a half 4 concluded shortly afterwards.

===== 0002601_19040903_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
SOCIAL SERMONETTES FOR WORKING MEN.

•BY JOHN TAYLOR. Author of Search Lights oa Laud, Labour an.d Social Problems."

FLOGGING A DEAD HORSE. • ‘• 11-. Chamberlain opened his autumn campaign by addressing a meeting in the Duke of Portland's Riding School at Welbeck. lie addressed himself manfully to the task of bribing the agricultural classes into support of his proposals."—" Daily News."

The Protectionist wire-pullers from Birmingham know how to advertise themselves and their wares` They boast that they have any quantity of money behind them, and they spend it freely in organising big meet- ings And employing a big army of speakers,and scattering big heaps of leaflets, and picture cards, and pamphlets broadcast, to capture the men who never think for them- selves. They have undoubtedly made a splash or two in the country by their sensational and extravagant pranks, but the game can hardly be worth the candle. I.—THE MEETING AT WELBECK WAS A GOOD

===== 0002601_19040903_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
EXAMPLE OF THEIR METHODS.

It was held in a b room. It attracted a big crowd. It was patronized by big aristocrats. It had a big boom from the newspapers. And it was a big failure. The Duke of Portland's Riding School is the most unlikely place in the •world for any speaker to make himself heard. It is one hundred and twenty yards long. It is thirty-five yards wide. It is fifty-one feet high. It's glass roof is supported by fifty columns, that seriously interfere with its acoustic properties. The building is so large that it requires eight thousand jets of gas to illuminate it, and no man could make a speech that could be heard all over the place, unless he had leather lungs, and a voice like a fog-horn. Do you ask me what attracted the crowd? Well, the Dukeries are always popular. Men cheerfully pay a shilling each to see that wonderful riding school, and the beauties of Welbeck when Joseph is not there. On the day the meeting was held they got in for nothing, and had the pleasure of feasting their eyes on such big aristocrats as the Dukes of Portland, and Rutland, and Sutherland, and earls, and nobles, and titled lords and ladies by the score.

There were cheap trips from Birmingham and all the country round, bringing cheap trippers at ridiculously low fares, and hundreds of these strangers never paid a penny for their day's outing. Those who knew their way about could get free tickets for the railway journey, and free drives through the Dukeries, and free lunches, and free drinks into the bargain. These are some of the methods by which an audience of twelve thousand people was attracted to Joseph's feet that day, and any man can get a big audience if he will spend money enough to tempt the crowd. _

But the people did not go there to hear Joseph talk- ing about Protection and Retaliation. They went to see the beauties of the place, and to enjoy themselves, and have a high old spree at some other fellow's expense. lI.—JOSEPH'S SPEECH BEAT THE RECORD FOR RECKLESS ASSERTION AND COOL ASSUNIPTION.

He has said some wild and random things in his time, but his speech at Welbeck fairly takes the cake. He says that" the effect of Free Trade has been disastrous to labourers in this country." Now, I ask any man who knows the history of the last seventy or eighty years whether that statement is the sober truth or an impu- dent lie? Since 1850 labourers' wages have risen more than fifty per cent., and the chief 'necessaries of life, such as bread, tea, sugar, boots, and clothing, are fifty per cent. cheaper than they were half a century ago. So that Free Trade has raised the labourer's wages, and given him a great deal more for his monoy. If this is a "disaster," then I do not know the meaning of words. He tells us that if we tax the food and produce that come into this country "the foreigner will pay the tax." Let us examine this impudent statement for a moment. The Danes are sending us butter, bacon, eggs, and agricultural produce to the value of £10,000,000 worth every year. A five per cent. duty would yield half a million annually. Suppose we compelled the Danish trader to pay this duty. He would simply add five per cent. to the price of his produce, and be just as well off as he was before.

The trader who sold the goods would have to. charge a higher price to every purchaser, so the import duty would be passed on from the Dane to the consumer.

If this is not true why did Joseph exclude maize from the list of articles he proposes to tax? If the foreigner is so eager and willing to pay our taxes let him pay the lot—maize and all. The truth is we cannot tax the foreigner without having the tax taken out of us by the increased prices of his wares. Sooner or later it is the unlucky consumer who has to pay the piper. We are asked to believe that the landlord, and the farmer, and the agricultural labourers have interests in common, and that whatever benefits one will benefit them all. His chairman, the Duke of Portland, is the owner of 15'3,163 acres of land: the Duke of RutlAnd has 69,000 acres; the Duke of Sutherland owns 1,358,425 acres. These men are enjoying princely incomes, and yet they are asking agricultural labourers. earning twelve or thirteen shillings a week, to vote for a tax on food. And they are telling us that whatever helps one of them will help the other also. I can imagine a hungry cat telling such yarns as these to a bird in a tree, or a mouse in a trap. Protection would never be felt a burden by the great landowners. Tt would increase their incomes by the rise of rents, as it did a century ago while the labourer would get no. higher wages, and taxation woald lessen the spending power of his money. But it is impossible to follow Joseph through his as- tounding fallacies, and his idiotic arguments. and his contradictory assertions. Where is the lunatic outside Bedlam who believes that you can put a duty of five per canton our food without making that food dearer? Who believes that if you have to pay more for all you eat and:drink and wear the cost of living will not be increased? Does any working man believe that .Pro- tection will mean more employment for hum? How can he persuade farmers that a tax on corn will profit them, when he tells the labourers that it will produce no rise in the price of bread? If he is going to give good things to everybody, and rob nobody, where does he expect to find the means to fulfil his own promises ? I can only stand aghast at his reckless statements and wonder what crazy thing he will say next. lII.—JOSEPH IS FLOGGING A DEAD HORSE. Protection in this country died sixty years ago, and some of us devoutly prayed that it might never have a resurrection. Nearly twenty years ago an effort was made by the late Mr. Ecroyd, 3.1. P., and others to draw the poor old gee-gee from its grave.and galvanise it into life. They did not dare to call it Protection, so they nicknarried it" Fair Trade," and they Spent a pile of money hi trying to win converts.

In Lancashire there was an expensive crusade carried on. and meetings were held in every town, but the cause did not prosper. It was examined and discussed on its merits by our ablest manufacturers and merchants, and then it was unanimously rejected as a foolish and dangerous experiment. Ever since Joseph took up thequestion of Protection he must have known that he was championing a 'dying cause, and that he was trying to bribe =en into his way of thinking, instead of appealing to the intellect, and judgment and conscience. What is his bribe to the workingman? He wants a duty of two shillings per quarter on wheat, barley, oats, and rye. He wants a duty of live per cent. on beef, mutton, butter, cheese, preserved milk, poultry, eggs, vegetables and fruit. And he offers to. take 4.d. per lb. off the tea duty and a farthing per lb. off the sugar duty. ,

I wonder if he thinks the working men of this coun- try are such asies and fools as to accept such a paltry bribe as this? If he knows thorn as I know them, he is aware that his cause is hopeless. They tell me that Joseph has been in office for more than twenty years,and his wages all the time were about £lOO a week. When he went into office this last time the duty on tea was onlyl4d. per lb. To-day it is Bd., and it was raised to help to ray for his reckless and criminal war in South Africa. The war is over. but the tax remains, and he asks us to tax all our other food that we may reduce that. Not if we know it. We know a trick worth two of that. Our game is to turn out the reckless and incompetent spend- thrifts who are squandering our money, and legislating without mandate, and replace them by men who will govern us economically, and efficiently. We want to reduce our expenditure to what it was when the Liberals were in office last time, and we can do it easily. If we reduce our taxation and live within our means we can redline the taxes on tea and sugar and tobacco without putting now taxes on all other kinds of food.

We intend to tax all land values, and make our aristo- cratic landowners pay their fair share of the costs of municipal and national expenditure. We mean to make the acquirement and purchase of land for allotments and

CHINESE LABOUR.

TORIES ARGUE IN FAVOUR OF IT. Another Op-en-Air Meeting.

Chinese labour was the principal subject under discussion at an open-air .meeting held on the site of the old bridewell in Irlam-street on Thursdayevening under the auspices of the Conservative Labour party. Coun- cillor Wilson again presided, and was supported by the deputation from Manchester, Mr. W. L. Williams and others. The attendance at the outset was small, but it gradually increased to about five hundred. The CHAIRMAN, in the course of his address, said that at the last Liberal meeting Mr. Howes said Aldermani Sutton's reply to the Liberal Association's request for I a debate meant that the Conservative Association repudiated the people over the way (the Conservative Labour party) and if the Conservative Association would have nothing to do with them, neither would the Liberals. He (Mr. Wilson) took it uponhimself to write to Alder- man Sutton eaclosing a report of Mr. Howes' speech and asking for his views upon the statement mentioned In his reply Alderman Sutton stated: '"My reply to the letter treceived from the hon. secretary of the Liberal Association was not intended as a repudiation byl the Conservative Association of the Conservative Labour I party. The letter I received from the hon. secretary of the Liberal Association and my reply to that letter dealt exclusively with the question affecting the associations I alone, and I fail to see any justification for the observa- tions of Mr.Howes.So far from our being desirous of repu- diating the Conservative Labour party,the Conservative Association takeSa sympathetic interest in the work ini which they are engaged, and wishes them every subeess in their undertaking to demonstrate to the working. classes which of the two political parties has proved the best friend of the working classes." Councillor WiLsoN then proceeded to reply to sosne statements made by Mr. Howes. Mr. FE ARNELEY alluded to Mr. Howes' advice to the audience at the last Liberal open-air meeting to "vote sober." He could not understand what Mr. Howes meant by insulting first the Liberal workingmen and then the whole of the workingmen of Warrington by assum- ing for a moment that they went to exercise their vote anything else but sober. Referring to the Chinese labour question, Mr. Fearneley said that when it first came to the front he was as much opposed to the intro- duction of the labour as any man in the kingdom. He was opposed to it in his ignorance. Proceeding to state the case as he now understood it he said that before the war the Kaffirs did the work of getting the minerals from the mines. The white men's work was distinctly different from that of the Kaffirs,for it was of the skilled kind such as surveying, engineering &c. It was con- sidered a loss of caste for a white man to work in the mines. When the war was over they could not get a sufficient number of Kaffirs to start the mines, because the Kaffirs had been engaged in a servant capacity to the British troops at the wages of 4s. 6d. per day. After the campaign they went home. Mr. Fearneley instanced how Mr. Creswell, one of the mine owners, offered to employ the men of a disbanded corps at the rate of 10s. per day. Some of the men had only worked in the mines two days when they became sick of the job be- cause as they were doing Kaffirs' work, none of the white men in Johannesburg would mix with them. The men therefore went back to their colonel and said they could not go on with the work. The Kaffirs could not be got to do it unless they iwere made; the white men at the top of the mines could not work without the Kaffirs, and the, result was the proposal to introduce the Chinese. The great majority of ministers of religion of all denominations in the mining districts gave their sanction and support to the introduction of Ceinese labour because they could see nothing for it but that,or the starvation of the white men who were walk- ing about unemployed. (" Rats.") The great majority of the white men who were doing nothing were also in favour of the labour. The Government did not intro- duce the Chinese to cheapen the white men's labour, for it was provided in the ordinance that so long as the Chinese labourer remained in the colony he should be employed only on unskilled labour in the getting lof minerals. He had simply to do what the Kaffiir had been doing previously. Mr. Fearneley contended that the effect of the introduction of Chinese had been to provide more work for the white men,and he said that at the top of one mine 136 white men were now employed as compared with 34 the month before the arrival of the Chinese. 4Tne speaker went into a comparison of the terms of the Transvaal indenture with the one in force in British Guiana which Mr. Howes had admitted was sanctioned by the Liberal party. The ordinance for British Guiana provided that the indentured labourer should work ten hours per day in buildings or sixteen hours by arrangement with his employer, and receive the living wage of a penny per hour. out of which he had to keep himself. The pay of the Chinaman in South Africa was is. per day of ten hours, and he was provided with food and housing free of cost. The British Guiana indenture provided that a coolie could be transferred, without his consent, from one employer to another,but a labourer in South Africa could not be transferred until he gave his consent in writing. The coolie in British Guiana was limited to a compound of five acres, but the Chinese labourer in South Africa had a village about three miles in extent. _ _ _

In conclusion, Mr. FEARNELEY asked his hearers to watch how the people of the Transvaal would exercise their votes when the new Transvaal Government came into existence next year. He was very much afraid that on the Chinese labour question their uiberal friends would be " down the nick" again. (Cries of "Never.") He thought the people of the Transvaal when they had the matter in their own hands would continue to pro- vide work for the white skilled man by first finding workmen who would get the rough minerals from the mines. (Applause.) Mr. MACARTNEY, the next speaker, met with considerable interruption. When he asked the .crowd how they proposed to improve their conditions of life, there were cries of "Change the Government." The mention of Mr. Chamberlain's name evoked some groaning. The speaker argued at somelength in favour of an alteration in the fiscal policy. Mr. Howes.he stated, dare not take up the challenge to indicate what the Liberal programme was. Mr. ORRELL, another member of the depulation, said they challenged the Liberal pai ty to point to any measure the which party had placed on the statute book daring the last forty years for the benefit of the work- ing classes. If Mr. Howes and the Liberal parti7 in Warrington were anxious for a public debate, the Con- servative Labour party would put forward workingmen to meet Mr. Howes or any other Liberal representatives. Mr. Howes said our foreign trade was not declining. In Lancashire alone there were 28,000 fewer cotton operatives than there were ten years ago and the decrease for Lancashire and Yorkshire was 48,000. Mr. Orrell met with interruptions, and there were occasional amusing retorts from the crowd. Trying to show that the Liberal party was divided he asked "On the clean slate where does home rule come ?" and immediately came the reply" on the back." Mr. Orrell asked his hearcrs to read for themselves and not to take any notice of what was said there, but this was too much for the audience who rtSared with laughter, and the speaker brought his remarks to a speedy close. Mr. FEARNELEY then'replied to a large number of questions. The first query was "Are the workingmen in the Transvaal in favour of the introduction of Chinese labour ?"

_ . Mr. FEARNELEY replied that the great majority of them were. There was a minority against it, but that minority had n3ver splken sine Lnrd Milner asked for an alternative propocal in case the Chinese were not imported. Another member of the audience asked " Are the Co- nservative Labour party prepared to hold a meeting on the recent Licensing Bill ?" Mr. FEARNELEY said that when nest he came to War-

STOCKTON HEATH.

THE EXTENSION OF THE TRAAIWAYs.—Both sets of tram lines have now been laid as far as Victoria-square, though the laying of the setts of the permanent way has not yet been completed. This no doubt will be finished by the early part of next week. The laying of the lines on the Swing Bridge will be the next step. So far as can be estimated, and from,what we can learn it will be quite two months vet before the trams will be running i nto the Square at Stockton Heath.

MEETING OF' THE PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE. ANOTHER INSPECTOR REQUIRED.

The monthly meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee was held in the National Schools on Thursday evening, Mr. G. Pilling presiding. There were also present :—Messrs. Whitley, Fairhurst, Mclntyre, Pearson, Mountield, Clarke, H. J. Westbrook, Bouchier, Welsby, Hallows, Swinton (surveyor), Farrington (inspector of nuisances), and G. F. Ashton (clerk.)

STREET IMPROVEMENTS.

It was decided to instruct Mr. Garnett to prepare plans and specifications for the laying out of Cawdor- street and Bedford-street.

SANITARY MATTERS.

ANOTHER IssPgcrort REQUIRED.

The Clerk read a letter from Sir. T. FL Spinks, addressed to Mr. J. H. Clarke, calling attention to the unsatisfactory condition of an ashpit at the rear of Bed- ford-street, which he described as a most serious nuis- ance and a menace to health. Nfr. Spinks added that he had recently nearly lost his child through poisoning caused by smells arising from the ashpit. The nuisance arose from Improper cleansing and lack of disinfectants. Mr. Swinton said he had never seen anything very wrong with the ashpit. Mr. Clarke said it was their duty to see that the ash- pit was properly cleansed and disinfected, and the com- mittee gave instructions to the inspector to see that this was done.

It was reported that at 'a meeting of the newly-formed Sanitary Committee on August 35th, Mr. Clarke was elected chairman. It was decided that in future galvan- ised pails and ashbins only should be provided. Having regard to the greatly increased and ever increasing number of residents,the committee considered the time had arrived when more frequent and efficient sanitary inspection should be given to the district and resolved that tke District Council be requested to appoint an additional sanitary inspector. Mr. Clarke proposed the confirmation of the minutes The meeting, he said, was well attended and sanitary matters were discussed. It was the unanimous feeling of the committee that the time had arrived when better attention should be given to the sanitary inspec- tion of the whole of the district. They all recognized that Mr. Farrington did his best, though he had so large a district to cover that he was unable to devote to Stokton Heath the time and attention to which it was entitled. Mr. Farrington could not always be at Stockton Heath and therefore it was, the committee thought, necessary that an assistant inspector should be appointed, and they proposed to send the recommenda- tionto the District Council in the strong hope that whet they desired would be done.

Mr. Pearson seconded. Mr. Westbrook supported the motion. He thought an improvement would be effected if it could be arranged that Mr. Farrington could live in Stockton Heath. He, however, thought they should have an assistant inspector appointed to devote the whole of his time to the inspection of the Stockton Heath, dis- trict Mr. Westbrook addni that he noticed that special inspectors had been appointed for the Frodsham and Budworth districts.

Mr. Pilling : That is hardly correct. Mr. Farrington has only been relieved to a certain extent of his duties in those districts. Mr. Westbrook; I only know that salariec are paid in both those districts to inspectors of nuisances. The minutes were approved.

--•,.- GRAPPENHALL.

THE CRICKET ClA:B.—Saturday's fixture was with Winnington 2nd at Winnington and proved another downfall—the third for Grappenhall Ist. The losers only scored 47. Banks being responsible for 15, Williams 14 and Jenkins 9, not out. The response for the home players amounted to 116, Dale playing a grand not out innings of 4S, while Dr. Cowper followed with 3. The latter was very successful with the ball, having six wickets to his credit—The 2nd eleven had no match,the one with Mere having been cancelled. THE GYAINASIUM.—This institution is be opened on the second Tuesday in October and promises to have another successful season.

FAILURES IN ENGLAND AND WALES.—According to I " Kernp's Mercantile Gazette," the total number of failures recorded in England and Wales during the week ending Friday, Aug. 36th, was 179, viz.:—New Bankruptcy Proceedings published in the London Gazette 83; Deeds of Arrangement registered. 96. The respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were Bankruptcies, 75; Deeds of Arrangement, 09—Total 144; being a net increase in Bankruptcies of 186, and a net increase in Deeds of Arrangement in 1904, to date I of 440.

TOWN COUNCIL. Business of the- -Next Meeting.

The monthly meeting of the Town Council will be held on Tuesday evening, when the following among other business will be transacted THE GEORGE HOTEL.-SPECIAL MOTION EY COUNCILLOR MAGINN.

The Town Clerk has reported that Messrs. Arthur Browne and Co., had given him notice that Mr. Thomas Halton (the tenant of the George Hotel) did not intend to proceed further with the action he had commenced against the Corporation for alleged damage caused by the falling of a chimney. The following letter from Messrs. Peter Walker and Son Limited was read at a meeting of the Finance Committee :

The Brewery, Warrington, 10th August, 1904.

Dear Sir,

Tn reference to our tender for the George Hotel Licence, and the proceedings of the Town Council on Tuesday evening, we have decided under the circum- stances to place the withdrawal of our tender in the hands of the Council.

Yours faithfully, Peter Walker and Son (Warrington and Barton), Ltd. J. Reid Walker, Managing Director.

J. Lyon Whittle, Esq., Town Clerk.

Resolved that the consideration of the matter be de- ferred until definite information can be obtained as to how the New Licensing Bill will affect the Licence. Councillor 31aginn will move that the offer of £2,550 made by Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons, Limited, for the purchase of the licence of the -"George Hotel,"sub- ject to the conditions named in their letter of the 9th. July, 1904, be accepted.

SANKEY BROOK. Mr. F. G. Ruiclock has been taking samples of water from Sankey Brook, Orford Brook, and from the con- densing water at Longford Depot. for analysis. He reports :—" On comparing these results it will be noticed that the sample of condensing water is worse than the Sankey Brook water, still however it does not appreciably affect the composition of the water in the brook, the volume not being sufficient. Even under the conditions of taking these samples when the brook was very low, on comparing the analyses of the two samples of Sankey Brook you will notice the figures are almost identical.

Tao water taken from the small brook (Orford Brook) was in a very bad state, strongly alkaline, and contain- ing a mass of putrid organic matter." THE BOROUGH SURVEYOR ON THE PAVING OF STREETS AND PASSAGES. The followin<, report dated August 29th, and prepared by the Borough Surveyor on the question of unpaved streets referred to by the Medical. Officer of Health in his annual report for 1903, was read and ordered to be entered on the Minutes of the Paving and Sewerage Committee. "Gentlemen, PRIVATE STREETS—DR. GORNALL'S REPORT. The total number of streets and passages mentioned in Dr. Gornall's report as requirinc, paving is 343. On examination I find that. there are lepassages and streets mentioned in the report as unpaved which had been done from as far back as eight years.ln the report there are 27 passages and streets which are mentioned twice over. There are 31 streets and passages which are only built on one side. Such streets and passages it has never been the custom to pave until both sides of the street are completed, except at the request of the owners and in exceptional cases. There are eight passages mentioned in the report that cannot be found. With the extension of the Borough Boundary in 1896 a considerable number of streets and passages which were unpaved were brought into the borough. A Local Government Board inquiry was held last week, and we are now awaiting sanction to proceed with 22 streets and passages mentioned in ,the report. The following table shows the work carried out by your committee since 1875, viz. : A period of ten years-1875 to 1884 cost, £16,501 do. dn. 1885 to 1894 cost, £27,697 do. do. 1895 to 1904 cost, £61,864 Daring the past ten years no less than 13 miles of streets and passages have been paved. There are only G miles of streets and passages mentioned in the report renain to be done. I think the Committee will see from the foregoing figures, and knowing the difficulties in obtaining material to keep down the undue cost, that the department has made every endeavour to keep pace with the rapid buildiqg operations that have taken place within the borough."

THE BENEFITS OF SCIENCE.

• It is seldom that any discovery. of science is such as will benefit all classes of people. Some of the greatest discoveries of the age, while they have proved directly beneficial to thousands, have also, at least for a time, injured other thousands. Even some of the best medi- cinal preparations, however much they may do to remove one class of disease, or suit some constitutions, at the same time either deepen the hold of another disease, or leave it entirely untouched, or if they benefit one class of patients, they may injure another. Now the great need of the age is some scientifically arranged preparation which will cope effectually with the pre- valent dseaies of this country, which will be certain to do good when fairly tried, which will be equally adopted to the needs of the merchant and the workman, the professional man, and he who wins his bread by the sweat of his brow, the student, the clerk, the factory hand, the miner, and the roadside lab ourer. It should be, too, such a pre paration as contains no injurious ingredients, and which may be taken with impunity by the weakly child, or the delicate lady, as well as by the stronger constituted man. Such a discovery would deserve to be called Me Perfection of Medicinal Preparations, and would indeed be an Invaluable Boon to Suffering Humanity. Now this much needed boon has been found, has been tried, has been proved, and wherever proved, it has been found satisfactory. We refer to Gwilym' Evans' Quinine Bitters, The Vegetable Tonic, which when once tried, has been always recommended, and has proved successful when all other medicine has failed to give relief, and we may say further, that it has proved per- manently beneficial, when other preparations at best gave only temporary relief. It is strongly recommended as The Best Remedy of the Age for Indigestion in its different forms, such as Sick Headache, Pains in the Side, Giddiness, Loss of Appetite, also for Nervousness -and 'Nervous Disorders, Sleeplessness, Neuralgia, Low Spirits, and all kinds of Weatmess. It has often proved very beneficial to persons suffering from great Weakness, either after an illness, long con- finement to ill-ventilated rooms or any other cane. it strikes at the source of the Disease, removing the cause of the illness, and strengthens those parts of the system which have been weakened by it, and therefore most liable to colds and other ailments. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters being purely vegetable, it is a Natural Tonic, and suited to all ages at all seasons of the year. It is everywhere acknowledged to be a powerful aid in restoring the body to a healthy state when deranged by disease. If you suspect that your health is beginning to fail, brace up your nerves, and fortify your constitution by taking now a course of this excellent Tonic, which is sold everywhere in bottles 2s. 9d. and 4s. 6d. each, but should any difficulty be experienced in procuring it, the Proprietors will forward same for the above prices, carriage free. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

WARRINGTON TRIPPERS RAID AN OR.CHA.RD. On Monday at the Oakmere Petty Sessions, Lawrence Spencer, Dalton Bank, and John Henry O'Nield, 21, Napier-stredt, both of Warrington, were summoned for stealing growing apples at Kingsley, on Sunday, August 21st. Robert Flatman, 103, Mersey-street, Warrington, was summoned for aiding and abetting.—John Starkey, farmer, Kingsley, stated that on the date named his son informed him of two men having left one of his fields with their pockets bulky. He went into the road and saw two men get into a waggonette about a hundred yards away. Complainant despatched one of his sons on a bic3cle to inform the village constable, and the moment be left, the men emptied their pockets of a number of apples and threw them into a field •of potatoes. He valued the apples at 2s.— Peliee-constable Fry said he intercepted the waggon- ette at Frodsham. The man in charge said two men had left the conveyance, but had not returned. Wit- ness returned a little distance and met a man whom he knew to be a stranger. He admitted belonging to a party from Warrington, but in consequence of their carryings on "he leftthem. The constable informed theiman he wanted two of them for stealing apples,and thelman stated he wouldpoint out the offenders when he reached the conveyance. Witnesq again went after the waggonette and detained it until the man came along, when he pointed out Spencer and O'Nield as having been in the orchard. The defendants were in the conveyance when he first questioned Flatman, the driver.—Flatman denied that he knew anything at all about the men having been in the orchard.—The summons against Flat- man was dismissed, and O'Nield and Spencer were wch lined 2s. Gd. and Bs. 9d. costs. WARRINGTON PA.RENTS CHARGED WiTII NEGLECT.— A FILTHY HONIE.

At Warrington Police Court yesterday, before the Mayor and other magistrates, Charles Cummings, Hind street, was charged with neglecting his children.—Mr. Browne prosecuted on behalf of the N.S,P.C.C. and said the defendant would not work. The children were clean and well looked after. The wife had had to pawn her boots to provide food.lt was decided to give defendant another chance and the ease was adjourned for a month.—John and Eleanor Johnson,hushand and wife, Napier street, were summoned for neglecting their children.—lr. Browne said this was a very sad case. The society did not blame the husband but brought him before the Court in order that he might show some reason why he had allowed the children to get into such a neglected state. The wife had given way to drink an neglected to keep the home and children clean. The house was in such a filthy state that Dr. Peacock considered it was unfit for habitation. The children were verminous and poorly fed.—Dr. Peacock said some of the bedroom furniture could not have been touched for months as it was covered with a kind of fungous growth. A neighbour stated that the woman waslcontinually pawning clothes to obtain drink. Witness had seen her chase the children and throw water on them.—lnspector Hackett's evidence revealed a shocking state of affairs, the house and sihildren being filthy. The woman had had repeated chances of amendment. The husband was afraid of his wife, and she had threatened him with a poker.— Mrs. Johnson pleaded hard for another chance, and the magistrates gave her the option of going to a home for six months! or undertaking a month's imprisonment in the second division. Defendant chose ti go to gaol for the shorter period. The case against the husband was dismissed.

Warrington Borough Police Court.

Minor Cases.

MONDAY—Before the Mayor (in the chair), Alderman Smethurst, Councillor Wilkinson, Messrs. Birchall,

• Pilling. Wright, and W. Roberts. POLICEM AN'S STRUGGLE WITEI PRISONERS.—John McDonough and Thomas Moran, of no settled abode, were charged with having been drunk mid disorderly in Horsemarket-street at 4.5 on Saturday afternoon :and with assaulting P.C. Burton.—The constable said prisoners accosted severat people and made use of filthy languace. He cautioned them, but they would not desist, and McDonough seized him by the le", butted him and tried to throw him to the ground! Moran then thumped him on the head and kicked him on the right knee. Witness closed with prisoners and a violent struggle ensued. Thomas Dooney, of Phillip-street. assisted him until P.C. Edwards •came upon the scene and prisoners were taketi te the Police Station.—The Chief Constable said he 'saw the officers taking the men to the Police Station. Moran was acting like a madman, and prisoners, within the hearing of children and ladies, were using the vilest and foulest language it was possible for human tongue to debase itself to. Prisoners belonged to a gang which went about pretending to hawk and intimidating people 'who would not give them money.—Prisoners were sent to gaol for a month with hard labour, and the magis • trates expressed their thanks to Dooney for his assist- ance.—William Rigby, another member of the gang,was fined 10s, and costs for having been drunk and disorderly. PENKETH.

BAND SUCCESSES.— The members of the Penketh Tannery Prize Band, were well repaid for their visit to Ellesmere Port on Saturday last on the occasion of the Ellesmere Port, Whitley and District Horticultural Society's Show and Brass Band Contest. The band have been practising assiduously,and their efforts were crowned with success, both the first prizes being. captured by the Penketh players. The test piece was "Songs of Ireland," arranged by H. Round, and there were eight entries. The judge was J. 0. Shepherd, esq. (Mus. Director Royal Court Theatre, Liverpool) and a noted judge at all principal contests including Belle 'true, and Crystal Palace London. The awards in the test piece were : Ist. Penketh Tannery; 2nd, Atherton Temper- ance; 3rd, Tranmere Gleam; 4th, Ellesmere Port Public. In the Quick Step Competition, Penketh Tannery were awarded Ist prize and Trawl:ere Gleam 2nd. The news of the victory was hailed with delight in the village, and the band met with a great reception on their return home.

GIPSY SMITH IN PRETORIA.

Writing of his missionary work in Pretoria, Gipsy Smith says that Ow tent used for the meetings there was the one used in Johannesburg with 2,000 seats. and although Pretoria has only a Population of 20,000 people, every seat was needed every night, "I think Pretoria was really the hardest bit of work we have had so far in South Africa," he says. "There was such opposition from the drink sellers and theatre managers ;I their business suffered much. A 'Atter is before me from one who says that the manager of one bar told him Gipsy Smith had not been in Pretoria twenty-four hours before his sales went down £4O. The mission emptied these places while it lasted at any rate. "The work in Pretoria was deep and searching. All sorts came to the meetings, Dutch and British ; the former were the first to yield. A Boer general and a grandson of the late President Kruger were amongst the inquirers. A whole football team, a fine set of fellows, who always played their games on Sundays,was converted, and many remarkable cases of well-known people about town. In all, 900 passed through the hands of the workers, and 500 signed the pledge."

The Examiner, Saturday, September 3, 1904.

(Copyright.)

for Captain Gerald Thornton was certainly a delightful six feet of manhood. He had money, heaps of it; he was handsome, he was dark; and his eyes were as blue as the skies, and tney tutu a way of looking at one from under his dark lashes which was entirely captivating. His figure was slim, long-legged, and athletic; his voice, directly he spoke, struck one by its rich, deep tone; and there was something delightfully refreshing about his hearty, ringing laugh; and as to his dancing, it was quite as it should be. You might search every London ballroom and you would assuredly not find another like him. And with all these admirable qualities what else need any man wish for? Surely nothing; but Nature, in mothering him, had been in one of her very generous and very rare moods, for besides these outward and visible signs there were inward and spiritual graces to match. Captain Gerald Thornton was a good fellow all through; so his brother officers of the Guards, one and all, voted him, as manly and upright a man as one woule meet in many a day's march—good-tempered ano good-natured enough to do anyone a good turn if need be. And there was always plenty of life and go about him. He was ready to take a hand in most things, from tooling a four-in-hand with admirable skill Ranelagh or Burlingham, or breaking a horse with a deuce of a temper, down to binding; up the damaged head of the sergeant's youngest hope on occasions when it elected to fall down the ladder from the stable loft.

Looked at all round; perhaps, it was not a sur- prising thing that Gerald Thornton, not knowing her small eccentricities, should fall in love with Augusta Temple, though, of course, that such a very elevated being as she should fall in love with him was another pair of shoes altogether. But fall in love she did, however difficult it may be to conceive that she could condescend to anything so trivial.

• Through the season before they had met pretty frequently at garden parties, dances, balls and receptions; and when the time came when Fashion decrees that the world shall flock to Scotland for the shooting, whether it feels any inclination to go there or not, they had somehow generally turned up on a visit to the same country houses. On the night of Mrs. Heaton's ball the question that had long been agitating the breast of Captain Thornton trembled on his lips and then fell—the murder was out. They were sitting in one of those delightful, convenient, and secluded nooks which a thoughtful and considerate hostess will always provide, and instead of Augusta turning away with scorn in her eyes and anger on her lips, as she should certainly have done, she simply blushed, smiled, said "Yes," and put her arms round his neck, without being asked to do so, and thus the matter was settled between them. _

And then there were good times to follow, though the divided skirts were laid by for the time being, and the distractions of the Knickerbocker Club she must forego. Gerald must not be "rushed "—he must come to things gently; for, although a very charming personage, he was, after all, only a mere man, with narrow and restricted views of things, and the usual selfish, masculine notions on the subjection of women, and all that kind of thing. Indeed, on one occasion he had even been heard to liken the case of the woman of intellect to a certain Scotchman, who transplanted his native thistle into a colony where the soil was rich and bountiful. Nothing but thistle grows there now, and the Scotchman has left. Like the thistle, the big notions which some im- prudent person has been putting into the feminine mind are spreading fast, and it is impossible to say where they will spread to. Well, Gerald must be led gently, and, after a proper course of training, he would come to view the march of intellect in its proper and advanced light. The first time that Captain Thornton's feelings experienced a slight shock was on a.certain morn- ing when he made his appea'rance at the solemn house in Grosvenor-square just as he usually did. Augusta, f =ling sweetly and looking prettier and fresher than ever, greeted him.

"Oh, no, Gerald dear, not this morning," in answer to the question as to whether she was ready to come for a ride. "I can't possibly come with you now,' so you must run away and play by your- self, like a dear. 14 11.30 our Knickerbocker Club holds its most important meeting of the season, and I must certainly be there; of course, all members who are of any importance will not fail to attend, and then I have just got the most perfectly chic new suit you can imagine; they are almost knickerbockers, and when I've got a little more used to them, then next time I shall have proper, real, live ones, just like yours." It was as well to put a bold face on it at once. Captain Thornton stood aghast, his eyes opening wider with astonishment.

"My dear Augusta, you don't mean to tell me that you, you are a member of that odious Knicker- uocker Club, and that all this time you have kept it a secret from me, and that you actually have the inrpudence to contemplate wearing such things, you will stoop to anything so vulgar, so contemptible? Where's your modesty and your womanly refine- ment? For Heaven's sake, be content to remain the pretty woman you are, and not turn yourself into a hateful thing of no sex at all." Gerald was very angry indeed. Augusta had not expected so much temper from him, as he paced up and down the room with an angrier light in his blue eyes than had ever been there before. But., of course, it was all rubbish to talk iire that '• it was only his poor manlike densenet sof ideas. He would very soon come round. It was certainly the bitterest pill Gerald Thornton had ever had to swallow in his whole life, when he watched from a safe distance the meet of the famous Knickerbocker Club, and before his own eyes his beautiful, charming sweetheart (where, ( h where, were her petticoats now?) start oft with a, pack of nondescript, bespeckled, red-nosed, no- figured females, helter-skelter. What mattered it to these superior beings that the small youngsters in the street yelled at them, that the men sneered, the girls giggled, and the matrons turned up their eyes in righteous cotisternation ! • The ice was broken, and Augusta rejoiced exceed- ingly, and tiffs more or less were the order of the day. However, she was once more launched on her work of Progression. Every day, and all day, she was busy; there is really not much time to waste over love; life is not long enough for any- thing so old-fashioned nowadays. When Captain Thornton asked her mildly when it would please her ladyship to go for a drive with him, Augusta would take out her watch with a solemn and impres- sive air and announce the fact that she was due at• a meeting in Whitechapel at two, and at a lecture to reduce the population of China at four. Poor Gerald ! It is something to have for one's sweetheart such a beautiful, lofty and intellectual being, and it is necessary to do one's best to live up to it; it all, no doubt, inspired due awe and reverence in his manly breast; but perhaps often in his weaker moments he would wish that his sweetheart were a little more like other people's. But then one day a dreadful thing happened. Augusta had gone to the meet of the Knicker- bockers, and Captain Thornton meanwhile was lunching with Mr. and Mrs. Temple. There was a loud ring at the bell, another, and yet another, before the pompous footman had time to arrive at the door in a sufficiently dignified manner. And then Augusta was carried in and laid on one of the soft couches in the drawing-room—she was very still and very white, and her eyes were closed. But there was nothing very much the matter; she had only had a bicycle spill; her precious machine was doubled and twisted up into an unrecognisable shape, but she had escaped with nothing more than a bad fright and a good shaking, which kept her in bed for a week.

"Darling, are you better ? " asked Captain Thornton, as he came into the pretty boudoir where she was lying for the first. time since her recovery. He took both her hands and looked into her pretty eyes with "all the love-light," as the lady novelist would say, shining from his blue ones; it was enough to see her at first, and he did not sal much—he only looked, and Augusta did not feet strong. "Ah, my darling, how thankful I am to have yolk well again. Now you will never risk your precious self any more on that wretched machine. I have begged you to give it up so often, sweetheart. You will now, for my sake, won't you? Promise me." Augusta gave a mocking little laugh, which %hewed her very white and even teeth, and shrugged her shapely shoulders. "My dear Jerry, how absurd you are; just because I have been unfortu- nate enough to have one spill, you get into a fright and expect me to give up the one thing I like best on earth; why, I couldn't live without my bike!" "But, Augusta, you have managed to live a good many years without it, and there are other things waiting for you; and don't you love me enough to give up just this one thing, for my sake? You know I hate the thought of it; it isn't a very serious thing I ask of you, my sweetheart." He looked very pleading, and very handsome; but Augusta did not heed him, for Augusta had a temper which was fast beginning to show itself ; her lips began to draw themselves into a tight line, as was their wont. when things did not go iust as

eh, wilik;cl that they should, and there was an angry light in her pretty eyes. "You are unkind and disagreeable, and very selfish, Gerald; but then men always are selfish, from beginning to end, so what can one expect frczu em.? Just because it doesn't please yet= narrow, squpid, mesrmline ideas, you expect to make me give up the thing I get the greatest pleasure_ from. The fact is, you are so mightily afraid of women asserting their independence because you think they will climb to heights that you could never attain, and then they will laugh at you. I tell you plainly I will never give way to you or to anybody else. No; not if you were to go down on your knees to me, and beg of me to until the crack of doom." Gerald had certainly no intention of going down on his hands and knees just then; they were both very angry, and silly, too. "Very well, Augusta, since in such a very small matter as this you will not respect my wishes, it plainly shews you do not love me, and in graver affairs we can never agree. It is fortunate that we have found out our mistake before it is too late. Good-bye." Without even a look behind he walked out of the room, and Augusta heard the front door shut violently a moment after. And thus caine the parting of the ways for two very foolish and very stupid young people.

Augusta lived on in the stately house in. Grosvenor-square, growing more intense and pre- ternaturally serious day by day. Surrounded by a protective atmosphere of learning, she continued to address meetings for flower girls in the Mile End- road, and whenever the March of Intellect was on the cards Augusta was sure to be there. Augusta's learning was prodigious. She went on lapping up knowledge and wisdom with her friends and bright particular stars of the Knickerbocker Club—certain ladies with short hair and serviceable, square-toed understandings, which produce an effect like a cold-water douche down the masculine back.

When Augusta elected to take up the study of a new science or a certain language, Sanscrit or Hindustani, or anything of that kind, she would simply canter in, have a look round, and in six weeks' time had mastered it to the bottom, and was prepared to argue down the most learned professor on the subject in the three kingdoms. It was certainly manifest that whatever Augusta did not know by this time was not worth any lady's while to know. Only in her eyes sometimes a close observer would discover a subtle dissatisfac- tion as if there were something or other wantinT somewhere.

_ The years had gone by and where was Augusta now? She had tired of her striving after know- ledge. The meetings in the Mile End-road and the gatherings for the Total. Suppression of Man had yielded her nothing—she was no further on than when she first began to climb the 'Ladder of Life, and the subtle dissatisfaction had been deepening in her eyes. Well, Augusta had become an older, a wiser, and a sadder woman; her parents were dead, she had given up her home, and even her bicycle. She had no ties now, and perhaps sometimes she would think a little wistfully of some of those girls she had so despised in her own girlhood; they were happy, with homes and hu4anls, and children to look after,

At last she Ilad begun to open her eyes and see her life as others saw it. Of what good. were all those old, topsy-turvy notions of hers, and where did they lead to? The world had managed to get along very well indeed for a good many centuries without her or her Knickerbocker Club either; and it would, no doubt, continue to do so—and so Augusta gave it all up to become a hospital nurse. In St. George's Hospital there was n)t a nurse so popular among the staff nor so much loved among the patients. Nurse Etta, as she chose to be called, had such a sweet, gentle voice, such a tender, sympathetic look in her eyes, and such a strong, yet gentle, touch. It was cold, cheerless weather, and there were many patients occupying the regular lines of snowy-white little beds. On this particular day the bitter east wind was blowing fiercely, and the roads were greasy and slippery with black mud after the recent snow.

At three o'clock, when Nurse Etta was just settling down to her cup of afternoon tea, after her easy morning's work, a fresh patient was carried in. He was a tall, broad-shouldered man. There had been a shocking bicycling accident in Piccadilly, and when they picked Captain Gerald Thornton up they picked him up as dead—a brougham had gone over his body, and his legs were evidently broken. The policemen shook their heads at the crowd which pressed round them as they placed him, with all, the tenderness our English bobbies know how to exercise, on the stretcher. Life was not extinct, but it was s terrible accident, and one which would lead to very grave complications indeed, the doctors said. As Nurse Etta leant over bed No. 27, and ministered unto her new patient's wants, there was an unsteadiness in her hand and a trembling all over her, which one of the doctors, as he turned away, wondered at exceedingly in the usual calm, impassive nurse. Gerald Thornton was very, very ill, there was no doubt about it, and whether he would ever partially retover remained to be seen.

Perhaps in that hospital they had never before had such an excellent nurse as Augusta. She was at her post always, and an'i,ipating every wish of the medical men.

"Will he pull through ? " Augusta asked one of the doctors, while the tears which she strove se hard to keep back rose to her eyes, and her pretty lips trembled. "Yes, I think he will pull along all right now," the kindly doctor answered; "but it will be entirely thanks to your good nursirm " And as he turned away to his other patients he wondered very much what connection there could be between the pretty, gentle nurse and this broad- shouldered, handsome man. The days went by, and Nurse Etta kept her vigil, nursing her patient with all the love and tender- ness that had been lying dead in her heart fo twelve long years. In spite of the rest which the rules of the hospital forced her to take, she was nearly worn out, for when she was not on duty she was just fretting her heart out to get back to the bedside of her patient, who lay all unconscious of the sweet, sorrowful eyes of the woman who watched over him. And then a day came when the fever had burnt (*self out of Gerald's tired body, when all the delirium subsided, and instead a peaceful, quiet ,leep took its place. The ever-watchful presence was by his bedside, to give him his medicine when he should wake. The eyes opened and looked 'up and met a pair cf familiar grey ones—ones that through every changeful scene of the long years of his Indian service had been with him day and night. The feeble hands stretched out. _

"Jerry, do you know me?" And then, regard- less of the other inmates of the ward, her head was down on the pillow beside him. They did not talk very much; he was very weak, and his strength was soon exhausted. But they were together now, and no more bicycling squabbles would come between them. One day, when Gerald had begun to gain a little strength, he raised him- self on his elbow. "Etta, I'm such a broken-down wreck of a man now, you won't want to bind your bright life to

But for all answer she bent down and mopped gamy further words with a kiss.

A GIRTON GIRL.

•By J. M. BECKETT, • Author of "Those Rubies," "Child Eyes," "Eliza,"

"That Slip of a Girl," Scc.

Of course it behoved Augusta Temple to live up to her name—any girl who had been so christened would feel it her duty to do so, for Augusta s do not grow on every bush, and they are generally superior persons indeed. Augusta Temple was certainly one of these in every respect, for she had been to Girton, where she had attended all sorts of possible and impossible lectures; she also frequently addressed meetings on "The Higher Education of Woman " ; she had just read a most brilliant and spirited paper at one of them on the "Total Subjection of Man," and had discoursed with vigour at a gathering in a certain hall in Piccadilly the knotty question of the "Complete Regeneration of the World "—all with capitals; and the said question, after much talking among Augusta and her friends, had been satis- factorily settled at one sitting. With many advantages of money, indulgent and doting parents, a luxurious home, and, in fact, every single thing that heart could wish for, still these good gifts that the gods had provided her with were not good enough. The restless spirit of this helter-skelter, go- ahead, dash-along end of the century had seized her; she had breathed it into her system years before, when her parents were not so well off as they were now, and Augusta had been sent to a high school. She had graduated—or whatever they do at such places—and she had lapped up the tenets of the high school faith as readily as a kitten does its milk.

To be good-looking, elegant, possessed of an average intelligence and learning, with charm of manner, sweetness, goodness and gentleness—to be, in fact, just a true woman—was, of course, not enough for a girl of Augusta's high intellectual attributes.

"Women, pooh! They were really awful bores, just poor, weak things, without an object in life beyond husbands' babies, and fancy work." Augusta would make a sweeping statement of this sort with a wave of her white hand in her positive way. As to children, of what account were they in the general scheme of things? They should be left entirely to nurses and people with no brains; certainly they were beneath the notice of such as Augusta altogether: There was nothing under the sun which she was not absolutely positive about—she had all her very up-to-date notions in battle array, and she never failed to march them forth on most occasions. She was an extensively-read young woman; indeed, she could generally floor the most learned professors who ever came to dine at her father's table. They never said much—those learned professors; they just left her to do all the talking. They were content to listen; probably they were struck dumb with Augusta's wisdom. She had whole pasbages from the "Celestial Guttersnipes," or the last issue of the "Skylight" library at her finger-tips. Shaksperei Dickens, and Thakeray, of course, were all very well for the poor female mind of the long ago, but nowadays, in this very enlightened age, nobody thought of reading them seriously; they had been completely swept away by the thrilling meanderings of the female novelist. As you will see, Augusta, was altogether a superior person, and a woman of intellect, and very delight- ful it must be to feel oneself such an entirely lofty and higher-educated personage. There was only one mistake about it all, and that was Augusta was pretty. Of course, it should not have been so, for what does intellect want with beauty? Nothing at all. But still such was the case, and pretty she undeniably was. Naturally, a woman of such high intellectuality could not fail to be a member of the Knickerbocker Club, which has its very superior quarters some- where out Barnes way. When Augusta and certain of her, special friends met together here their con- versation was warranted to strike awe into the average mortal's mind. _ _ _ _

The. Knickerbocker Club was a noble institution. Its •obj ect—which nobody had ever yet been quite able to discover—was a glorious one, and then one had only to witness the club bicycle parade, which took place every Tuesday and Friday, to be duly impressed, for a most imposing sight it was. Away they would fly on their wheels like lightning, twenty or thirty of them, in their beautiful divided skirts, and their very much undivided impudence, with their heads down and their backs up, in and out of the swarming carts and cabs, with anxious care writ large on their faces,which, in their unbounded faith in themselves they vaguely supposed as a nonchalant, don't-care-a-bang air. We have all seen something very much like them on an organ, I fancy. If one of them did condescend to come a cropper, and found her feminine modesty rolling in the duet, what matter that? And yet these ladies of the Knickerbocker Club never waxed more eloquent in their inconsistencies than on the subject of the total loss of chivalry in man. Man, they said, was a very debased animal indeed.

That sometiines an extra shadow would cloud Augusta's mother's brow, and.a faint sigh of some- thing very like regret escape from her, did not concern her daughter at all; mother was a dear, good, sweet creature, but so hopelessly behind the times. It was stupid to wish sometimes that one's only child would confide to her little everyday, commonplace, foolish flirtations and love troubles, and that sometimes she would be content to sit at home in the cosy drawing-room and busy her- self with her fancy work and suchlike everyday trifles, which were quite enough to interest her. Of course, this was all old-fashioned, and mother did not understand—she was only a mother, after all, and she had never been to Girton and attended lectures on the higher education of women. She had only one object in life, and that was to make her husband happy, and to be ready to listen patiently whenever Augusta chose to confide in her. Augusta's father, perhaps, was not quite so sympathetic with his daughter's quips and cranks; indeed, he had been known to use very bad language about her when, descending the stairs in the gloaming, he had come in sudden contact with Augusta's .new bicycle, which was displaying the charms of its personality in the hall, which is certainly not the right place for bicycles at all. Father and mother, perhaps ofttimes wished that their offspring would be a little more ready to spend her time in the dainty, pretty, girlish rooms their love and care had provided her with, that she would not always take life quite so seriously; in short, that she would a little e learly indicate to the world at large to what sex rie really claimed to belong. But there was one thing peculiar about Augusta, and that was that she was by no means indifferent to her personal appearance; shn was ,quite aware of her charms, and she dressed vell, if somewhat masculinely, in the general way; also, it did not appear to be beneath her to make herself as amiable as possible to that debased creature— the mere man—whenever he came across her path. But this is merely by the way. Augusta and her family had come up from the country to their town house, in Grosvenor-square. The season was just beginning, and they had arrived on the scene prepared to enter into it as far as Augusta's advanced notions would allow. Among other things, she rejoiced greatly to be once twain in the areas, so to speak, of her Knicker- bocker Club, from the delights of which she had, for three long, weary months, been debarred. To-night there was Mrs. Heaton's ball, which would be quite one of the events of the year for the "society " papers to gush and wax eloquent over. Augusta, in her very superior and lofty way, condescended to take an unusually long time in the performing of her toilette, and she certainly did look a very charming and beautiful figure, indeed, as she came downstairs in an unusually pretty and frivolous frock of something flimsy, and cloudy, and billowy, which floated about her in an altogether charming manner, warranted to create havoc in the breast feminine—and masculine too. And who would have thought that underneath all that society chiffon lay all those very advanced and very lofty comical notions? _ _ _

Augusta was soon being whirled away into tho vortex of Mrs. Heaton's revels.

That Captain Gerald Thornton—" Beauty " Thornton as he was known among his special friends of the Guards—would be there, perhaps had had a good deal to do with the especially fascinating costume Augusta had selected to don to-night, though if her very dearest friepd had dared even to hint vaguely at such an idea She would have been treated to a cold stare and a stately shrug of the superior shoulders. But, of all the inconsistencies of the world, as everybody who has had anything to do with them is perfectly aware, the Woman of Progression is the iinc!t inconsistent. Well, perhaps she might be forgiven for stooping to such very feminine, and therefore very foolish. weakness.

"GO AND PLAY GOLF."

Lord Stanley of Alderley's Advice to the

Premier:

Speaking on Wednesday night at Wellington Hall, near Chester, in support of the candidature of his son, the Hon. Arthur Stanley, for the Eddisbury division of Cheshire, Lord Stanley of Alderley said Mr. Balfour, who was the frontispiece for Mr. Chamberlain— (langhter)—said he only intended at the next election to ask for a mandate to threaten retaliation in order to push wider Free Trade. The speaker hoped nobody would be such fools as to give Mr. Balfour a mandate for anything, except to go home and play golf. (Laughter.) When Mr. Chamber- lain returned from South Africa and saw the horrible muddle the Government were in with the Education Bill he had sufficient of the twilight, of his old Liberalism to see what a bad policy that was for a man who posed as a popular leader. Mr. Chamberlain, however, made a great mistake if he thought that starting a new abomination would make people more ready to condone the old abomination that political blunders had a cumulative effect.
