MOTOR NOTES.

A case which raises some points of the greatest interest to motorists in general has just been heard at Greenwich. A professional chauffeur was summoned for breaches of the Motor Car Act, and his employer was also summoned for causing the offences to be com- mitted.

There were three charges in all. The first was con- nected with not having the weight of the car painted on its side, although it was drawing another vehicle and exceeded 15cwt. unladen. This is a purely techni- cal offence, and might well happen with any large car towing a disabled.sister. The second summons, which resulted in a fine of 40s. and costs, was for having on the towed vehicle a brake which only acted on one wheel ; and the third, ultimately dismissed on account of an error in the manner in which it had been issued, was for failure to have two independent brakes acting upon the car to which the first ',summons referred.

Now this last is a print which is too frequently neglected and it is satisfactory to note that official notice has been taken of the matter. The Motor Car Act distinctly enjoins that two independent brakes shall be fitted, and that each brake shall act upon the two wheels carried on the same axle. This wording, there- fore, makes it quite plain that the brake acting upon the two driving wheels can only be regarded as one, and not as two, as some makers insist. Moreover, the

phrase "two independent brakes " puts this feeble minded contention out of court at once.

It seems most extraordinary that it should be necessary to legislate for the safety of the drivers themselves ; but it is even more remarkable that there should arise an occasion for enforcing the penalty which non-observ- ance of this regulation 11lows. The man who deliber- ately sets out on a tour knowing full well that one brake has been taken off and that the second is not really reliable is on the mental plane of the servant maid who goes to look for an escape of gas with a lighted candle When the inevitable occurs, no doubt both of them feel themselves hardly used by fate—if they have a chance of thinkingat all—but in each case it is only the obvious effect of an easily preventable cause.

The side entrance, which has been such a feature on many of the large cars this year, has much to be said both for and against its use. When lady passengers are to be carried, it is certainly more convenient for them to be able to step into the vehicle from the kerb stone instead of being compelled to go first into a muddy or dusty roadway in order to reach the tonneau door. On the other hand, many of the side-openings are so narrow as to make matters very little better for thera,so far as their dresses are concerned, for, by the time they have squeezed in the little aperture, their gowns are crumpled and tumbled,and, not infrequently,muddied as well.

If the side-entrance is to he adopted, the wheel-base must be extended,so as to allow for a proper-sized door being used. On the Decauville brougham, the side- doors employed are exactly the same as those upon an ordinary horse-drawn brougham, and by this means the full advantages of the side-entrance are attained. The chassis used for this vehicle is extra-long ; but, so far from this being a drawback, it is an advantage, for a long car steers far more steadily, especially over greasy roads. _ _

Many of the alleged side-doors are not doors at all, but are merely a species of sliding panel. Upon other cars, the front seat sometimes swings back and thus allows entrance to the rear part of the car, which is it- self quite enclosed. Personally, we prefer a door of some description to be fitted independently of the fore portion. There is more or less of a scramble in getting in or out when the passengers in front have to be dis- turbed for the purpose, whilst in the event of having to jump out man emergency, the advantages of a rear or side door will be at once appreciated.

The Motor Cycling Club's Team race resulted in a win for the Coventry Club with the M.C.C.'s tearn oteond.

The event was run off upon Oxfordshire roads and all arrangements made had been so well planned out before- hand that everything passed off sithout a single hitch. We should like to 'see the Auto-Cycle Club take a lesson in management and organ ization from this body and bring off their little fixtures with equal success. Indeed when it is borne in mind that this latter association charges heavy entrance fees, the competitors have every right to expect that the work shall be done at least quite as conscientiously as that performed in a sister club where the officials are honorary and no profit what- ever is attempted to be reaped from the different fixtures its committee plans for the season.

The Trimo" was the only tri-car which survived the Thousand Miles Trial. This machine has certainly come off with flying colours in open competitions on the road this year and has demonstrated the reliability which can really be attained with a machine of this class. It will be remembered that this was about the only one to make the run from London to Edinburgh at Whitsuntide within the specified twenty-two hours.

More side-cars have recently been seen upon the high roads leading out of London, .but it is doubtful whether these will ever attain the amount of popularity that was predicted for them. rhe position for the passenger is the most comfortable of all; but the side-strain on the mltor cycle to which the carriage is attached is evidently too great, unless the frame is exceptionally well designed. The terrible accident recently reported as having happened to Mr. Langley while touring in Dovedale was much exaggerated in the general press but at the same time it was quite bad enough. The wonder is that under the circumstances, it was not much worse. Mr. Langley, according to his own account, was driving his 12 h.p. Decauville down a steep hill, at a speed he estimates to have been between forty and fifty miles an hour, when it struck a hump in the road surface. It turned aside, by the force of the impact, to the side of the road, caught a chain railing, the shock throwing the driver out. This was fortunate, as it happened, for the vehicle then turned over, and, otherwise, he must have been pinned underneath. The front wheels were damaged—one, indeed, was wrecked; but the engine itself appeared to be absolutely unhurt, a fact which speaks volumes for the soundness of its construction.

At the same time, a car can never be regarded as the same after such an accident has happened. The obvious defects ruay be repaired ; but there are certain injuries which may not be imrnesliately apparent but are almost certain to have been inflicted. Personally, if I were unfortunate enough to have so had an accident with my car, I should never attempt to use it myself again for a long journey, for T should never again have complete faith in its reliability. There are so many parts that must be injured after an accident like the above, that I should not feel safe at proceeding at more than a very slow speed indeed. • But then I cannot understand any- one driving down a hill at that rate. The car must have been going quite at the higher figure given—a terrible bat fora vehicle which was, apparently, beyond control—to encounter any obstruction whatever in the road. Collisions are sometimes unavoidable, and an accident resulting therefrom can be understood; but to be run away with by your own car seems to suggest bad driving in the first place and a want of resource in the second, and neither of these qualities are pleasing characteristics in a man who drives a fast vehicle.

It is possible that the brakes failed, though it is hardly likely that both were useless. If they were, then the car had been very carelessly prepared for the road. At any rate, before it had attained so great a speed, and directly the driver began to fear it was getting too much for him to control, he had two or other devices at hand to retard its progress, if he had understood sufficient of its mechanism. He could have slipped his change speed lever into the lowest notch, retarding his spark and throttle to the utmost. He could have switched off, with the lowest speed in gear, and run her down against the compression of the engine. Or, as s last resource, he could have slipped in his reverse. If his foot brake was working, the second plan would not have been anadvisable one to adopt because on the Decauville the foot brake takes out the clutch; but the first would have answered very well. So long as a driver has steady nerves and a cool head, there is not the slightest reason why any car should get beyond control.

A WEDDING FIASCO Ay RUNCORN.

There was .a.i:erial-ple wedding fiasco at Runcorn Parish Church on Sunday. A young man of Newton had arranged to marry a young woman who resides in Cross- street. The banns had been published, and the ring bought, and the Rev. W. Scholes attended the parish church to perform the ceremony at 9 a.m. The bride and her attendants duly put in an appearance, but the bridegroom came not; and as the hour of morning service was drawing nigh, the clergyman postponed the ceremony until noon. In the meantime a search was made for the bridegroom, who was found at his home. He explained that he had overslept himself, but promised faithfully to turn up at church at twelve o'clock. A large crowd assembled to witness the postponed ceremony, but they were doomed to disappointment, for on this occasion neither bride nor bridegroom were on view. It was understood that the bridegroom had changed his mind, and that the marriage was " off." The disappointed bride is of poor parentage, and it is said that the neighbours in the locality where she lives are subscribing funds in order that she may take legal action.

THE ST. HELENS SENSATION. Mrs. Burntired Before the Magistrates THE CHARGE OF ATTEMPTED SUICIDE.

Mrs. Ellen Burndred, of 33, South John street, Parr, who figured so prominently last week in connection with the St. Helens sensation, was brought before the magistrates on Monday on remand, charged with at- tempted suicide. The previous week-end St. Helens was thrown into a state of great excitement by the exhumation of the body of a girl who had lived with Mrs. Burndred, and the excitement was greatly increased when it was stated that Mrs. Burndred had attempted to drown herself. The facts laid before the coroner's jury on Monday week were to the effect that the custody of a girl named Sarah Ann Jones had been entrusted to Mrs. Burndred by the guardians in August, 1901. In April of this year the girl went back to the workhouse, but returned to Mrs. Burndred in June last. The girl,who,it is alleged, had been insured in 1901 for £2O 125., died, after ashort illness, on the 7th inst. and was buried on the 11th. Her body was disinterred by the police late on Saturday, the 20th inst., and a post-mortem was carried out the next day by a number of doctors. Mrs. Burndred had herself lost five of her children, but she seemed a respectable woman, and attended the Salvation Army meetings, though she was not, as has been erroneously stated in some quarters, officially con- nected with or a member of thearmy. Early on Sunday morning the 21st,Mrg. Burndred was rescued from a deep pool of water near Tickle's Brick- works, at Parr. and she was consequently locked up. At the police court she was charged on the following Mon- day with this offence, and was remanded to gaol for a week, notwithstanding the application by her solicitor, Mr. 11. L. Riley, for bail. The inquest on Sarah Ann Jones has been adjourned to September 12. pending the result of the analysis of the organ. 3 of the girl's body. The police court was crowded yesterday, and a lot of people bad to be refused admission. Mrs. Burndred appeared in a nervous condition when she stood up in the dock. Chief Constable Wood asked for the woman to be remanded in custody to Walton Gaol until next Monday. Mr. Riley made a strong claim for the right of Mrs. Burndred to be admitted to bail. There was only one charge against her, and the bench could only take cor,- nisance of it. It was uot because the bench had read in the papers that there was a possibility of some other charge being laid against her that they should allow these proceedings to hang over her head in that manner, when they could be concluded in half-an-hour. That consideration was not good ground for them to do indirectly what they could not do directly—viz., to deprive her of her liberty. There was no reason on earth why that charge of attempted suicide should not be beard and dealt with, either by dismissing her or sending her for trial. Benches of magistrates through- out the country recognised the futility and the absurdity of trying to punish people far something which, imme- diately after the period of punishment had expired, they could do again unless their mind and disposition had undergone some great change. He suggested that the bench might dismiss her with a caution. The magistrates remanded Mrs. Burndred in custody.

===== 0002601_19040903_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
AN EARLESTOWN MYSTERY.

Woman Found Dead on the Common. On Sunday afternoon a young girl named Evans was playing on Newton Common, Earlestown, when she came upon the dead body of a woman, at present unknown. Information was given to the police, who removed the body to await identification. Four small abrasions were found on the neck, but there were no signs of a struggle having taken place. The following is a police description of the dead woman : Aged about 50 to 55; stoutly built; grey hair and eyes; dressed in black shirt; tight fitting jacket, with fancy work on the wrist and collar, and an old pair of men's lace up boots.

===== 0002601_19040903_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
THE INQUEST.

On Monday evening Mr. Brighouse held an inquiry at the Earlestown Town Hall. The Coroner read the police report which stated that at 1.30 p.m. on Sunday, a girl named Margaret Evans was playing on Newton Common with other girls when she noticed the body of a woman lying on the ground. She told a boy named Harold Case, who was along with several other boys in Viaduct-street. and they went and saw the body. They found deceased's clothes disarranged, her right leg was bent at the knee her hat was on her breast and a torn War Cry," which, bad the name "St. Amant" written upon it was found close to her head. Case went to the Ship Inn and told a barmaid, who gave information to P.C. Campsie. When he arrived at the spot deceased's clothes had been put straight by a man named Roscoe who had come from the Ship Inn. The woman lay on her back in a kind of a dingle and had four abrasions on the front of her throat which had apparently been recently done. There were also a number of blood stains on her body which was removed to the mortuary. Mr. Brighouse stated that he ordered a post mortem examination to be made and that had revealed the fact that the woman bad died from natural causes. They had not yet succeeded in identifying deceased, but a pedlar's certificate was found upon the body giving the name, of Elizabeth Plum,- and address 20, Charter-st., Manchester.—In view of the fact that the woman was believed to have been seen in the company of a man on the previous night, he would adjourn the inquest so that the police might try and find out the identity of both the woman and the man. Mr. Attfield (foreman of the jury) said he would like to ask a question with regard to the long distance jury- men had to travel to the mortuary on a case of that sort, and he would like to know if a waggonette could not be chartered to convey he members of the jury to the mortaary. The Coroner replied that in several-places, where the mortuary was situated some distance out of the town,a I conveyance wassupplied for the jury. In Southport they had the good fortune to have a Town Council and they providedia conveyance for jurvmen.He would suggest that Mr.Tr.spector Forster be asked to put the matter be- fore Superintendent Cocker who might be able to allow the expenses of a waggonette in such cases, out of the contingent fund. He quite agreed that it was a long distance for jurymen to walk. Mr. Carlin, a juror, suggested that the mortuary ,should be removod into the Town Hall yard where it would be very handy. Another juror said that it would be objectionable in such a populous district. The Coroner said that the mortuary at Bootle was ir the Town Hall yard, and thousands of people passed it daily and never knew it was there. COuncillor Beaty stated that the district had been put to a great expense in building the mortuary at the cemetery. ?GMT'. Attfield said there bad been agitation on foot for ten years to have the mortuary removed to a more con- venient centre. The Coroner remarked that he hoped the Press would cease to give flaring and sensational head-lines in the papers. It caused‘persons to be trightened to walk about lonely places. The inquest was then adjourned.

The Examiner, Saturday, September 3, 1904. THE ST. HELENS TRAGEDY. INQUEST AND VERDICT. Two Men Committed For Trial.

At the St. Helens Town Hall on Wednesday, the County Coroner (Mr. S. Brighouse) conducted his inquiry into the circumstances attending the death of Thomas Duffy, 27 years of age, a colliery labourer, of 11, Francis. Street, Sutton, who was found in a dying condition on a plot of waste land in Warrington New-road, shortly after midnight on Saturday week. Duffy was found to be suffering from a bad wound on the head in addition to other bruises, and died a few minutes after he was discovered. A young man named Richard Briody, better known as Patrick Hackett, of 27. College-street, was afterwards arrested, and, pending the coroner's inquiry, was remanded by the borough magistrates on a charge of causing Duffy's death. The affair has created great excitement in the town, and during the seven hours, which yesterday's proaeedings occupied,a large crowd gathered outside the Police Court, where the inquiry was held. Chief-Con- stable Wood attended on behalf of the police authorities, and Mr. H. L. Riley. appeared for the man Briody, who had.been allowed out on bail by the magistrates. _

P olice-Constable Perry deposed that about ten minutes to one on Sunday morning, the 21st inst., he received information of a row, and went with Constable Murant to Wa rrington New-road. They found Duffy lying on his back unconscious. He was without hat or cap, and his shirt front was open: Blood and froth were coming from his mouth, and blood from his nose. He never recovered consciousness, and died about ten minutes later. The body was removed to the mortuary. George Riley, of 9, Frazer-street, Parr, said that he and three companions, named Herring, Connor, and Ledger, were standing at the corner of Parr-street and Warrington New-road about twelve o'clock on the night in question, and saw some men running along the road. They followed tla see what was the matter, and saw Briody, with his coat and cap off, running towards St. Helens. Near the Baths Hotel he saw several men there. Duffy and a man named Egan were lying in the road, and witness saw another man, named Bellard, kick Duffy three or four times, once on the head. At the request of the coroner witness went into the body of the court, which was filled, and picked out Bollard as the man who kicked Duffy. He afterwards , continued his evidence. He said he told Bollard OW it was not fair t 9 kick a man whilst he was on the ground, and was about to strike Bellard for doing so when he saw Briody coming up with his coat off. Witness did not see Briody kick or strike the deceased, though he appeared to attempt to kick him. Witness and his companions went towards the Albion Hotel,and were joined by Briody and Bellard, who had left. Duffy on the ground. Bellard said to Briody, "Let's go back and finish him," and Briody replied, "What use are you if we do go back ?' I've just knocked five or six out myself." In reply to Chief-constable Wood, witness said he had known Briody for some time. James Ledger, collier, gave evidence corroborative of 'Riley's statement, adding that Briody said to him, "1 I have just knocked out four or five Welshmen." He turned back with witness and his companions, and showed them where Duffy was lying on the ground unconscious. Witness afterwards saw Duffy lying unconscious on the waste land. where someone had removed him to, and, becoming alarmed, he went and gave information to Constable Rigby.—ln reply to Mr. Riley, witness said he knew that Briody had been a pro- fessional boxer for sometime, and when a boxer spoke of "knocking a man out" he meant with his fist. Briody explained to witness that he and his companions had met a party coming in the opposite direction, and when one of his companions asked for a match they were told they were a match for them.

gichael Herring, a collier, also rave corroborative evidence.

Peter Clark,a labourer, stated that he was going along Warrington New-road, and saw a man lying on the foot- path. There were two other men near. One was kneeling down, apparently feeling at the unconscious man's heart, and the other was standing by. Witness askei who had done that, and without saying anything in reply one of thp men ran away. The other one was also going to run, but witness asked him not to do so, as he would only get locked up. The man who remained said he did not know the unconscious man was as bad as he turned out to be. Witness and he carried deceased on to the waste land. Witness did not know who the man was. Witness met a group of people against the Albion Hotel. Amongst them was Briody, and witness I asked him if it was he who knocked that man out. Briody replied, "Yes I have knocked six men out." Michael Giblin, of Collins Green,one of the men with deceased's party, said they left the beerhouse in Sand- field-crescent at closing time, and in Warrington New- road, on their way home, near the Baths Hotel, they met five or six men coming towards St. Helens. They did not know the other men, but one of the other lot asked for some matches. They replied that they had not got one, and someone then shouted that they would have to have one. A man whom they called " Paddy " took his coat off. The first blow was struck by the I other party at Patrick Egan, and the latter struck back. A row thencommenced, and witness was twice kicked. Deceased and James Egan were both on the ground. Witness then ran away, and was afterwards joined by the other members of the party, who thought that deceased had also made his escape. Witness did not see deceased struck, and he denied that his party was spoiling for a 'fight. Several other witnesses were called, including Patrick Egan, one of deceased's party, who denied that any of his friends shouted that they were a match for the other side.—James Egan, brother of last witness, said he had to remain in bed on Sunday owing to the treatment he received.—Patrick Doherty, another member of Duffy's party, gave similar evidence. Michael Farrelly, a wire roller, 18 years of age said he was returning to St. Helens with Briody and his I companions on the night in question. Near to the I Baths Hotel they met the other party, and when they asked for a match the reply was. "Which way will you have it?" Bellard was at once knocked down Witness was struck on the face and knocked down and kicked.

Thomas Johnson, a collier, said Briody handed his coat and hat to him, when their companions were attacked. A FRACTURED SKULL. Dr. Masson, who had made a post-mortem examina- tion, said there was a fracture of the base of the skull. Death was due to compression of the brain by a clot of blood arising from the fracture of the skull. He was of opinion that the fracture had been caused by a kick and not by a fall on a projecting substance, though it was possible for the fracture to be so caused. THE ACCUSED'S VERSIONS. Richard Briody, who was under magisterial remand on a charge of causing Duffy's death, was then called, and was examinei by Mr. Riley. He said that on the night in question live of them were returning to St. Helens, when they met another party of five men going in the opposite direction. A man with them wanted a match, and one of the other party said, "I am a match for you any time," and struck Bollard. The same man sparred up to witness, and there was a general scrim- mage. . William Bellard, a collier, was also called. . VERDICT OF MANSLAUGHTER: TWO MEN CO MMITTE D. The Coroner having summed up, the jury retired, and were away for about twenty minutes. The Foreman, in answer to the coroner, said they were unanimously agreed tbat the fracture of the skull was brought about by injuries inflicted by Richard Briody and William 13e11ard, both of whom used more violence that was necessary.

The Coroner said that was a verdict of" Manslaugh- ter "against both men, and it was his duty to commit Briody and Renard for trial upon that charge.

SPORTS AT CROFT.

Warrington Winners.

There were several Warrington winners at the fifth annual sports promoted by the Croft Horticultural Society which were held in a field lent by Mr. E. W. Sankey on Saturday. There were 321 entries, and the weather being beauti folly fine nearly 2,000 spectators assembled. .1. On the 25th inst. there was launched from the ship- building yard of Messrs. Workman, Clark & Co.,Belfast,l the steamer Victorian, the first of the two turbine- driven ships ordered by the Allan Line. The Victorian is the pioneer turbine vessel for the Atlantic or any other ocean service, and, as such, her launch is an event of more than ordinary interest. She and the Virginian, now being built on the Clyle, are sister ships as regards dimensions, capacity and power. This pair of twelve thousand tonners will form a notable reinforcement to the fine fleet of the Allan Line, which already numbers twenty-eight steamers,and com- prises several vessels of ten thousand tons each engaged in the mail, passenger, and general service between the United Kingdom and Canada. Splendid ships, however, as are the Bavarian, Tunisian, Parisian, and lonian, the Victorian exceeds them in size by 2,000 tons, and, as regards speed, is exp9cted to be a long way in advance of them. That she is one of the handsomest vessels ever built in Belfast was the opinion of every expert who saw her. on the stocks before the launch or in the water after- wards. She is a striking contrast to the ordinary straight-sided ocean steamer of to-day. Her lines fore and aft are sharp and clean, swelling gracefully into a noble breadth amidships, which suggests high qualities of steadiness and stability, as well as a capacity for speed, which could hardly be excelled. Before she took the water people viewed with interest her three pro- pellers—a novel feature in an Atlantic liner. , It is not, however, her grace of outline, nor the promise of perfect internal equipment (which the work already done indicates)] that makes the Victorian so interesting an object to the shipping world just now. It is her position as the pioneer of a departure which may revolutionise the character of the ocean liner from a shipbuilder's and passenger's standpoiht with as great a degree as did the departure which introduced the twin-screw and the five hundred footer. For a considerable time it has been debated whether the turbine would be practicable as a means of propell- ing the giant ships that carry our cross-Atlantic passenger traffic, and while other great shipping com- panies were looking for inure light and hesitating to plunge into a practical experiment that might involve heavy loss, the Allan Company boldly assumed the res- ponsibility of giving the lead. They lost no time either. Ten months ago the keel of the Victorian was laid by Messrs. Workman, Clark 43: Co. Yesterday she was successfully launched, and before the end of the year it ' is expected she will be ready for sea. Her trial trip will of course be the crucial test, and upon it much will depend. For it may be taken for granted that other important companies which have decided to adopt tur- bine engines for new liners will watch with attention the earliest performances of the Victorian. Originally the Victorian was designed to be driven by reciprocating engines, but after some progress had been made with her hull, Messrs. Allan decided that she should have turbines instead. Messrs. Workman, Clark &-, Co. made the necessary alterations in her structural design, and at the same time undertook what was a far more difficult and delicate task, the construc- tion of the great turbines—the largest ever made— which are to drive her. This was by arrangement with Messrs. Parsons & Co., for it was the Parsons' turbine that was decided upon. A high-pressure and two low- pressure turbines will drive the three propellers of the ship, which, by the way, strike one as being unusually small to 'drive a monster possessing a cargo capacity of more than 8,000 tons, besides accom- modation and equipment for upwards of 1,300 passengers. These propellers, however, revolve at a very high speed —from 270 to 300 revolutions per minute. The central one, arranged as in a single screw vessel, is worked by the high-pressure turbine, the others, which, are arranged as in a twin-screw ship, hy the low-pressure turbines' The two latter have each a reversing arrangement which enables them to be driven full speed astern, either together or independently. Thus the ship will be as easily and effectively manceuvred as regards turning or backing as an ordinary twin-screw. This dis- poses of the objection which has sometimes been urged against turbines, that they are defective with regard to reversing motion. The principle of the steam turbine is less generally understood than that of ordinary reciprocating engines. Briefly, a turbine engine is a fixed cylinder upon the inside surface, of which are mounted rings of brass blades projecting radically inwards. Inside this revolves a drum armed on its outer surface with similar rings of blades set at an angle to the fixed blades, and arranged so that they are "sandwiched," so to speak, between those of the fixed cylinder. Steam is admitted at one end of the turbine,and passes through longtitudinally in a zig-zag path, being deflected from the fixed rows of blades in the tiirbine caging against the rows of blades on the drum, causing the latter, which is built on the propeller grafting, to revolve, and thus drive the pro- peller. The "fixed blades " (those in the cylinder) act as guides to deliver the steam with proper direction and velocity against the "moving blades" (those on the I drum.) Thus the full power of the steam is utilised, and in a direct and continuous way. The term" blades " when used in connection with the machinery of an ocean liner seems to suggest a screw propeller or something equally formidable. But these turbine blades are surprisingly small—no larger than a lady's little finger. Their, number, however, is prodigious, there being no less thane million and a half separate pieces used in the bidding of the three turbines of the Victorian.

To see these appliances being made in Messrs. Work- man, Clark and Co.'s fine engine works, suggests the manufacture of a Titlnic 'musical box or some mechanical toy for the amusement of a giant. The ponderous steel casing and drtlm of each of the low- pressure turbines (the mass that means strength and resisting power to steam pressure) weighs sOmething like ninety-five tons, while the "blades," moving and fixed, are so tiny and fragile-looking that the contrast is remarkable. it seems preposterous to suppose that these insignificant little points, even in multitude, can be the agency to send the great liner through the sea at a speed of something like eighteen or twenty knots an hour. That is what they are expected to be nevertheless.

It may easily be imagined when the principle of the steam turbine is graSed that everything depends upon the proper "set "and inclination of the two sets of blades. Hence the difficult and delicate character of the constructive work. The manufadture of the turbines is well forward. Messrs. Workman, Clarke Si. Co. have a special engine shop,equipped with the necessary plant devoted to them, and Mr. C. E. Allan, one, of the mem- bers of the firm, personally superintends their construc- tion. It will, however, require some weeks longer of skilful and arduous labour before the turbines are ready to be fitted in the Victorian. Thanks to the economy of space the builders have been able to provide accommodation on board the Victorian, such as is probably not to beequalled by any vessel of her size afloat. .

The length of the Victorian is alo feet ; her breadth, GO feet ; her depth 40 feet 6 inches. She is .;ivided by bulk-heads into eleven compartments, and with the sub- divisions'of her double-bottom she has twenty water- tight spaces. Electric light throughout, a com- plete printing outfit, and an installation of Marconrs wireless telegraphy are among the arrangements for the comfort and convenience of passengers. As regards facilities for the handling of cargo, the ship is as perfectly fitted as possible. She has no less than

BATHING FATALITY AT LOWTON.-A JUROR FINED. An inquest was held on Monday before Mr. Brighouse county coroner, at the Reading Room, Lowton, touch- ing the death of Walter Vaughan, aged fourteen, o r Sadler's Fold, Lowton. On Sunday afternoon last the deceased and a companion were bathing in a large sheet of water known as "The Flash." The deceased, who could not swim, got out of his depth, and before any- one could render him assistance he was drowned.—The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death."—A police constable informed the Coroner that he had warned Henry Prescott, a railway pointsman, of Wash End Farm, Lowton, to attend and serve as a juror, but he informed him (the constable) that he did not think he was bound to attend, and he would see about it.—The Coroner said his instructions to the police were that jurors, if they hada reasonable excuse, were not to be inconvenienced, as they gave their services, but as Mr. Prescott had set the court at defiance he would inflict a fine of 20s. for his non-attendance.

WARRINGTON EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

A NONCONFORMIST GRIEVANCE. _ The Schools Selected for the Training of Pupil Teachers. OVERCROWDING QUESTION AGAIN.

The Warrington Education Committee held its monthly meeting at the Town Hnll,on Monday evening -under the presidency of Mr. F. Monks. There were also present :—The Mayor(Aldermaa Bolton), the Revs. John Yonge and Father Wilson.Aldetvnen Bennett (vice-chair- Inan),and Smethurst,Councillors Ellison,Davies, Dodds, Burton, and Tinnion. Colonel 3. D. Buckton, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, R. T. Fairclough, T. May, 3. Cpson. J. Bibby, G. Walker, E. Plinston, S. Fulton Smith, C. H. Unsworth, and J. Moore Murray (director of education.)

MR. MAY AND PCBLIC FINANCE. "NOT HTS SPECIAL FIELD OF ACTION." THE STRENGTH AND PAYMENT OF THE TEACHING STAFF.

At a meeting of the Finance Committee accounts totalling £3,03.i 9s. 64. were passed for payment. It was resolved that arrangements be made with the Royal Insurance Company for the •insurance of the furniture in the various elementary schools in the Borough for the sum of £lO,OOO, and of the furniture and fittings in the new Educatioa Office for the sum of £6OO. Mr. A. J. JOLLEY moved the confirmation of the minutes and pointed out that the above resolution was simply carrying out the directions of the full committee with regard to the insurance of furciture for which they were responsible and which in case IA fire they would have to replace. Councillor DODDS-seconded. Mr. MAY said the financial statement Was of such a character that it should not be passed in a formal way without having ,some •explanation from the chairman. There was en increase of £3O in the School Attendance Committee's accounts .and he thought it should be explained that that was due to the bills for the maintenance of chil- dren in Indestrial schocils'having been sent in earlier than usual. There was an advance of £l5O in the School Management Committee's expenditure and £l5O increase in elementary school teachers' salaries without any explanation. The last item—general accounts— which used to be ranch larger, was £420 as against £265 —another increase of £l5O. There were thus four in- creases of £l5O which made £soo—(Laughter, anda member: Bad aritimetic)—and they were being passed without a word of comment. It seemed to him that that was a very les way of doing business. He moved that the minutes be passed, with the excep- tion of the accounts, although he 'would be willing to withdrawthe motion on hearing a satis- factory explanation. The chairman of the committee promised on a former occesien that as soon as it could be conveniently done, and when it would afford useful Information to the committee, a compare, such as he (Mr. May) =snow making, would be given each month. He would like the increases.to he explained, as he wished to take an intelligent interest lathe business of the committee. There was no seconder ofthe motion, which therefore fell to the ground. Councillor DODDS said that substantially be was in sympathy with Mr. May who at the last meeting had the support of Mr. Walkeriin asking for a comparison between the salaries paid when the committee took over the education of the Borough and the amounts paid now, together with the seasons for any difference that was shown. He (Councillor Dodds) asked for the inform- ation at the last meeting of .the committee for he felt that as the chairman said'he would be willing to give them, the particulars should be supplied. Mr. UPSON asked if the furniture of the Secondary school had been insured. Mr.JoLLEY replied in the affirmative. With regard to what had fallen from councillor Dodds, he could only repeat at he had said on former occasions—'-that every one of the members of the Finance Committee and the officials were most desirous of helping any members of the full committee who wished for information, if that informatioe was available. He ventured to think he was voicing the sentiments of his colleagues when he said the committee was notprepared to give informa- tion that was inaccurate or likely to be misleading. In response to the request of Mr. Walker, certain figures had been got oat,and Mr. Walker had expressed himself pleased with the information. He (Mr. Jolley) wished to say in the most emphatic way that the particulars now furnished were not what Mr. May asked for, as he requested a compare "such as he was making nowt." A' compare which included the statement that four items of £l5O came to £5OO, and that an item involving the expenditure of £69 10s. 9d. (the School Management Committee's accounts) included an increase of .£lso,was not the kind of compare the members of the committee were prepared to give. (Laughter.) Their erudite and erring brother Istd made enormous arclueological research, but his special field of action did not quite seem to be that of a critic of public finance. (Laughter). Mr. MAN: I must protest against personalities. I. have never replied to personalities, and an Education Committee should not be defiled with them. Mr. JOLLEY then presented a statement giving particulars of the staff and salaries pdid at the end of the first month of the committee's existence —April 30th, 1903—as compared with the end of July this year. The staff in the elementary schools at the end of Aptil,l9o3, numbered i ' 316, which included six candidates. At the end of July this year the staff did not include any candidates and was 345 strong. It would therefore be fair to say that the committee had to deal with an increase of five. The salaries paid for April, 1903 to teachers of all grades' totalled £1571 9s. id. In July last the amount was. £2020 19s. 4d. Of that increase, £l6B Cs. 4d. was due to the raising of salaries of existing members of the staff, £169 ss. 4d. to the improvement of the quality of the staff, and £125 Bs. 104. to additions to the staff. In April of last year the committee employed 76 certificated assistant teachers, and in July this year the number was 132—an increase of 56, which be was sure everyone would agree was a desirable improvement. rhe number of ex-p. t's had de- creased from 110 to 91 during the same period, article 68 from 26 to 20, and pupil teachers from 79 (including six candidates) to 54. He ventured to think he had the confidence of the full committee when he assured them that the Finance Committee had not the smallest desire to keep anything back. They wished to give the fullest possible information which should be accurate, trustworthy, and helpful, but he, as chairman, had hitherto resisted several requests for information in the form they were given, because he felt, and still felt, that. the figures so readily lent themselves to mis- representation and misunderstanding. The CIIAIRMAN said the discussion was rather irregular, but he thought it was desirable that the information should be given. In future when anyi member wished for specific information, he (the chair- I man) thought he ought to give notice and have the , matter properly brought before the committee. The minutes were approved. A NONCONFORMIST GRIEVANCE. THE TRAINING OF PUPIL TEACHERS. ACCOMMODATION DIFFiCULTY.

At a meeting of the School Management Committee, the director having reoorted the arrangements it had been found necessary to make to cope with the over- crowded state of 'Hamilton-street, St. Ann's, St. Barnabas', and Et. George's schools, it was resolved that the arrangements be approved, and that application be made to tike Board of Education for tha recognition of the use of the Infants' depart- ments at those schools for staniard I thildren. A communication ,Was submitted from H.M. Inspector reporting upon the suitability of the schools in the Borough with a view to the practical training of pupil teachers therein an& upon his recommendation, the fol- lowing schools were selected as those at which pupil teachers may, in future, be apprenticed, viz :—Fairfield, Hamilton Street National, St. Alban's, St. James' Latchford ()fixed Department), St. Mary's Latchford, Warrington Parochial (Infants' Department), and War- rington British. A report upon the work of the Secon- dary school was received from the Board of Education drawing special attention to the fact that all the avail- able accommodation at the institute is utilized. The full report was published in last week's Examiner." A letter dated the Bth August, 1904. from the Board of Education, returning the plans of the Secondary school and intimating that they show satisfactory arrange- ments for the instruction of about 260 scholars, was submitted and noted. The Rev. Father WILSON mored the adoption of the minutes. He called attention to the fact that the schools mentioned la the first resolution—Hamilton- street, St. Ann's, St.Barnabas' and St. George's—were not the only ones in which, through overcrowding, Infants who should now be going up into the senior departments had to be kept in the infant department and there taught standard I. work. Through this arrangement, the committee lost probably ss. per head on each child in Government grant. At best the children in the infants' school earned only is. per head, whereas the amount paid in the senior department was 12s. The evening continuation school arrangements for next session were the same.as last year's. with the exceptioh that the Warrington Parochial Evening School had been discontinued as there did not seem to have been sufficient demand for it. The report of the Board of Education on theSeCondary school was most satisfactory and one upon which the committee might well con- gratulate themselves. Certainly it was one upon which they ought to congratulate the Director of Education and the staff of the school. A defect in ventilation to which attention was called was being remedied. If there was any sting in the report at all, it was contained in the last sentence of the first paragraph: "If the growth of the school is not to be checked, further class-rooms will be needed in the immediate future." He would ask those who were most nearly concerned to " mark, . learn and inwardly digest" the significance of that sentence. Mr. George G. Sumner, one of the teachers

at the Technical Institute, had resigned and his name would therefore be withdrawn from the list of teachers for the coining session. Mr. PLINSTON moved. an amendment that the minutes be confirmed with the exception of the one relating to the selection of seven schools at which pupil teachers may be apprenticed. He contended that sufficient con- sideration had not been given to the Nonconformists of the town. Of the seven sehools,four were Anglicas, two Roman Catholic and one Undenontinational. There could not be more than four pupil teachers assigned to , each school, and thirty-five was the number allowed to ' be sent to the schools for training during the ensuing, twelve months. In considering the question the com- mittee ought to have taken into account the fact that there were a great many more Nonconformist children in the town than there were Roman Catholic. Yet the members of the last nimed body had two schools to which pupil teachers could be sent. He thought each denomination should have its fair share. _ _

The DIRECTOR explained that the schools had to be recognized by the inspector as being suitable for the training of pupil teachers, and the committee asked the inspector to state which were suitable. The seven schools named were the first which the inspectors selected. No application had been made from a Non- conformist for admission to anylother school. All who had applied at this stage had asked to be sent to the schools named. There were five other schools which the inspector said would, if necessary, be passable— Wycliffe (mixed), Sacred Heart, St. Barnabas', Silver. street, and St. James'. If there was any demand from Nonconformists,the committee would take it into cor, sideration. The inspector had recognized some of the schools for the training of more than four pupil teachers, as the staff and buildings were satisfactory. Mr. PLINSTON : Isn't four really the number per- mitted? The DIRECTOR said that in accordance with the code, the inspector could, if he considered a school was suffi- ciently staffed and suitable in other respects, grant per- mission for more than four to be trained there. Mr. PLINSTOM : According to the explanation, other i schools are passable. If that s so, I would like to press my amendment, so that those schools may be recon- sidered, and perhaps a better selection made. The CIIA.IRM.A.N : It is not necessary. If there is any application, the committee will arrange for it. The Rev. J. YONGE said he hoped he would not be misunderstood if he asked to be allowed to second the amendment. He did so, not because he felt particularly aggrieved at the school he was associated with not being selected, but because he thought all the members of the committee must feel it eminently desirable that there should be no feeling in the town that any section of the community had been unfairly dealt with. He thought the committee would be well-advised in letting, the recommendation go back for a little moreconsidera- tion than it received atthe last committee meeting. On that Occasion several Nonconformist members were absent and probably they would attend the next meet- ing. He did not know that an) thing would be lost,and something might be gained by referring the matter back.

The Rev. FATHER WILSON thought both speakers seemed to be under a slight misapprehension as to the origin of the list of schools. The authority who had recognized them as suitable was not the committee but his Majesty's inspector. He thought the criticism brought to bear on the resolution showed the wisdom of the committee when it refused to undertake what it knew would be a very odious task, and asked the inspector to make the selection. If he understood the Director rightly it would not be competent even for the committee to admit pupil teachers to the five "passable" schools unless the inspector specially approved of the course—and he presumed the inspector would not do that, after the action he had taken, unless there was some adequate reason. No such reason seemed to exist at present. The rule was that there should be four teachers for each department rather than for each school. Mr. UPSON did not think it was sufficient argument for the rejection of the amendment to say that the schools had been selected by the inspector. (Hear,hear). Possibly he was a new man and was not aware of the denominational distribution of the schools. He (Mr. Upson) believed the selection had been made according to the buildings, the inspeotor taking what he supposed to be the best buildings in the town for the purpose. With reference to the distribution of the pupil teachers, he would point out that this year theyh ad only nineteen applications, but after this year they would have thirty-five. The committee decided twelve months ago that thirty-five would be required to keep up the staff of assistant teachers. He maintained that the seven schools selected did not admit of that number of pupil teachers being trained. Father Wilson had said quite properly that four pupil teachers were to be admitted to each department. If they took Warrington British school as an example it had only one department; and therefore only four Nonconformist pupil teachers could be sent to it. It was perfectly true that the inspector might say that they could send an additional one, two, or three, but that would not meet the case and itl would be a violation of the regulations for the admission of pupil teachers into the profession. They knew that the tendency of all printed minutes was to become narrowing in their effects. Parents'might apply next year for their children to be admitted to a particu- lar school, and be told at the office that the committee had a list of seven schools only from which they could make their choice. The whole tendency would be to restrict the pupil teachers to seven schools. Further consideration should be given to the question, not only by the School Management Committee but by the inspector. If, as the Director said, there were five additional schools, let them be mentioned in the minutes, and the Nonconformist parents would know that they had more opportunities than were afforded at one par- ticular school for the training of their boys and girls. Councillor TINNION said it did not seem to matter whether the choice of schools or the recommendation as to their suitability came from the committee or the inspector. If it was not exactly the best recommenda- tion that could be made, there was no reason why it should not be referred back. He thought every member of the committee would admit that there was plenty of room on a reasonable basis for the re-consideration of the matter. So far the educational work in Warrington had been ?carried on with as little friction as possible between NonconformiSts, Roman Catholics, or the Established Chinch. Anything that would tend to a continuance of that spirit was to be encouraged. He did not think Father Wilson stated the case quite cor- rectly when be said that the five other schools named were hardly suitable, and that the first seven were the only suitable ones. If the claims and recommendations of the other schools were laid before the inspector, committee, or whoever made the selection, it would be found that they could without any injustice select some of those schools which, in his opinion, were entitled to the consideration asked for. Mr. FAIRCLOLTGII supported the amendment in order that more information as to the number of pupil teachers in the town as compared with April 1903 might be forthcoming. From what he had seen of the different schools, there were not as many pupil teachers as there ought to be,and the question ought to be thoroughly gone into. Mr. Upson said that after this year there would be thirty-five apptications,but he (Mr. Fai relough) did not think there were thirty-five pupil teachers at the present time. The MAYOR asked the reason why any school should be debarred from taking in pupil teachers. He had understood that if a school was good enough for edu- cational purposes, it was fit to train pupil teachers in. Thebroader point of view from which they looked at the subject thebetter. The more teachers they trained, and the better they trained them, so much better would the education of the town be. He was sorry to see that the selection of the inspector and committee narrowed down the number of eligible schools. He supported the amendment. Alderman BENNETT said there was a great deal to be said from the point of view which had been expressed by the inspector. It seemed on the face of it desirable that pupil teachers should obtain their experienee on the most favourable conditions possible. (Hear,hear). At the first blush he had thought it would be better to take the inspector's suggestion, but as the debate had proceeded he had realized rather acutely that there was another side of the question. If they could remove any semblance of a grievance from the Nonconformists it was desirable to do so, as they were a very influential and important body, and some of their schools would lend themselves to the purpose they contemplated at the present time. it was a pleasure to them to know that from the commencement of the work of the Educa- tion Committee there had been no sectarian feeling displayed, and if a fair spirit was manifested now, that state of things would be continued. (Hear, hear). Mr. MAY supported the amendment because he con- tended that restricting the number of schools would tend also to restrict the number of pupil teachers, and increase the cost of education. His reading of the regulation in regard to pupil teachers was that the schools were selected that were most suitable for train- ing purposes. There must be some qualification in the staff of the school—time for the work and special attention paid to the young people. These were the reasons which had biased the inspector in the selection of the schools. Mr. JOLLEY Said some speakers had used arguments which were in favour of the action or the committee and at the same time announced that they were going to support the amendment. Were they in that matter to be guided by educetional or by denominational considerations? He hoped most sincerely that educa- tional considerations would have their due weight. They certainly had their 'weight with the School Manage- ment Committee. and he had no doubt they weighed with the inspector. Tt would be impracticable Ito carry out the suggestion of the Mayor if the work was to be done efficiently. The MAYOR: I must correct you there. T thought the inspector was casting a slur at every school that was not on the list, as being unfit for the training of teachers. ("No, no.") Mr. JOLLEY thought the Education Committee would be simply neglecting its duty in the most flagrant - manner if it allowed pupil teachers to be engaged at any school in the town. There were schools which were unfitted owing to the buildings, and by the nature of the staff,for the in. of pupil teachers. The aim of the committee was to raise the standard of the teachers, and he hoped members would not allow denominational considera- tions to come into their minds in voting on the matter..

===== 0002601_19040903_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, September 3, 1904.

It was not a question of Roman Catholics, Anglicans or Nonconformists, but a question of education, partly of staff, partly of building. The schools which had been selected in the schedule were the best buildings in the town, and the most suitable, they hoped. When the inspector deliberately recognized them as being the most fitted, he (Mr. 'Tolley) submitted that the commit- tee ought not lightly on mere denominational grounds or for what was an imaginary grievance to go against the action of the School Management Committee. The Sites and Buildings Committee were doing their hest to supply new schools, and they would be even more suitable for the training of pupil teachers than those on the list. The education would be absolutely undo- nominational, and the difficulty some gentlemen ex- perienced,and the grievance—which as far as he knew was an imaginary one—would disappear. (Hear, hear.) The amendment was carried, and the other minutes were agreed to. THE OVERCROWDING OF SCHOOLS. NECESSITY FOR MORE ACCOMMODATION AGAIN URGED. "DAMNING AND MURDEROUS" FACTS. At a meeting of the School Attendance Committee, on the 18th August, a letter, dated the 7th July. 1904, from the Board of Education, stating that, in view of the committee's assurance that steps will at once be taken to provide adequate public elementary school accommodation in the neighbourhood of the Hamilton- streetNational School,they will raise no objection to the average attendance at that school temporarily exceeding the recognized accommodation, was submitted. The Director also reported with reference to the pressure upon the accommodation of other schools in that dis- trict. It was resolved : "That, in view of the over- crowded state of the Hamilton-street, St. Ann's and St. George's Schools, the general want of school accommo- dation in that district, and the length of time which must elapse before new permanent schools can be erected, this committee strongly urges upon the Educa- tion Committee the necessity for carrying out the sug- gestion which was made by the School Attendance Com- mittee on the 21st March. that temporary school accommodation should be at once provided—if necessary, by means of the erection of iron buildings." Councillor ELLisos, in moving the confirmation of the minutes, pointed out that the resolution regard- ing the provision of temporary accommodation was the result of the committee's consideration of the state of affairs lathe Cockhedge district with regard to school accommodation. The Director supplied the committee with a number of figures at the meeting' and he had since drawn up a table giving full details as to the accommodation at every school. The schools were grouped in districts, and attention was more particu- larly directed to the first group (including the schools referred to in the resolution), as this was of pressing importance. The table showed over 350 more children on the books than there was accommodation for, whilst the average attendance for the first week of this school year was only just under 100 less than the accommoda- tion. These numbers referred to the total for all the schools, but the figures for the accommodation could not be regarded as accurate. It must be remembered that H.M. Inspector had stated that these accommoda- tion figures were misleading, and if anyone would visit any of the schools which had only as many children in as the accommodation allowed., they would find that it was almost impossible to move about. As a case in point, the Wycliffe school was supposed to have accom- modation for 733 in the mixed department. The average attendance for the week ending August 19th was 574, and yet the rooms were so overcrowded that they hail had to borrow one of the rooms from the infants' department, and there were other cases where similar borrowing had taken place. It would be seen that 274 children had been admitted to schools in this district, nearly all of whom had been sent up from the infants' departments, or had been old enough to enter the infants' departments, andl.so children had been refused admission. In order to meet the difficulty the School Management Committee had agreed to arrangements by which some 281 children would be kept in the infants departments:although they were old enough to be promoted. This meant a loss of from ss. to 6s. on each child, and the total number of children in this group of schools who would earn the lesser grant, on account of being in the infants' schools, amounted to 378. The attendance officers' time had been largely taken up since the holidays in finding places for those children who had been refused admission to the schools. The difficulty of getting children to school was seen by studying the figures. The School Attend- ance Committee were convinced that this state of affairs called for immediate action. They felt that unless temporary provision was made it was useless to attempt to enforce the bylaws. The result 'of this would be deplorable when con- sidered from the standpoint of the children's future. The committee were informed that the teachers of the infants' schools which were so crowded had been 11 instructed, as far as possible, to refuse admission to ' children under five, and they approved of the instruc- tion; but if that was the condition of affairs to-day what was going to happen? Within the last two years the increase of number of children on the books in Warrington had been over 700, and if it was necessary to find more accommodation three or four years ago how much more necessary was it to-day? Little or no accommodation had been provided since that date, and it was evident that the school population was increasing at the rate of about 300 a year, so that the committee ha.d considerable leeway to make up before proceeding to consider their normal increase. Seeing that it would be. some two years before any of the new schools would be ready for use, they asked the committee to approve of the resolution, and instruct the Sites and Buildings Committee to proceed with the provision of temporary accommodation, if necessary, in the form of temporary iron buildings or rent of rooms. Mr. WALKER seconded. Mr. MAY supported the confirmation of the minutes, and alluded to the facts submitted by the Chairman. They were" damning and murderous," because they meant the overcrowding of little children, and compul- sorily forcing them into schools and poisoning them with bad air, thus increasing infection in the borough. Therefore he contended he was not using inflammatory, excitable, or exaggerated language in saying that the schools were very bad indeed. He would put the matter in that way. (Laughter.) They could not continue as a committee to be responsible for such a state of things and the question was : "How are we to gel, at the work as economically and as quickly as possible ?" They had suggested iron buildings in the most crowded and deficient localities to be obtained, if possible, second hand, to save the expense of a new building, so that they might be put up at once. He believed thew) i was a building available n the borough at the present moment, so that it could be got ready for the present rschool year. Were they to wait another 12 months for the new schools, or take the practical suggestion of the committee, and no longer allow this disgraceful stat of matters to continue_? _

parents whose wages were low, he supposed that element could not be altogether excluded, but it ought, as far ns possible,to he excluded from the purview of the Sch 00l Attendance Committee in dealing with the cases. The committee was not appointed for the purpose of administering out relief. When there was real distress. the persons to deal with it were the Guardians and the expense of relieving it should bea Union and not a town charge. Colonel BUCKTON seconded, and contended that it was wrong to ask a man to pay only 2s. a month to provide for his child. The committee had to be fair and just to both sides—just to the parent, not to over- charge him, and fair to the committee and to the rate - payers. (Hear, hear.) Mr. PLINSTON opposed the amendment on the ground that the committee had given careful attention to the matter. It was the duty of the committee to look at the amount of the wages earned by the parent, the num- ber of children to be kept, and the rent to be paid. Taking all these matters into consideration the com- mittee came to the conclusion, he thought unanimously, that in the case in question 2s. a month was the full amount. They were convinced that it would have been an impossibility for the man to pay more. They had fixed larger amounts according to the income. Councillor TissioN was not prepared to vote for the amendment but he would support an amendment that would deal thoroughly with the question raised by Mr. Jolley as to whether the Education Committee was to become a charitable institution or the Board of Guardians was the proper authority to deal with cases of distress. Mr. MAY pointed out that in the case under notice the man was earning 18s. a week, had a wife and family of seven, and only occasional employment. It was utterly impossible for the committee to impose a larger sum. Councillor ELLISON said the committee had cons; derA the question fully from every point of view, and in a case of this particular kind it was always pointed out to the parents that they had got to keep the children at home,it being imperative that they should do so. There- fore the committee expected them to contribute towards the cost of the school. They tried to carry that out in every case as far as possible. It was arranged that in the present case the parent should commence with 2s. monthly, and it was impossible, under present circum- stances, to pay more. Considering everything tie did not see how the committee could do anything they had not done, and the cases were exceptional and deserving. Further, the people had been given to understand that the amount was subject to revision at any time. The Rev. J. YONGE asked if the parents could be • made to pay the money. The DIRECTOR quoted the regulation on the subject which showed that the parent was liable to contribute such sum as might be agreed upon with the school authority. If the parties disagreed,the authority could apply to a Court for an order. The amendment was defeated by a large majority. ARE CHILDREN OUTSIDE THE BOROUGH PROVIDED FOR?

Councillor Burton remarked that it had been °aid that the increase in the number of school children per year was about 300. He would like to ask if those children were of parents residing in the borough, or what number of them came from outside the borough. The CHAIRMAN : I think that is one of the ques- tions that had better not be asked. (Laughter.) Councillor BucKToN said he wished to emphasize the urgency of providing more school accommodation, but, although he was anxious to see the accommodation provided, he did not altogether agree with some of the .conolusions drawn from the figures submitted. He would point out that the accommodation recognized by the Board of Education was for average attendance, and not the number on the books. (Hear, hear.) The printed statement was therefore, liable to be misleading as altogether the net figures would show that the accommodation in the borough was much larger than the average attendance it had to provide for. He did not agree that an iron building should be erected. It was within his remembrance that in one part of the town a, school had been demanded by the Board of Education for the last four or five years, and they had not got it yet. Where that particular school was re- quired, land could have been obtained at any time. The MAYOR: We are waiting for the Education Department to approve the site we have selected. Councillor ELLISON said he would like to emphasize the necessity of obtaining some rooms if they could not get an iron building. He thought they might ob- tain rooms for the infant departments. Mr.FAIRCLOUGH : You have plenty of infant rooms. Councillor ELLISON : But they could make room for others.

The minutes were then approved. SITES AND BUILDINGS COMMITTEE.

This committee accepted the tender of Mr. J. H. Heywood to erect two partitions for the division of the two classrooms in the Secondary school for £79 ss. The minutes were approved.

FLOGGING IN THE NAVY. Letter to the Prime Minister.

The following letter has been addressed to the R ight , Hon. Arthur J. Balfour, First Lord of the Treasury, on behalf of the Committee of the Humanitarian League : Sir,—With reference to your reply to Mr. Swift Mac Neill on August 13, in which you state that there is no flogging of men in the Navy, we venture to draw your attention to the fact that the use of the "cat " has not, as is widely supposed, been abolished, but is merely (to quote an official deecription) "held in reserve," and may be revived at any time by order of the Admiralty. During the present mancevres, for instance, one of these degrading instru- ments of torture will be-carried on board every vessel in the British fleet, as an emblem presumably, of the confidence which the Government reposes in the character of our sailors—and this at a time when the "cat" has long ceased to exist in connection with the IBritish Army. Yot; also intimated, in your answer to IMr. Mac Neil, that there is no intention to discontinue the punishments of birching and caning in the case of "boys" in the Navy, that is to say, young men up to the age of 18. We would remind you that on March 19 a memorial on this subject was addressed by the Humanitarian League to the First Lord Of the Admiralty, which has led to a great amount of discussion, over two thousand letters, &c., having appeared in the press, and over twenty ques- tions having been asked in Parliament. In spite of the extreme unwillingness of the Naval Lords to give information, and the- persistently evasive nature of the replies of Mr. Pretyman, it has been elicited • (1) that flogging with birch or cane is frequently inflicted for trivial offences(2) thatall such punishments are inflicted publicly (3) that as many as 24 cuts with the birch are given on the bare skin (4) that the weight of the naval birch considerably exceeds that of the birch used in police courts, and (s)that no proper returns are made of the number of canings inflicted annually. Under these circumstances, and in.view of the fact that it is found possible in the Navies of other great Powers to nrintain discipline without resort to corporal punishment, we would urge on your consideration that the time has now come when the British sailor, no less than the British soldier, should be treated as a rational person, capable of dignity and self-respect, and that all flogging whatsoever—whether with "cat,' or birch, or cane—should be finally and specifically abolished.

THE LATE MR. EDWIN RICHARDSON.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS,

Rugby Football.

(By " Ovalist.")

Warrington hope and intend to make things hum during the coming season, and they are able to put a team in the field which should go a long way towards realising the highest expectations. The Mather-lane folk ought to be trembling in their shoes at the forces Warrington are bringing in order to affect their sub- jugation on Saturday. The first team contains all last season's clever backs with the addition of three new forwards, about whom so much has been said during the last few weeks. The full fifteen will thus be: Hallam, fullback; Fish, Isherwood, Dickenson and Harris, three-quarters ; Davies and Hockenhall, half backs ; Preston, Shugars, Belton, Boardman, Thomas, Cook, Naylor, and Jolley, forwards.

Leigh are opposing with Burgess full back; Neville, Bennett, Messer and Johnson, three-quarters: Dunbavin and Brady, half-backs; Boardman Berry, Silcock, Blackburn, Lee, Roberts (W), Kelly and O'Neill, forwards.

Making a paper comparison between the two teams, Warrington appear to be immensely superior to the home lot. Still they never got an easy thing at Leigh yet, and they will need to play hard and well to get two points out of the Mather-laners. The latter will be just as keen as Warrington to open the season with a win and are sure to put forth every effort.

The many Warringtonians who are sure to be present if the weather be fine, will be interested in seeing the debut of Burgess at full back. Somehow I can hardly imagine him being a success in this position, seeing that he never was noted for coolness on the field of play, and was never above the ordinary either as a-kick or catch. That he will do his best and work hard wilierever he is we all know, but Leigh must be hard up when they have to put the ex-War- rington half-back in the rearmost position.

Their new season's goods are not displayed in the shop window this time. The new men are all down on the "A " team where they will be fully extended by the Warrington second string, who are likely to prove a very warm lot. To-day they will be represented by Knight, full-back; Savage, Brooks, Watkins, and Kenyon, threequarters ; J. Davies and R. Bate, half-backs; J. Swift, Harmer, Lunt, Morrison, and Ashton, forwards. Davies is a youngster from Penygraig who had a trial last Saturday and:acquitted himself creditably.

The Warrington Committee have been labouring under considerable difficulties in picking the tw fifteens for there was never so much good material to choose from. All this reserve strength is a big advantage, for it makes the men who are chosen play to keep their places. There is nothing like com- petition for sharpening even footballers up. The selections which have been made will, I think, give satisfaction. The premier fifteen is about the heaviest that could be chosen, and the juniors are a promising lot. They possess plenty of pace in the three-quarter line, and that is where it is most wanted.

A special train will leave Bank Quay for Leigh at 2 o'clock, and the game will be in charge of Mr. Joe Priestley.

• The new season opened on Thursday with an echo of last season. Leeds and Batley met at Headingley to replay a last season's League match owing to a breach of the working clause by Leeds. Leeds played several new men, including Pacques, the Cumberland county forward, and Clarkson,a full back from Ossett, while Batley also had the assistance of a new back in O'Brien, late of Holbeck, and several new men in the pack. The game was scarcely taken seriously, neither side exerting themselves over much, and the play in consequence was scrappy. Leeds, if anything, had the better of the first half, but the combination was ragged, and, thanks principally to the clever work of Turton on the left wing,Batley held them at Bay. In the second portion Leeds were the first to score,Naylor getting over after a good run. Littlewood failed at goal. Batley, however, after this had all the better of the game, and Oakland got over (after bad play by Grace and Ward), for Turton to kick the goal they won by.

===== 0002601_19040903_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
Cricket.

To-day the curtain will be rung down on the War- rington cricket season of 1904, and the footballers will enter the arena. Taken all und the season has been gratifyingly success ful,and SVarrington's first eleven have no reason to be disappointed with their performances. On reviewing the various matches it is found that not until July 16th did the team suffer defeat, and three more reverses have followed. To go more into details, the opening match was played, on April 16th and Sefton were accounted for by a margin of 16. The subsequent engagements have resulted as follows :—April23rd,South Manchester defeated by 98; April 30th, Bolton defeated by 19 ; May 7th, Bootle defeated by 62; May 14th draw with Wigan; May 21st, draw with St. Helens; May 28th, Ashton-under-Lyne defeated by 26 ; June 4th. St. Helens defeated by 16; June 11th, Sale defeated by 113; June 18th, Earlestown defeated by 122; June 25th, draw with Bolton; July 2nd, Levensluilme defeated by 95; July 9th, South Manchester defeated by 58; July 16th, Sefton won by 145; July 23rd, Earlestown defeated by 131 ; July 30th, draw with ' Manchester ; August 6th, Bootle won by 42; August 13th,Ashton won by 124,and only 7 wickets down ; August 20th, draw with district eighteen • August 27th, draw with Sale. The season's record therefore is—played 20, won 12, lost 4 drawn 4. To-day sees the concluding match at Arplelv,an,d Preston will be the visitors.

Saturday's match at Sale was productive of interest- ing cricket, but neither side had the satisfaction of claiming the victory. Warrington lacked the services of E.A. Connor and P. B. Hawtin, whereas Sale played at, full strength. The Warrington batsmen gave a credit- able display and complied 170 for the loss of eight wickets. Sale had an hour and a half .in which to save the match, but they were equal to then. task and when stumps were drawn had knocked up 121 with only 5 wickets down.

The finest performance with the bat was that of Lynes, the Warrington professional, who made 57 inhis usual vigorous style. His remarkably hard hitting is evidenced by the fact that he placed twelve boundaries, and made only three singles. The visitors had the assistance of B. Silcock, an old club member who has not done ditty this season, and his patiently-compiled 34 was the next best score. J. W. Bibby placed 23 to his credit, and J. Callon's innings realized 15. A. Thewlis. the ex-professional, was in good form and knocked up 15 not out.

On the Sale side, J. Cookson secured first honours. Not a faulty stroke marred his innings which added 46 to the home score. He was magnificently caught by Richardson from one of Lynes' deliveries. C. R.lHartley batted creditably for 3.5 and Greenhalgh contributed 26.

Of the bowlers, W. Battersby accomplished the smart- est feat for Sale, capturing three wickets for 20 runs. Thewlis was the most effective in his deliveries on the Warrington side, two wickets falling at a cost of 25.

Warrington second sufferedoa reverse at the ikands of Sale second, who ran up a margin of 41. Warrington started disastrously, two %rickets falling without a single run on the score sheet. .T. Thompson and E. Pierpoint put a different gomplexion on the game, fifty runs being registered in a little over half an hour. Pierpoint was bowled by Rylance after knocking up 24, but Thompson continued to play in grand style and made 73. The inninzs closed for 1.26, to which Sale responded with 165, W. Thornber being the top scorer with 40.

Moore and Daresbnry accounted for Welker's Brewery by a 12 majority, the most successful batsman being J. Smith who compiled N.

Crosfleld's entertained Stretton and Appleton and came out victorious by 24. G. Gough was responsible for a capital 17 not out, but none of the visitors attained double figures.
