Bowling.

To-day the municipai bowling green at Victoria Park will be formally opened by the Mayoress at 2.30. The Mayor and Deputy-Mayor will then indulge in a game, after which the match League V. Works Association will be decided. The following teams have been selected :—League: Bull's Head. G. Ball sen., A. Shaw ; Cemetery, J. Berry. G. Ball, jun. ; Brooklands, T. Brookes. J. Earle; Liberal Club, A. J. Jones, H. .T. E. Jefferson, .1. Unsworth Daintith ; Chapel House, Fearnhead, C. Roberts, J. Weston; Penketh Park, J. Wareham, J. Bowyer; Horse and Jockey. G. Higham, T. Bretherton; Works : J. Willett (captain), Cros- flelds ; G. Holbrook (assistant captain). Rylands ; A. Gleave, White Cross: J. Monks, C.L.C. ; Herman Williams, Firth; Harry Williams, Pearson and Knowles: J. Holland, London _and North-Western. .T. W. Worsley, Pearson and Knowles ; Tom Bridge,

Fletcher.'Russell and Co.; T. Ellison, White Cross; T. , Horrobin, London and Northwestern; R. Fairbrother, I Artingstalls ; Joe Harrison, Houghton's: J. Ashton, Firth: H. Fogg, Stubbs; John I,Vorsley. Pearson and Knowles. Reserves : E. Shaw, Fletcher, Russell and Co.; T. Clare, Firth. There will be a charge of six- each to witness the match.

The works handicap was brought to a close on the King andiQueen green on Saturday, and a large crowd witnessed a series of keen contests. To John Monks belongs the honour of carrying off firbt prize, and his trial of skill with F. Woods proved interesting. Woods made a good effort but was outplayed, particularly in the big lengths. The compP3te prize list is as follows :-1, John Monks, clock; 2. Fernley Woods, watch ;3, George Shaw, case of cutlery; 4, F. Dean, cruet; 5, E. Clare, mattress and bedding. The other winners were :—Trousers: T. Humphries, Harry Williams, S. Lee, W. Kerfoot, R. T. Edington. Knives and forks: T. Horrabin, G. Monks, J. Jolley, J. Smith, R. Fairbrother, J, W. Worsley, Herman Williams, A. Peters and J. Russell. Table cloths: J. Dewson, J. Jennings, W. Mee, C. Jordan, C. Littlewood, C. Taylor, J. Andrews, H. Daintith, W. Drury. J. Wells, F. Ball, J, Tomlinson, W. Kenwriaht, J. Simister, J. Daintith, W. Bowering, E. Shaw, R. Dagnall and John Shaw.

Of course, the occasion could not be allowed to pass without a little speech making, and before the players left the green Mr. Willett, chairman of the committee, congratulated all interested in the competition on the splendid weather with which they had been favoured. He was sorry that neither the president (Captain Cros- field), nor his brother Mr. A H. Crosfield, ras able to be with them as they were in Switzerland, but Mr. Samuel Taylor, one of the vice-presidents, had kindly offered to distribute the prizes. He was also sorry that there was not such a big entry as last year, although it must be admitted when they took into consideration the Warrington and District League, which must of necess- ity rob them of some little interest, they had done well.

The company then adjourned to the well-appointed club room, where a convivial gathering was presided over by Mr. Samuel Taylor, one of the vice-presidents. Mr. Brtmelow occupied the vice-chair and the attendance includedj the energetic secretary, Mr. W. H. Smalley. The prizes were distributed by the Chairman, who com- mented upon the praiseworthy manner in which the games had been conducted throughout the season. When Mr. John Monks came forward to receive his handsome timepiece and bronzes he was warmly cheered. The Chairman expressed the opinion that the honour had been well earned, as Mr. Monks had "played an honest game all through." (Applause.) Appropriate observa- tions were addressed to the other successful players, several of whom, in answer to calls for a "speech," expressed the pleasure it afforded them to be among the prize winners. Mr. Taylor added that as long as the games were conducted in a sportsmanlike manner, the bowlers would receive the support of gentlemen through- out the town. (Applause.) Songs were rendered and a very enjoyable evening was spent.

With Saturday's matches, the Warrington and Dis- trict League competition of 1904 concluded. The fourteen matches arranged for each team have now been played, and keen.but friendly in a degree which has given pleasure to all concerned, has been the rivalry. Never was a championship more thoroughly earned, and the members of the Cemetery Club will have the hearty congratula- tions of their fellow-competitors upon the honour which has fallen to them. On Saturday they increased their lead to 4 points by defeating Brooklands to the extent of 49. One or two games were closely fought, T. Bandies defeating H. Williams, and J. Bandies account- ing for E. Taylor by a couple of points.

The only other match was between Fearnhead and th Liberal Club, and the victory of the villagers left the Liberals in undisputed possession of the wooden spoon. I hope the Bewsey:streetclub will have better luck next season, for their displays this year have been very poor. Several of Saturday's games proved very interesting, J. E. Warren defeating W. Turner by 15-13.

J. Berry did not figure in the Cemetery team on Saturday, so that the individual championship falls to A. Shaw, of Bull's Head, who has played 14 games and won 12, with a total of 202 points for and 134 against.

Cycling. Although Saturday afternoon was beautifully fine and the roads were in excellent condition, there was only a very moderate turnout for the Warrington Cycling Club's run to Delamere. The outward josrney through Frodsham and Kingsley was tkoroughly enjoyed and on arrival at the picturesque resort tea was served at the well-knowA Abbey Arms. Afterwards the wheelers in- dulged in a stroll round part of the forest and the beauties of the neighbourhood were much admired. A capital spin home concluded a successful outing.

Bold was the destination on Tuesday evening, but as the weather was extremely threatening only eight mem- b9rs assembled. A smart shower caused the cyclists to seek refuge at Sankey Chapel House where a pleasant hour was spent. The rain cleared off for the return journey and the run, though brief, was enjoyed.

Angling.

With few exceptions, the anticipations of sport amongst the coarse fish were realised last week-end. Canal fishing was more productive of sport than river fishing, and the Bridgewater Canal yielded odd catches, here and there, of fair proportions. The Canal at Acton Bridge yielded some roach fishing, too, but further afield not a great deal is reported as having been recorded. Several clubs were out match-fishing, fair sport being registered.'

The members of the Latchford Angling Society visited Northwich to fish another match at Barrow Lock, but not much sport was had, though the drive was much enjoyed. There were 33 competitors, and the following were declared winners :—lst, A. Mattock, Bozs. Bdrs. ; 2nd, A. Holland; 3rd, T. Wright; 4th and sth a tie, F. J. Emson and J. Jones ;18th, T. Worthing- ton; 7th, T. H.Houghton ; Bth, J. Barber; 9th, W. W. Starkey; and 10th, T. llwigger. There were 16 prizes altogether and they were all won. After the match all the members sat down to a substantial repast at the Sportsman's Inn, Northwich, Mr. Harry Taylor presid- ing, and Mr. E. Gorton in the vice-chiiir. The prizes were distributed on Monday evening last, and the date of the next match was provisionally fixed for Saturday, September 10th at Grappenhall. The Shakespeare Angling Society fished a contest at Agden Bridge on Saturday, last, Mr. L. Spencer being the winner.

To-day the Challenge Shield Competition and the Championship in connection with the Worslev Canal Fishery will be decided in two sections, one at London Bridge, Stockton Heath, and the other at Worsley. A good entry has been received.

In connection with the Warrington Anglers' Associa- tion, the Juvenile Branch will fish their- contest. at Grappenhall this afternoon.

The members of the Rope and Anshor Angling Society will fish a contest at Broken Cross, on Septem- ber 4th, and the Latchford Excelsior will visit the same place on September 18th. --- There will be A n open contest at Agden Bridge on Saturday, September 24th. Mr. Mattock of the Boat House Inn is offering £1 to a shilling entrance fee. Further particulars will be published in a few days.

The first match in connection with Crosfield's Angling Society was held at Thelwall on Saturday afternoon last when close upon one hundred of the employes of the works took part in the competition, thirty-three of which number caught fish. This was considered fairly good, inasmuch as the day was exceptionally bright, and restrictions were placed upon size, which all had a tendency against any abnormal weight being recorded. Several members of the works managementwere present during the competition, at the close of which it was decided to enter a team to fish for the Worsley Chal- lenge Shield which takes place to-dayat London Bridge. The distribution of prizes in connection with the above took place in the assembly room at the works on Wednesday, when Mr. Henry Roberts presented the prizes to the successful competitors. Ist prize, J. Holden; 2ne and 3rd, divided by J. Mather and E. Williams; 4th prize, H. Bromley ; sth and 6th, divided by H. Bell and W. Houghton; 7th, T. Clare; Bth, T. Higham ; 9th, J. Payne; 10th, R. Bate; 11th, R. Wilcox; 12th, .T. Jones; 13th, T. Livingstone; 14th, J. Wilcox 15th, S. Slater ; 16th, J. Bibby.

Mr. E. England of Stockton Heath has generously forwarded a cheque for £.5 to the Town Clerk towards a bowling prize to be competed for either this year or next, which shall carry with it the title of champion of Warrington and district. Mr. England also promises an annual donation of one guinea, and suggests that the competition be open to all bowlers within a radius of three miles of Warrington. The:Town Clerk has grate- fully acknowledged Mr. England's sportsmanlike action and we are sure it will also be warmly appreciated by the large army of bowlers in the district. PROVISIONS ARE DEARER.—Advt.

AN OSTEND SENSATION.

Reported Suicide of a Warrington Lady-

A Warrington young lady named Miss Louisa Kindoer is reported to have committed suicide at Ostend by throwing herself into the sea near the Royal Palace. A telegram giving a brief account of the tragic event appeared in several London papers on Tuesday, and it was stated that the day before her death Miss Kindoilt who was a native of Warrington, had attempted to throw herself from the window of her hotel, but was prevented from doing so. Inquiries in Warrington re- veal the fact that Miss Kindon is the•daughter of Mrs. Martha Jane Kindon, 22, Sharp-street, Warrington, but up to last evening no official intimation of her death had been received. With a view tO obtaining definite information, one of Miss Kindon's brothers left Warrington for Ostend on Wednesday. Miss Kindon, it appears, was 27 years of age and of prepossessing appearance. Her father, Mr. John Kindon, who was employed by Messrs. Pearson and Knowles for upwards of thirty years, died some time - ago, leavinghis wife with four sons and three daughters. One of the sons is marriedand lives in,Plerpoint-street. Miss Kindon was a clever needlewoman, and left War- rington six years ago to fulfil an engagement with a London firm of embroiderers. Three years later she was sent to represent the firm as a traveller in Belgium,with Brussels as headquarters. She was in touch with home - by correspondence, and in a recent letter told her mother that she would be visiting Ostend about this time. Naturally the sad news which gained currency on Tuesday came as a great shock to the family, for Miss Kindon was of a bright and happy dis- position and there was no sugsrestion in her letters of any trouble. Besides being a good linguist„ Miss Kindon was a clever horsewoman. She was held in esteem by a large circle of friends in Warrington,and her last visit to the town was made about last Christ- mas. Had it not been for the Ostend journey, she had intended coming over to England again this summer. Unfortunately Mrs. Kindon did not know the name of the firm with which her daughter was connected, and consequently was unable to communicate with theta., when the report appeared.

WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH OF

WARRINGTON.

ENDING AUGUST 27th, 1904.

During the week, the following births and deaths were registered :—Warrington Registration District births,46 ; deaths, 26. The excess of births over deaths was 20.

The birth-rate was equal to 35.8. The death-rate from all causes was equal to 19-8 per 1,000, against 21-8 the average of the previous 4 weeks and against 18-5 the corresponding period of last year. The death-rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 9-9 per 1,000, against 716 the average rate of the previous 4 weeks and against 411 the correspond- ing period of 1903. The ages of mortality were:- 0-1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65—up. Total 14 4 2 2 4 26

The deaths included-1 from scarlet fever. 1 from whooping cough, 11 from diarrhcea, 1 from phthisis, 2 from lung diseases, 10 from all other causes. The following cases of infectious disease have been reported during the week :—Scarlet fever, 25 cases, erysipelas, 1 case, Total number of patients in hospital, week ending August 27th, 112. -AMETEOROLOGY.—The mean temperature was 54 Fall. and therefore 1 deg. lower than the preceding week. The maximum temperature 67 F., was recorded on the 27th inst., and the minimum temperature 41 F., on the 24th Angust._ _ _

The rainfall measured 1•81 inch. Thelgeneml direction' was S.W.

J. GUEST GORNALL, Medical Officer of Health.

A WONDERFUL BILE BEAN CURE. Biliousness, Sickness, and Debility Ended.

"Everybody seems to want Bile Beans," said Mrs. - Janet Paul, of 6, Sir Michael-street, Greenock, to a' reporter of the "Greenock Herald," and added, " really shall have to lock my box up for my neighbours- have all found out how good they are, and are so con- stantly coming in to beg a few, I can hardly keep anY for my own use." In full explanation of her experi- ence, Mrs. Paul continued : Some time ago I began to feel out of sorts and depressed with frequent attacks_ of biliousness. I had racking pains across the small e the back. Then came headache, retching, and griping' pains in the stomach. This condition developed, until vomiting commenced. The vomiting seemed as if would never cease. and I had scarcely recovered Iran' one attack before another came on.

"I tried to eat, but could not. My appetite scat gone, and my inside revolted at the mere thought 01 food. I consulted several doctors. One said I had cold, another treated me for catarrh of the stomach, another for rheumatism. No two had a similar notion of the actual cause of my pain, and each tried different remedies from the others. All failed to do Me the slightest good. I had reached the stage of despair when Chas. Forde's Bile Beans were recommended, and I tried them. Before many days I felt brighter, and the paiqs in my back and head were less acute than they had been for weeks. I sent round for a further supply of Bile Beans and commenced taking them regularly. They made so much difference in me,that in a week or so I was like it clock that had been run clown and been put in complete order again by a clever operator. The improvement continued, vomititr, and biliousness ceased; the pains went, and now for the last few months I have been better in health than I can remember to have been for a long time. Bile Beans have cured me and set me ur, again, and since I talked so much about what they tor me, everybody I know seems to be taking them. Bile Beans are a certain cure for biliousness, indiges- tion, constipation, piles, debility, immmia, headache, female ailments, summer-end fag, wind, etc. Obtainable from all chemists at is. 11d., or 2s. 9d. for family b 0 containing three times is. Id. size. Fire in Legh-street. _ _ _

A fire which did considerable damage broke onto the premises of Messrs. Ingram Limited, electrical fitters, in Legh-street,shortly after two o'clock yester— day morning. Mr. Walter Hill, f Legh-street, gave the alarm, and the fire rigade turned out in charge of Snperintendent Turner. 14- good supply of water was obtained from the mains, but two hours' work was necessary before the' flames were extinguished. The building which con- tained a large electrical stock was practically gutted and the loss is roughly estimated at £3OO. FortunatelY the damage is covered by insurance. The fire spreads to a storeroom in the occupation of Mr. H. M. Ashton. and here about 115 worth of damage was done. An employees' dressing room at the Winmarleigh Cafr' was also burnt out. The origin of the fire is not • known.

The Premier of Victoria, says a Melbourne Corres- pondent, has caused the suppression of an advertisement caricaturing Mr. Reid, the Federal Premier, in bathing costume. Among the passengers on board the Union Castle, liner Kenilworth Castle, which sailed on WednesdaY from Cape Town for England, are Sir Arthur and Lady Lawley, Mr. Walrond, private secretary to Lord Milner. 9n d CommandantiKritzinger.

A WOMAN'S FORTUNE. Alice Hirst, an Edinburgh woman, told the St. Helens; magistrates, on Thursday, that there was a fortune ol £8,400 awaiting her when she came to St. Helens. A certain Father Webster was her cousin, and he was at St. Helens. He had been to Bedford-Leigh the daY before looking all over for her, so she came to St. Helens to find him. Dr. Cotton said the woman suffered from delusions, but when the p)liceman said she scp=' yesterday, under the influence of drink, she said he War' raving. It was he that was daft, not her.—The bener' sent her to the workhouse for fourteen days.

WIFE DESERTION AT WIDNES. Mary Jane Pickering, residing at 45, Anne-streft Widnes, was on Thursday morning granted a 10s. ma'o-. tenance order against her husband. He had left ber penniless, and had only sent her on an average 4s. fld; per week. which was inadequate to keep the 'home goia, Defendant said the reason he had left home was that his son had told him to go as they could do very well without him. He had taken his word, and gone awq• but in June last was persuaded to return, but on l° stayed a few days.

The Examiner, Saturday, September s, 1904. THE WONDERS OF YOSEMITE. BILLIARDS. Where Trippers are Unknown. I THE STROKES OF THE GAME. ; There are plenty of men by nature fond of travelling, , who yet dislike visiting what they call "show places,' BY lest they run across the herds of trippers who now swarm , in their hundreds and their thousands over the greater part of the world at certain seasons of ths year. One c,an thoroughly sympathize with such individuals, for , truly the sight of some scores of tourists flourishing ! cameras and guide-books, munching sandwiches, swilling bottled beer, and, worse of all, bawling banalities one to another at the tops of their voices, is not calculated to sooth the spirit of the man or woman of contempla I. - tive mood or artistic temperament. The man of taste and refinement who visits Yosemite, the world-renowned SIDE. valley of California, however, need have no fear of being disconcerted by trippers of any kind; for if a Although the most moderate of players can thousand shouting bank-holiday-mak4s were to be easily impart side to a ball, the properties of turned loose into that vast valley, they would be out side are but little understood by the ordinary of sight and hearings° quickly that their very existence player. I therefore intend in the course of this would be doubted by all save those who had actually article and several ensuing ones to go very set eyes on them in the first instance, thoroughly into this fascinating branch of the The origin of the name" Yosemite" is said to be an game and to treat of side in all its various rami- Indian word, " Ahometae," signifying "Great Grizzly fications. Bear." This was the name of the tribe that formerly As already stated, any player can impart side inhabited the district,and it was the title by which thefor a player to become a cueist of chief of the tribe was known. The Indians themselves a ball, but however, call the valley " Awahnee," which in their ' fair ability, that is to say, able occasionally to language means " chasm 'or" gorge." make a forty or a fifty break, he must be able There are many noteworthy points of interest in the , when necessary, to hit a ball without giving it worldrenowned Yosemite Valley; indeed, they all need- any side at all, or rather very little indeed, for to be seen to be thoroughly appreciated. To begin a ball only travels with absolutely no side when with we have the silver Merced River, which runs hit truly in the centre. The very best profes- , through the very middle of the Valley. This river is sional players, though they can always hit a ball about sixty feet in breadth in the summer, though very near the centre, find but seldom the exact during the winter it often develops into a raging true centre, any point on an imaginary central torrent. En common with almost all the lakes and streams in that region of California,, the Merced is so line from the base to the apex of the ball as the extraordinarily clear that fish swimming in it many feet striker stands facing the ball, being in this , under water are generally distinctly visible. The Bridal instance taken to mean the centre of ball. The Veil Fall is of especial interest to lovers of folk-lore line AB in diagram 1 shows this central line owing to the numerous legends connected with it DIAGRAM 1. What gave rise in the first instance to the majority of these remarkable tales nobody has been able ' A to discover, though plenty have attempted to do so. , One strange point in connection with the . Bridal Veil I Fall is that though it leaps into a precipice over a thou- sand feet deep, its doing so scarcely breaks the awful stillness of the surrounding ravines and mountains, the effect being, in consequence, weird in the extreme. Naturally this incident in particular has many strange tales associated with it ; yet, in reality, it needs no explanation, for the fall is silent simply because the water is converted into spray long before it reaches the pool'and rocks below. Cathedral Spires rise out of the great Cathedral Rock nearly a thousand feet into the air, and form one of the most striking sights in the ' Valley. Beheld from a distance they appear to be ; joined. As a fact they are far apart. These points alone are grand enough, and worth going many hundreds of miles to gaze upon; yet they sink into insignificance beside the immense waterfalls, cascades, and gigantic peaks revealed later. The three peaks known as Three Brothers, for instance—and so named in memory of three sons of a notorious Indian chief, who, with his entire tribe, was captured and put to death in the grassy ravine which lies almost hidden between these mountains—stand out against the sky to the east of El Capitan mountain, and rise almost per- pendicularly to a height of five thousand feet. Indeed, if you will reflect for a moment that the height of St. Paul's Cathedral in only three hundred and sixty-five feet, sand that of the Eiffel Tower one thousand feet, you will have some idea of the impression created by the sight of these terrible perpendicular escarpments. The Great Fall of Yosemite, or rather, the triple fall, for the entire volume of water, after shooting forth into the Valley at its northernmost extremity, makes a sudden leap of nearly three thousand feet in three cataracts. The first of these cataracts is close upon one thousand six hundred feet, and from its base until the next direct descent come a succession of small cascades for about six hundred feet, ending h. a final leap of four hundred feet into the talus below. The scene presented by these waterfalls on a bright moonlight night—and the moonlight of California is very different from the moonlight we are accustomed to over here— no pen has ever been able to portray. Perhaps the site from which the best view of all may be obtained of the valley and its many prominent peaks and waterfalls is Glacier Point, about a mile to the east of Sentinel Rock, and over three thousand feet in height. Persons liable to be attacked by sudden vertigo when looking down from a great height, however, are "forbidden to trespass on this rock," as the authorities quaintly put it; for the sight of the appalling preci- pice yawning immediately beneath has been known to turn the heads even of experienced mountaineers. I remember upon one occasion standing with a friend on the projecting ledge. Noticing that his complexion, usually a bright red,was turning gradually green and that his eyes were bulging more and more out of his head, as he seemed to rivet them upon some invisible speck in the bed of the almost bottomless ravine. I asked him in jest if he felt inclined to throw himself over. "Not precisely that," he replied, after a pause, raising his eyes slowly and looking at me strangely, "but—well, if you must know, I feel horribly inclined to throw you over." I halapened at that moment to remember a pressing engagement, and so left the ledge at once of my own accord.—From an interesting article on "The Wonders of Yosemite," which appears in the "September Windsor Magazine," a fine autumn number containing complete stories by Rudyard Kipling and Anthony Hope, as well as the usual budget of good fiction by other favourite novelists, a valuable list of articles includes "The Control of the Trains" in an important series on "The Ways of our Railways." and a study of the work on the art of the Hon. John Collier with no less than sixteen of his best pictures finely reproduced.

small holdings compulsory instead of permissive, as the Tories have made it, and our land laws will be ordered to suit the needs of the men of this generation. Every man who has eyes to see and ears to hear what is going on around him knows that these reforms are corning, and the agricultural-labourer has wit enough to fightand struggle and vote for them. Joseph's bribe is contemptuously rejected everywhere Norwich, and Mid-Herts, and Market Harborough, and East I)orset, and Devenport, and Oswestry, and all the other bye-elections tell the same old story. The fiscal policy has been offered to the electors in every imaginable guise, but they are not taking any, and if the Tories will only go to the country on this issue we will lick them worse than the Japs ever licked the Russians. What the Tories were doing to let Joseph capture their political organisation and commit them to the policy of taxation of food and prohibitive tariffs I cannot tell. He has torn the Tory party to pieces, and wrought as much mischief in their camp as he wrought among the Liberals when he found that the Grand Old Man would neither die nor resign to make room for him. He has wrecked each party in its turn, and he will wreck his own career at the finish, and there will be "none so poor to do him reverence." Winston Churchill was not far wrong when he prophesiod that Joseph's sun would set in a clouded sky. But as long as he goes about making such mischievous and foolish speeches az the one he made at Walbeck he may do infinite harm to the cause of progress, and he must be rgsisted and opposed to the end of the chapter.

ten steam winches and derricks for working the holds, AN EPOCH-MAKING SHIP, and she is provided with insulating chambers and re- frigerating plant so as to enable her to carry fruit, dairy produce, Stn., from Canada. The First Turbine Atlantic Liner. As already remarked, the Victorian is largely an ex- perimental ship. Turbines have proved a success for small high-speed channel steamers, but whether they will be an equal success for ocean steamers remains to LAUNCH OF THE VICTORIAN. Ibe proved. The trial trip of the Victorian will practic- ally solve this problem, which has been agitating the minds of shipping men for some time pall Whatever the result may be therefore, with the Allan Line rests the honour of leading the way with an important economical venture, and with Messrs. Workman, Clark & Co., that of conducting a new departure in ship- building and engineering science, which may mark an important epnch in both departments. The Victorian, when ready, will take her place on the Allan Canadian Mail and Passenger Service from Liver- pool, to be followed by her sister steamer Virginian. now building on the Clyde. These steamers, with the l3arvarian and Tunisian, will land to land, provide a service which by reason of the shorter mileage of the route will compare in length of voyage favourably with the swiftest of the other Atlantic Mail Services, and as much of the voyage is in land, encompassed seas, the Canadian route cannot fail to become increasingly popular with that great section of ocean travellers to whom a short and smooth sea voyage is an attraction. The Victorian, it may be added will be fitted out with 'ell possible despatch.
